Every righteous deed is a remittance to this account — *"whoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it"* (99:7) — and the account pays in currency no earthly bank holds. Work the twofold condition; the *ʿaẓīm* handles itself.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:10

> **And those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they shall be the inhabitants of Hellfire.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Other Side of the Promise**

Every promise in this religion has a shadow, and the Qur'an never hides it. Verse 9 gave the promise; verse 10 gives the warning, in perfect parallel structure: belief-plus-deeds earns forgiveness-plus-reward; disbelief-plus-denial earns the Fire. *Kaffarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* — "disbelieve and deny Our signs." The doubling is precise: *kufr* is the inward covering-over of truth (the root means to cover, as a farmer "covers" seed with soil), while *takdhīb* is the outward act of calling it a lie. The verse thus condemns not mere ignorance or weakness, but the settled, active rejection of recognized signs — truth seen and then refused. This is why the Qur'an elsewhere distinguishes those who never received the message from those who received and rejected it; the warning here targets the latter, the conscious deniers.

**Signs Denied: What "Āyātinā" Encompasses**

*Āyātinā* — "Our signs" — spans three registers the Qur'an constantly interweaves: *cosmic* signs (heavens, earth, alternation of night and day — 3:190), *scriptural* signs (revealed verses — 2:99), and *human* signs (prophets, and supremely the Prophet ﷺ — 7:157). Denial (*takdhīb*) can target any register: the materialist denying cosmic teleology, the scripturalist denying new revelation, the tribalist denying the prophet's person. The Qur'an's vehemence against *takdhīb* reflects its structure: signs are *evidence presented*, and calling evidence lies while perceiving its force is perjury against reality itself. This is why unreached peoples are explicitly excused — *"We never punish until We send a messenger"* (17:15) — while reached-and-refusing deniers are not: culpability tracks *presentation*. The verse thus quietly defines the missionary urgency: every sign presented raises the stakes of denial, making clear, compassionate proclamation (*balāgh mubīn*) an act of rescue, not aggression. Present signs purely; the presented bear their own verdict.

**"Inhabitants" of the Fire: The Horror of Belonging There**

*Aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm* — "companions/inhabitants of Hellfire." *Ṣāḥib* means a companion, one who keeps company; the damned are not visitors to the Fire but its *residents*, its company-keepers, as though they belong to it the way a citizen belongs to a city. *Jaḥīm* (from a root denoting intense blazing) is one of the Qur'an's proper names for Hell. The image completes the verse's quiet logic: human beings become companions of what they keep company with. Those who kept company with God's signs in this life keep company with His mercy in the next (v. 9); those who kept company with denial keep company with the Fire. Eternity, in this vision, is not arbitrary assignment but the ripening of association — the harvest of whatever the soul planted itself beside. The verse is severe, but its severity is the severity of truth-telling: a physician who hides a fatal diagnosis is not kind. The Qur'an diagnoses, warns, and — in the very next verse — returns to reminding believers of the blessings that make the promised end attainable.

**Warning as Mercy: The Pastoral Function of the Fire-Verses**

A modern reader may ask why tenderness (v. 9's promise) needs this shadow at all. The Qur'an's answer is pastoral: warnings *are* mercy to those still able to heed. The Prophet ﷺ likened himself to a man shouting fire-warning to his sleeping tribe (al-Bukhārī 6482; Muslim 2283) — the shout's harshness *is* its kindness. Remove verse 10 and verse 9's promise cheapens into assumption; urgency evaporates, striving slackens, and souls drift. The Qur'an maintains promise-and-threat (*waʿd wa waʿīd*) in deliberate equilibrium across hundreds of pairings — the two wings of religious motivation: hope flies toward the Gardens, fear flees from the Fire, and the bird needs both. Al-Ghazālī prescribed the dosage: hope should predominate in the despairing and the dying, fear in the healthy and heedless — medicine matched to patient. Verse 10 doses the heedless: read it when complacency whispers that striving is optional. The Fire is real, its residence is companionship, and companionship is chosen daily — with signs or against them.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:11

> **O you who believe! Remember God's Blessing upon you, when a people were prepared to stretch forth their hands against you, but He withheld their hands from you. Reverence God, and in God let the believers trust.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Hands Stretched and Hands Withheld: Providence in the Archive**

This is the final *remember God's Blessing* in the sūrah's opening movement (cf. vv. 3, 6, 7), and it commemorates deliverance: *a people were prepared to stretch forth their hands against you* — the idiom for violent assault — *but He withheld their hands from you*. The early reports offer several occasions. One tells of a Bedouin who seized the Prophet's ﷺ own sword as he rested unarmed beneath a tree and tried to kill him, but was thwarted (R, W). Another — favored by al-Ṭabarī for its fit with the following verses on Israel — tells of the Banū Naḍīr, a Jewish clan of Madinah, who plotted to drop a stone on the unarmed Prophet ﷺ when he came seeking help with blood-money, until Gabriel warned him and he withdrew (R, Ṭ, W). Yet the verse's plural *you* suggests the broadest reading is intended: God's standing protection of the fledgling community against enemies who outnumbered and outmatched it (R). The infant Muslim community survived its cradle years the way a child survives infancy — not by its own strength but by being carried. Communities, like individuals, should keep a written archive of deliverances; memory of past rescue is the raw material of future courage.

**The Sword at the Tree: The Preservation Narratives**

The preservation stories deserve full narration, for they are this verse's *sīrah* footnotes. Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh reports: returning from campaign, the Prophet ﷺ napped alone beneath a tree, sword hung on a branch. A Bedouin enemy, Ghawrath ibn al-Ḥārith, seized it, unsheathed it over the sleeper, and sneered: *"Who will protect you from me now?"* The Prophet ﷺ woke and answered with one word: *"God."* The sword trembled from the man's hand. The Prophet ﷺ picked it up and asked: *"And who will protect you from me?"* — *"No one."* — and let him go. The man returned to his people saying: *"I have come to you from the best of men"* (al-Bukhārī 2910, 4135; Muslim 843 — versions vary). The Banū Naḍīr episode (R, Ṭ, W) runs darker: hospitality offered as cover, a millstone readied above the wall where the Prophet ﷺ sat unarmed, Gabriel's warning, the quiet withdrawal — and the clan's subsequent siege and expulsion (cf. Sūrah al-Ḥashr). Both narratives enact the verse's verbs exactly: hands *stretched* (*basaṭa*), hands *withheld* (*kaffa*). Providence operates both modes — the dramatic (trembling swords) and the mundane (timely warnings) — and the community that archives both never lacks evidence for trust.

**From Memory to Trust: The Verse's Two-Step Logic**

The verse moves in two steps: *remember* the blessing, then *trust* (*tawakkal*) in God. The order is the argument. Trust is not a mood one summons from nothing; it is memory projected forward. The believer who rehearses how God withheld hostile hands yesterday finds it rational — not merely pious — to expect His help tomorrow. This is why the Qur'an returns obsessively to sacred history: every narrated deliverance (Moses at the sea, Muḥammad at Badr, the community against its plotters) is a precedent filed as evidence for trust. And trust, the theologians insist, is not passivity — the Prophet ﷺ who trusted God also posted guards, dug trenches, and wore armor. Trust means the heart's reliance rests on God while the limbs take the means; it is the opposite both of anxious self-reliance (means without God) and of lazy fatalism (God without means). *Reverence God, and in God let the believers trust* — piety in conduct, reliance in outcome. Do your part impeccably; sleep peacefully about the rest.

**Tawakkul's Mechanics: Tying the Camel**

*Tawakkul* (trust) is among Islam's most misunderstood virtues — confused both with recklessness and with anxiety — so its mechanics need stating. The Prophet ﷺ gave the master-formula to the Bedouin who would leave his camel untied "trusting God":

> *"Tie it, and trust."* (al-Tirmidhī 2517; graded ḥasan)

Means (*asbāb*) then reliance (*iʿtimād*): the limbs work the world God made causal, the heart rests on the God who made it so. ʿUmar rebuked Yemenis who pilgrimaged without provisions claiming trust: *"You are not trusters but sponges — the truster sows his seed then trusts his Lord"* (cf. IK). Ibn Taymiyyah systematized: abandoning lawful means is disobedience disguised as devotion; depending on means is idolatry disguised as prudence; the straight path takes means *as* obedience while depending on God *as* Lord. The verse's own word order teaches it: *reverence* (limbs' obedience) *then trust* (heart's reliance) — piety first, peace after. The student studies *and* trusts; the patient medicates *and* trusts; the community arms *and* trusts. Do your impeccable part; sleep the trustful sleep. What hostile hands can reach the one whose Guardian never sleeps (2:255)?

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:12

> **God had made a covenant with the Children of Israel, and We raised among them twelve chieftains. And God said, "I am with you!" Surely, if you perform the prayer, and give alms, and believe in My messengers and support them, and lend unto God a goodly loan, I shall surely absolve you of your evil deeds, and shall cause you to enter Gardens with rivers running below. But whosoever among you disbelieves thereafter, surely he has strayed from the right way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Cautionary Tale Begins: The Covenant's Other History**

With this verse the sūrah pivots from Muslim blessings to Israelite warnings — a cautionary history for a community that has just been told to fulfill *its* pacts (v. 1) and remember *its* covenant (v. 7). Al-Rāzī frames the logic: the Jews and Christians also held a covenant with God and broke it despite His blessings, and their story is exhibited so the new covenant-bearers can see the disease before catching it (R). The Israelite covenant echoes across the Qur'an (2:40, 63, 83, 93; 4:154; 5:70; 7:169), nearly always followed by an account of unfaithfulness — the pattern itself is the sermon. History in the Qur'an is never antiquarian; it is always diagnostic. A wise community studies the failures of its predecessors the way a physician studies case histories: not to gloat over the dead, but to recognize the symptoms in the living.

**Twelve Chieftains: Representation and Responsibility**

*Twelve chieftains* (*naqīban* — *naqīb*, a guarantor-chief) for twelve tribes (cf. 7:159–60; 2:60). The early reports connect them to the scouting mission before entering the Holy Land (vv. 21–25): twelve trusted men sent ahead, of whom ten returned with terror and two — Joshua (Yūshaʿ ibn Nūn, of Joseph's line) and Caleb — with faith (R, Ṭ, Z). The *naqīb* was a witness-guarantor for his tribe, answerable for what his people did with what he reported. Here lies a leadership lesson of permanent value: representatives shape the morale of those they represent. Ten chieftains spoke fear and produced forty years of wandering; two spoke trust and were vindicated by history. Every committee, cabinet, and council replicates this scene: the report brought back from the frontier — of markets, of enemies, of possibilities — becomes the reality the community inhabits. Choose scouts for courage as well as competence, and weigh fearful reports with special skepticism.

**The Prophet's Twelve: The Institution Renewed at ʿAqabah**

The *niqābah* (chieftaincy) institution echoes remarkably in the Prophet's ﷺ own statecraft. At the second ʿAqabah pledge, he appointed *twelve naqībs* over the Madinan converts — nine from Khazraj, three from Aws — saying:

> *"Bring me twelve leaders from among you to stand surety for their people, as the apostles stood surety for Jesus son of Mary."* (Ibn Hishām; Ibn Kathīr's *Sīrah*)

The parallel is deliberate: twelve tribes, twelve guarantors — then; one ummah-in-embryo, twelve guarantors — now. The *naqīb*'s office combined representation, moral surety, and mobilization: he answered for his people's pledge-keeping and organized their contribution. Later Islamic institutions inherited the title — the *naqīb al-ashrāf* (head of the Prophet's descendants), Sufi orders' *nuqabā'* (saintly deputies) — each preserving the core idea: *structured responsibility*, faith organized through accountable heads rather than left to crowds. The verse thus legislates by precedent: communities need chieftains — not tyrants, not figureheads, but guarantors who scout faithfully, report truthfully, and answer personally. The ten failed the office; the two honored it; the Prophet ﷺ renewed it; every Muslim organization re-staffs it still.

**"I Am with You": The Conditional Promise**

*Inn Allāha maʿakum* — "I am with you!" Then the conditions: prayer, alms, belief in *all* My messengers with active support (*ʿazzartumūhum* — to honor, aid, and defend), and lending God "a goodly loan" (*qarḍan ḥasanan* — spending wealth and effort for religion's cause; cf. 2:245; 57:11, 18; 64:17; 73:20). The reward: absolution of evil deeds and Gardens with rivers. Note the covenant's architecture — ritual (prayer, alms), doctrinal (all messengers, which later generations violated by rejecting some, including Muḥammad ﷺ; cf. 2:89–91; 4:155–57; 5:70), and financial (the goodly loan). God wants the whole person — body, belief, and purse — because partial surrender is the seed of total relapse. And the closing warning — *whosoever disbelieves thereafter has strayed from the right way* — establishes the principle of proportionate accountability: the more light given, the graver the darkness chosen. Twelve tribes, twelve guarantors, one promise, one warning. The mathematics of covenant has never changed.

**The Goodly Loan: Lending to the Richest Borrower**

*Tuqriḍū'Llāha qarḍan ḥasanan* — "lend God a goodly loan": the Qur'an's most startling financial metaphor (2:245; 57:11, 18; 64:17; 73:20), startling enough that Madinan Jews mocked it — *"God is poor and we are rich"* (3:181), or per al-Rāzī's reconstruction, *"God's Hand is shackled"* (5:64). The metaphor's logic: charity is not expenditure but *deposit* — lent to the Borrower who cannot default, at rates no market offers: *"Whoever lends God a goodly loan, He multiplies it manifold"* (2:245), up to *"seven hundred times"* (2:261). *Ḥasan* (goodly) conditions the loan: from lawful earnings, given cheerfully, untainted by reminders or injury (2:262–264), preferably secretly (2:271). Abū'l-Daḥdāḥ heard 2:245 and donated his entire orchard — six hundred palms — to God, walking his family out of it (IK). The covenant's inclusion of the loan beside prayer, alms, and messenger-belief proves wealth's spiritual weight: the purse is the third leg of fidelity. Ritual without charity is a tripod missing its leg — prayer ascending, purse anchored, the soul tilted. Lend goodly; the Borrower's credit is creation itself.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:13

> **Then for their breaking of their covenant, We cursed them and hardened their hearts. They distort the meaning of the Word, and have forgotten part of that whereof they were reminded. Thou wilt not cease to discover their treachery, from all save a few of them. So pardon them, and forbear. Truly God loves the virtuous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Anatomy of a Broken Covenant: Curse, Hardness, Distortion, Forgetting**

The verse lays out a four-stage pathology of covenant-breaking, and its order repays study. First, *laʿannāhum* — "We cursed them," understood as exile from God's Mercy (Z; cf. 2:88; 4:46–47; 5:64, 78). Second, *hardened their hearts* (*qasāwah*) — the heart that repeatedly refuses soft truth grows calluses, like hands that grip too long without gloves (cf. 2:74; 6:43; 39:22; 57:16). Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī preserve a striking metallurgical image: pure gold is soft and malleable, but alloyed with base metals it hardens — so the heart alloyed with hypocrisy hardens against the imprint of truth (R, Ṭ, Z). Third, *taḥrīf* — distorting the Word, whether by rearranging scripture's words or by twisting its meanings (cf. 2:75; 4:46; 5:41). Fourth, *forgetting part of the reminder* — the neglected portion of revelation quietly slips from memory (cf. 20:115 on Adam; 6:44; 7:165; 20:126). Notice the causation runs both ways: sin hardens the heart, and the hardened heart sins more easily. This is the spiritual equivalent of addiction medicine — each indulgence rewires the organ of choice itself. The only exit is the one the verse's ending provides: pardon, forbearance, and virtue — the softeners of hearts, human and divine both.

**Hardened Hearts: Diagnosis, Symptoms, and Cure**

*Qasāwat al-qalb* (heart-hardness) is the Qur'an's master-diagnosis of spiritual death, and its clinical picture deserves full assembly. Symptoms: unmoved by revelation (*"then their hearts hardened after that, becoming like stones or harder"* — 2:74), unmoved by others' suffering, unmoved by death itself (funerals attended as social events), and — the terminal sign — *finding worship burdensome* while finding sin effortless. Causes: exactly this verse's sequence — covenant-breach, compounded by persistent sin (*"nay, but what they earned has rusted upon their hearts"* — 83:14; *rān*, rust), heedlessness (*ghaflah*), and over-satiation with dunya. The Prophet ﷺ prescribed the cardiology:

> *"Do not speak excessively without remembering God, for excessive speech without God's remembrance hardens the heart, and the farthest people from God are the hard-hearted."* (al-Tirmidhī 2411; graded ḥasan)

And the cure-protocol: weeping remembrance (*dhikr* — *"hearts find rest in God's remembrance,"* 13:28), Qur'an recitation with reflection (39:23: *"skins shiver, then soften"*), visiting graves, feeding the poor, wiping orphans' heads (Aḥmad 7576). The verse's placement of hardness *between* curse and distortion teaches causation's direction: hardness is both sentence and cause — punishment for breach, engine of further breach. Examine your own cardiac softness regularly: when did revelation last move you to tears? The answer is your echocardiogram.

**"Pardon Them, and Forbear": Mercy After Treachery**

*Thou wilt not cease to discover their treachery, from all save a few* — likely recalling plots such as the Banū Naḍīr's (v. 11; Ṭ). And yet: *fa'ʿfu ʿanhum wa'ṣfaḥ* — "So pardon them, and forbear" (cf. 2:109; 3:159; 7:199; 24:22). The command astonishes precisely because it follows confirmed treachery rather than preceding it. Pardon before knowledge of the offense is naivety; pardon after full knowledge is mastery. Qatādah (d. early 2nd/8th c.) held this pardon abrogated by 9:29's command to fight hostile People of the Book until they pay the jizyah (IK, Ṭ, Th) — but al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī object that this sūrah likely postdates Sūrah 9 (revealed 9/631 versus the Farewell Pilgrimage of 10/632), so the pardon stands: once opponents accept lawful terms, past treachery is forgiven rather than nursed (R, Ṭ). Read beside verse 5's table-fellowship and marriage license, the verse reveals this late sūrah's trajectory: toward normalized, peaceful relations with the People of the Book wherever they accept coexistence. *God loves the virtuous* (*muḥsinīn*; cf. 2:195; 3:134, 148; 5:93) — pardon here is not weakness but *iḥsān*, the excellence of worshipping God as though you see Him. The strong can afford to forgive; the verse teaches the community to be strong.

**Pardon Practiced: The Prophet's Amnesties**

The pardon-command was performed at scales history still studies. At Makkah's conquest, the Prophet ﷺ faced Ibn Abī Sarḥ — his former scribe turned apostate-mocker — dragged for execution; after ʿUthmān's intercession he pardoned him, later explaining his silence had hoped someone would act (Abū Dāwūd 2683) — pardon extended to a *scribe-apostate*, the harshest category. Hind, who chewed Ḥamzah's liver at Uḥud, received pardon and Islam (Ibn Hishām). The Jews of Khaybar, after war and the poisoning attempt, were not expelled but contracted as farming partners on half-shares (al-Bukhārī 2329; Muslim 1551) — enmity converted to enterprise. Ḥāṭib ibn Abī Baltaʿah, who spied for Quraysh before the conquest, was pardoned for Badr's sake: *"What will tell you? Perhaps God looked upon Badr's people and said: Do as you wish, I have forgiven you"* (al-Bukhārī 3007; Muslim 2494). Each pardon weighed the verse's calculus: treachery *known* (*thou wilt not cease to discover*), pardon *chosen* (*fa'ʿfu*), virtue *loved* by God. The pattern rebukes both naivety (discovering nothing) and vindictiveness (forgiving nothing): discover everything, forgive strategically, love virtuously. Strength that cannot pardon is mere force; pardon that cannot discern is mere softness. The verse commands the third thing: strong discernment forgiving.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:14

> **And with those who say, "We are Christians," We made a covenant. Then they forgot part of that whereof they were reminded. So We stirred up enmity and hatred among them, till the Day of Resurrection. God will inform them of what they used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Those Who Say, We Are Christians": A Covenant Named by Self-Description**

The Qur'an's phrasing — *alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā*, "those who say, 'We are Christians'" — names the community by its own self-description (*naṣārā*, Helpers, after Jesus' helpers; cf. 3:52; 61:14). Like Israel, they entered covenant; like Israel, *they forgot part of the reminder*. But the consequence differs, and the difference is instructive. Israel's breach brought curse and cardiac hardening (v. 13); the Christians' forgetting brought *internal* fracture — *We stirred up enmity and hatred among them till the Day of Resurrection*. The classical commentators read this as sectarian factionalism over theology (IK, Ṭ) — the christological schisms that split councils, empires, and communions for centuries. Factionalism-as-punishment appears elsewhere too (6:65). The mechanism is painfully recognizable: when a community forgets part of its Founder's teaching, the forgotten part becomes the battlefield; each faction absolutizes a fragment and anathematizes the rest. Unity requires the *whole* reminder held in balance; partial memory produces warring orthodoxies. Every divided religious community — including, soberly, the Muslim ummah — should read this verse as a mirror before reading it as a history.

**Naṣārā: The Beautiful Name and Its History**

*Naṣārā* (singular *naṣrānī*) derives from *naṣr* (help, victory) — the Helpers of Christ, named for the apostles' pledge: *"Who are my helpers unto God?" The apostles said: "We are God's helpers"* (3:52; 61:14). Some link it to Nazareth (*al-Nāṣirah*), Jesus' town. Either root honors: helperhood or homeland. The Qur'an uses it consistently (2:62, 111, 113, 120, 135, 140; 5:14, 18, 51, 69, 82; 9:30; 22:17) — never pejoratively in form, always descriptively. The *qālū* ("those who *say*") frames it as self-designation respected: God addresses them by the name they chose, the way dignified discourse uses peoples' preferred names. This etiquette binds Muslim speech permanently: Christians are *Naṣārā*/Christians, Jews are *Yahūd*/Jews (2:62) — titled by revelation, never to be degraded by believers' tongues. Polemic against doctrines (vv. 17, 72–73, 116) never licenses insult against persons; the verse that sentences factionalism *names* its subjects honorably. Disagreement's manners are revealed before disagreement's substance: call people beautifully, then argue truthfully. The community that masters this order can dispute for fourteen centuries without dehumanizing once.

**Forgetting as Fracture: The Sociology of Schism**

*Nasū ḥaẓẓan* — "they forgot a portion" — *fa-aghraynā baynahumu'l-ʿadāwata wa'l-baghḍā'* — "so We *stirred up* (*aghraynā* — instigated, incited, set) enmity and hatred among them." The causation is sociological law disguised as history: *partial forgetting produces total fracture*. Trace it in Christian history as the commentators did (IK, Ṭ): the forgotten balance between Christ's humanity and divinity became the Arian-Nicene wars; the forgotten balance between grace and works became Reformation's century of blood; the forgotten balance between scripture and tradition became schism-upon-schism — each faction guarding its remembered fragment against the others' fragments. The Qur'an generalizes the law to all communities: *"Every party rejoices in what it has"* (30:32) — fragment-joy, whole-blindness. The Muslim ummah's own Sunnī-Shīʿī-Khārijī fractures, its madhhab partisanships, its modern ideological camps — each replays the verse: a portion forgotten (the caliphate's consensual spirit, the law's merciful aims, the creed's middle path), enmity stirred, hatred till Resurrection unless repented. The verse's mirror terrifies precisely because it reflects universally. The cure is stated by inversion: *remember the whole* — the entire reminder, balanced — and enmity finds no fuel. Wholeness remembered is unity preserved.

**Differences Deferred to the Day of Clarification**

*God will inform them of what they used to do* — religious differences, the Qur'an repeatedly teaches, will not all be resolved by debate in this world; they await Divine clarification in the next (2:113; 3:55; 5:48; 6:164; 10:93; 16:92, 124; 22:69; 32:25; 39:3, 46). This is not relativism — the Qur'an is unsparing in judging doctrines true or false — but eschatological patience: final verdicts belong to the Final Court. The practical ethic follows: argue firmly but live peacefully, witness without coercing, and leave the gavel to God. The verse thus quietly rebukes two opposite errors — the zealot who would settle all theology by force now, and the indifferentist who concludes that since God will sort it out, truth does not matter. Truth matters infinitely; that is precisely why its final arbitration is reserved for infinite Wisdom.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:15

> **O People of the Book! Our Messenger has come unto you, making clear to you much of what you once hid of the Book, and pardoning much. There has come unto you, from God, a light and a clear Book,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Messenger Who Reads the Hidden Pages**

*Our Messenger has come unto you, making clear* (*yubayyinu*) *much of what you hid of the Book, and pardoning* (*yaʿfū*) *much*. Concealment of scripture (*kitmān*) is a recurring Qur'anic charge (2:42, 140, 146, 159, 174; 3:71, 187; 6:91). The commentators give examples: prophecies of Muḥammad's coming (7:157) and the Torah's penalty of stoning for adultery — which the Prophet ﷺ brought back to light when two Jewish adulterers were brought to him for judgment, ordering the Torah's own penalty carried out (JJ, Ṭ, Z; cf. 3:23). But note the second verb: *pardoning much*. Revelation does not expose everything hidden; it exposes what guidance requires and passes over the rest. Divine pedagogy is surgical, not vindictive — it uncovers enough to establish the proof and covers the rest in mercy. Human reformers should imitate the sequence: reveal what must be revealed to right the wrong, and veil what veiling harms no one. Exposure is a medicine with side effects; the All-Wise Physician prescribes the minimum effective dose.

**Kitmān: The Anatomy of Scriptural Concealment**

*Kitmān* (concealing) in the Qur'an is never mere silence — it is *interested* silence, hiding truth for advantage. Its Madinan forms, per the commentaries: suppressing Muḥammad's ﷺ prophecies (7:157: *"they find him inscribed in the Torah and Gospel"*), suppressing stoning (v. 41's hand-on-scroll), and — the Qur'an's broadest charge — *"mixing truth with falsehood while concealing truth knowingly"* (2:42; 3:71). Motives repeat across history: *status* (the learned fearing obsolescence if new truth spreads), *wealth* (selling lenient rulings — 2:79's *"woe to those who write the Book with their hands then claim it is from God, to sell it for a paltry price"*), and *tribalism* (truth favoring outsiders must be buried). The Prophet ﷺ warned his own ummah inherits the disease:

> *"Whoever is asked about knowledge and conceals it will be bridled on Resurrection Day with a bridle of fire."* (Abū Dāwūd 3658; al-Tirmidhī 2649; Ibn Mājah 261)

Scholars who hide rulings inconvenient to rulers, witnesses who swallow testimony, experts who bury findings — all commit *kitmān*. The verse's double verb remains the standard: *clarify* what guidance requires (no concealment), *pardon* what mercy covers (no vindictive exposure). Clarify the stoning-verse; pardon the rest. Truth's servants disclose dutifully and veil mercifully — concealing nothing owed, exposing nothing gratuitous.

**Light and Book: Two Names for One Arrival**

*There has come unto you, from God, a light* (*nūr*) *and a clear Book* (*kitāb mubīn*). Most commentators read "light" as Muḥammad ﷺ and "clear Book" as the Qur'an (Ṭ) — the Qur'an is called *mubīn* repeatedly (12:1; 26:2; 27:1; 28:2; 43:2; 44:2) — though some read both as the Qur'an (Z). Light is the Qur'an's master-image for guidance through prophets and revelation (6:91; 7:157; 21:48; 42:52; 64:8), and there are those who would extinguish God's light (9:32–33; 61:8–9). The pairing teaches that guidance needs both a *person* and a *text*: the Book without the Messenger becomes a dead letter fought over by literalists; the Messenger without the Book becomes a personality cult dissolving at his death. Together — living example plus fixed text — they form a complete guidance system, each correcting the other's misreadings across the centuries. The congregation that holds both tightly will not lose its way; the faction that drops either will wander, lamp in hand but map discarded, or map in hand but lamp unlit.

**Nūr: The Qur'an's Theology of Light**

*Nūr* (light) carries the Qur'an's richest symbolic freight, and the verse plugs directly into its main circuit: God Himself is *"the Light of the heavens and the earth"* (24:35 — the Light Verse, with its niche-lamp-glass-tree-oil parable the mystics plumbed for centuries); His Name *al-Nūr* crowns the litany; revelation is light (42:52: *"We made it a light guiding whom We will"*); believers walk *"with a light before them"* on Judgment Day (57:12; 66:8) while hypocrites beg *"wait for us, let us borrow your light"* (57:13). The Prophet ﷺ *is* light here (Ṭ) as the lamp (*sirāj munīr*, 33:46) carrying God's light to creatures — the commentary tradition's constant image: Muḥammad ﷺ the moon reflecting the Divine sun, the niche-glass transmitting the eternal Flame. Darkness in this system is never a *thing* but light's *absence* — falsehood has no ontology, only obscurity. Hence guidance's mechanics: not combat with darkness but *arrival* of light — *"truth has come and falsehood has vanished; falsehood is ever vanishing"* (17:81). Bring the lamp; shadows flee unargued. The verse announces both lamp (Messenger) and lamplight-manual (Book): carry one, read the other, and no darkness in any age can hold its ground.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:16

> **whereby God guides whosoever seeks His Contentment unto the ways of peace, and brings them out of darkness into light, by His Leave, and guides them unto a straight path.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Seeking God's Contentment: The Sincerity Condition**

*Whosoever seeks* (*ibtighā'*) *His Contentment* (*riḍwān*) — guidance is offered universally but received conditionally, and the condition is sincerity of seeking. The *mubtaghī* — the seeker of God's good pleasure — is the Qur'an's portrait of the sincere believer (2:207, 265; 4:114; 5:2; 59:8). The point cuts against both despair and presumption: no one is guided who does not want to be, and no one who truly wants to be is refused. Revelation is light, but light benefits only open eyes; the verse makes the opening of the eyes a human responsibility. In practical terms: approach the Qur'an asking "what does my Lord want from me?" and it will answer; approach it asking "how can I refute my opponents?" and it will seem silent. The Book mirrors the seeker's intention — a mercy to the sincere, a locked door to the manipulative.

**Riḍwān: Contentment as Life's Highest Currency**

*Riḍwān* (God's contentment, good pleasure) is the Qur'an's supreme value-term — outranking even Paradise's pleasures: *"God has promised believers Gardens... but contentment from God is greater"* (9:72). The righteous act *seeking God's countenance* (*wajh* — 2:207, 265; 76:9; 92:20), and the Hereafter's climax is mutual contentment (5:119; 98:8). The Prophet ﷺ revealed its eschatological scene:

> *"God will say to Paradise's people: 'Are you content?' They will say: 'Why should we not be, having received what no creature received?' He will say: 'Shall I give you better?' — 'What is better?' — 'I bestow upon you My contentment, never displeased with you thereafter.'"* (al-Bukhārī 6549; Muslim 2829)

Trace the economy: deeds purchase Gardens, but *intention* purchases *riḍwān* — the same deeds, differently motivated, yield different heavens. Ostentation (*riyā'*) earns applause-now, nothing-then; sincerity (*ikhlāṣ*) earns contentment-then, the only currency that survives death's exchange. The verse's *seeking* (*ibtighā'*) is thus Islam's master-motive: before every act — prayer, charity, career, marriage — ask the *riḍwān*-question: does this seek His pleasure? Affirmative: proceed, and guidance compounds. Negative: abandon, however profitable. The contentment-seeker cannot finally lose: even failed enterprises, sincerely Godward, deposit *riḍwān* no bankruptcy touches.

**Many Darknesses, One Light: The Grammar of Guidance**

*Subul al-salām* — "ways of peace" (or the way to God, since *al-Salām*, Peace, is His Name (Ṭ)). *From darknesses* (*ẓulumāt*, always plural) *into light* (*nūr*, always singular) — the Qur'an's grammar never varies here (2:257; 14:1, 5; 33:43; 57:9; 65:11), and the commentators draw the metaphysics: falsehood is multiple because it is fragmentation — every error a different shard — while truth is one because it is unity, reflecting the Oneness of God Himself, who is *the Light of the heavens and the earth* (24:35; Nūr among His Names). Error has dialects; truth has a mother tongue. The verse ends with *a straight path* (*ṣirāṭ mustaqīm*) — the life lived in accord with Divine Guidance (1:6; 2:142; 3:101; 6:87; 43:61; 67:22). Three images in one verse — ways, light, path — converge on one promise: the sincere seeker is not left to grope. Roads are marked, darkness is exited, and the path underfoot runs straight. Guidance is not a puzzle God sets but a hand God extends — *by His Leave*, always by grace, never by entitlement.

**The Straight Path Daily: Ṣirāṭ in Practice**

*Ṣirāṭ mustaqīm* — the straight path — is Islam's most-recited phrase (seventeen-plus times daily in *al-Fātiḥah*), and this verse shows its machinery: *guidance* is God's act (*yahdī*), *seeking* is man's (*ibtighā'*), *leave* is grace's (*bi-idhnihi*). The Prophet ﷺ diagrammed it memorably: he drew a straight line, said *"this is God's path,"* then drew crooked lines branching off, saying *"these are paths, each with a devil inviting to it"* — then recited *"this is My straight path, so follow it; follow no other paths, lest they scatter you"* (6:153; Musnad Aḥmad 4437; graded ḥasan). The diagram's sociology: deviation never announces itself as deviation — each crooked path has its *inviter* (ideologue, influencer, appetite) promising shortcuts. Steadfastness (*istiqāmah*) is therefore daily re-commitment, not once-achieved status — the Companions asked for a comprehensive counsel and received: *"Say: I believe in God — then stand straight"* (Muslim 38). Practically: the straight path is *this* prayer's concentration, *this* transaction's honesty, *this* glance's lowering. Straightness is fractal — the whole life straight if each moment is. Seek contentment moment-by-moment; the moments string into the path; the path ends, as the next verses will show, at contentment itself.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:17

> **They indeed have disbelieved who say, "God is the Messiah, son of Mary." Say, "Who would have any power over God if He desired to destroy the Messiah, son of Mary, and his mother, and those on earth all together?" Unto God belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them. He creates whatsoever He will, and God is Powerful over all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Qur'an's First Charge Against Christological Excess**

*Laqad kafara'lladhīna qālū inna'Llāha huwa'l-masīḥu'bnu Maryam* — "They indeed have disbelieved who say, 'God is the Messiah, son of Mary.'" The *laqad*...*inna* construction is among Arabic's strongest affirmations; the Qur'an leaves no doubt about its verdict on identifying God with a human being (cf. 4:171; 5:72, 75, 116; 9:31). Note the title used: *al-Masīḥ*, the Messiah — the Qur'an honors Jesus with his messianic title even while refuting his divinization (cf. 4:157, 171–72; 5:72, 75; 9:30–31). The refutation does not demean Jesus; it defends him from an honor that would destroy his true honor — the honor of perfect servanthood. To call a flawless ambassador the king is not to praise the ambassador but to abolish his ambassadorship. The verse's mention of *his mother* alongside extends the critique to Mary's divinization (cf. v. 116) — the Qur'an sees where unchecked exaggeration travels and meets it at the destination in advance.

**Al-Masīḥ: The Messiah's Many Meanings**

*Al-Masīḥ* (the Messiah, Christ) is Jesus' Qur'anic title of honor, and its etymology opens treasuries. Arabic lexicographers offer: the *anointed* (touched with blessing — *masḥ*, wiping), the *traveler* (who roamed preaching — *siyāḥah*), the *sinless* (wiped clean of fault), or healer (whose touch — *masḥ* — cured blind and lepers, v. 110). The Gospels' *Christos* (anointed king) converges with the first sense. The Qur'an deploys the title strategically: honoring Jesus *maximally* as man (Messiah, Word, Spirit, prophet, messenger — 4:171) precisely while denying him *minimally* as God. The honorific does argumentative work: *"the Messiah"* evokes the *awaited servant-king* of prophecy — a role *within* God's plan, not identity *with* God's essence. Muslims, the verse implies, honor the Messiah *more* coherently: affirming his virgin birth, miracles, ascension, and Second Coming (4:159; ḥadīth literature), while preserving the Creator-creature line his own words drew (v. 72, 117). True messianic honor, the Qur'an insists, is *servanthood perfected* — *"the Messiah would never disdain to be God's servant"* (4:172). A Messiah who *is* God needs no anointing; the Anointed is, by title, the Appointed — and appointment presupposes an Appointer.

**The Destroyer Argument: Contingency as Proof of Creatureliness**

The refutation is devastatingly simple: *who would have power over God if He desired to destroy the Messiah, his mother, and all on earth together?* Whatever can be destroyed is contingent; whatever is contingent is created; whatever is created is not God. The argument's beauty is that Christians themselves supply its premises — Jesus hungered, thirsted, suffered, and (in Christian telling) died; Mary bore, nursed, and aged. A being with a birthday and a body is a being under power, not a being *as* power. *He creates whatsoever He will* (cf. 24:45; 30:54; 42:49) answers the unspoken objection — "but Jesus had no father!" — with a principle that dissolves it: fatherlessness proves nothing about divinity, since God creates as He wills; Adam had neither father nor mother (3:59), and no one deifies him. The verse thus implicitly refutes the Nicene formula "begotten, not made": whatever is begotten in Mary's womb is made, and whatever is made belongs to *those on earth all together* — under sovereignty, not as sovereignty.

**Adam and Jesus: The Qur'an's Parallel That Ends the Debate**

The fatherlessness-objection receives its canonical demolition in Āl ʿImrān, revealed for Najrān's Christian delegation, and must be quoted here as this verse's sister-argument:

> *"Truly the likeness of Jesus before God is as the likeness of Adam: He created him from dust, then said to him: Be — and he was."* (Qur'an 3:59)

The logic is airtight *a fortiori*: if fatherlessness proved divinity, Adam — fatherless *and* motherless, dust-born by direct command — would be diviner still; since no Christian deifies Adam, fatherlessness proves nothing. The Najrān delegation, unable to answer, declined the Prophet's ﷺ *mubāhalah* (mutual imprecation, 3:61) and accepted treaty-and-jizyah instead (IK) — history recording the argument's force. *He creates whatsoever He will* (v. 17's clause) generalizes the principle into cosmology: creation's *modes* vary (dust, rib, womb, fatherless womb — 42:49–50 catalogs the variations) while creation's *status* never does — made, owned, sustained. Variety of manufacture never implies variety of manufacturer-nature. The Christian asked "how can God have a son without consort?" receives the Qur'an's counter: *"How can He have a son when He has no consort and created all things?"* (6:101). Begetting belongs to biology; God authors biology. Worship the Author, honor the wonders — including the wonderful Messiah — and confuse the two never.

**"God Is the Messiah" Versus "The Messiah Is God": A Theological Precision**

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī note a remarkable precision: the Qur'an criticizes the claim that *God is the Messiah* — the confinement of the unlimited Divine Being to a human manifestation. Strictly, official Christian doctrine says the reverse predication, "the Messiah is God," while affirming Christ's humanity alongside. But the two commentators argue the distinction collapses in practice: a doctrine that attributes creation, universal rule, and incarnation to Christ is *tantamount* to confining Godhead within him, whatever its careful formulations. The lesson for interfaith engagement is balance: represent the other's doctrine accurately (the commentators knew the difference), but follow ideas to their logical terminus (they did that too). And the verse's closing — *unto God belongs sovereignty* (*li'Llāhi mulk*) *over the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them* — sets the positive doctrine against the negated one: reality is a kingdom with one King, and Jesus and Mary are among its most radiant subjects, which is glory enough for any creature.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:18

> **And the Jews and the Christians say, "We are the children of God, and His beloved ones." Say, "Why then does He punish you for your sins?" Nay, but you are mortals of His creating. He forgives whomsoever He will, and He punishes whomsoever He will, and unto God belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them, and unto Him is the journey's end.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Children of God": The Only Verse of Its Kind**

This is the Qur'an's sole critique of Jews and Christians calling themselves *children of God* — though it tirelessly refutes divine sonship in general (2:116; 6:100; 9:30; 10:68; 17:40, 111; 18:4; 19:35, 88–93; 21:26; 25:2; 37:149, 153; 39:4; 43:16, 81–82; 52:39; 72:3). The claim has scriptural roots the commentators recognized: Israel as God's "firstborn son" (Exodus 4:22–23), David and Solomon as His "sons" (Psalm 2:7; 1 Chronicles 28:6), the Gospels' "children of God" (Matthew 5:9; Luke 20:36; John 11:52; John 20:17). Ibn ʿAbbās reports the occasion: some Madinan Jews, warned by the Prophet ﷺ of punishment, answered that as God's children and beloved they had nothing to fear (Ṭ, Z). The commentators understood the claim as metaphorical or genealogical — an extension of the "sonship" of Ezra and Jesus (9:30; IK, R, Z) — not a literal assertion of divinity (JJ, R). Al-Rāzī links it to the broader claim of exclusive favor running through 2:111 and 2:135. The Qur'an's response respects the metaphor's poetry while demolishing its presumption — a model for critiquing beloved religious language without mocking it.

**"Father" in the Bible, "Lord" in the Qur'an: Why the Vocabulary Differs**

The "children" debate opens Islam's deepest vocabulary decision: the Qur'an *never* calls God "Father" — not once in 6,236 verses — while "Lord" (*Rabb*) occurs ~980 times. The omission is-de deliberate theology. *Rabb* (Lord, Master, Nurturer, Sustainer) conveys authority *and* care without biology's shadow: the Lord owns, feeds, heals, and judges — all paternity's positives, none of its begetting-entailments. *Father*, however metaphorically intended, smuggles generation-imagery into Godhead — and history proved the smuggling consequential: metaphorical sonship (Israel, David, peacemakers) slid, via Jesus' unique sonship, into ontological sonship (Nicene *homoousios*). The Qur'an amputates the slide at its root: *"He begets not, nor is He begotten"* (112:3) — the two-verse demolition of all divine-generation language. This is not coldness: the Qur'an's God is *nearer than the jugular vein* (50:16), *answers the distressed* (27:62), *loves* the virtuous (5:54), and runs to the approaching servant (al-Bukhārī 7405) — intimacy exceeding any father's, grounded in lordship rather than likeness. Muslims addressing God find *Rabb* warmer than *Father* precisely because truer: sonship presumes; servanthood (*ʿubūdiyyah*) approaches — and approach, the mystics testify, yields a nearness no metaphor could grant.

**Punishment as Disproof: The Argument from Your Own Admissions**

*Why then does He punish you for your sins?* The refutation is a judo throw, using the opponents' own admissions: both Jews and Christians affirm divine punishment (Jews typically limiting it to a brief period; cf. 2:80; 3:24). But beloved children exempt from accountability cannot simultaneously be punished sinners — the two descriptions contradict. *Nay, but you are mortals* (*bashar*) *of His creating* — bounded by birth and death you did not choose and cannot escape, exactly like the Jesus and Mary of verse 17. The argument generalizes into a universal solvent for religious entitlement: no lineage, no communal membership, no inherited title exempts anyone from the moral law. The Qur'an applies the same solvent to Muslims who might presume upon their own titles — the ummah is "the best community" only *on condition* of enjoining right and forbidding wrong (3:110). Chosenness in the Qur'an is always vocational (chosen *for* a task) and conditional (kept *by* fidelity), never a blanket immunity. Every community that reads this verse about others must overhear it about itself.

**Chosenness Conditional: The Qur'an's Doctrine of Election**

*Beloved ones* (*aḥibbā'*) deserved its own refutation-history, for chosenness-language recurs in all three Abrahamic faiths and corrupts identically. The Qur'an affirms Israel's election historically — *"I favored you above all peoples"* (2:47, 122) — then documents its conditionality: favor *in their time* (IK, Q), revocable upon breach (vv. 12–13), transferable in function to the faithful (3:110's *best community* — conditional on reforming). The Prophet ﷺ demolished Arab-electoral pride at its root:

> *"O people! Your Lord is One, your father is one. No superiority of Arab over non-Arab, nor non-Arab over Arab, nor white over black, nor black over white — except by reverence."* (Musnad Aḥmad 23489)

And to his own daughter: *"O Fāṭimah, daughter of Muḥammad! ... I avail you nothing against God"* (al-Bukhārī 3527; Muslim 206) — the Prophet's ﷺ *own blood* denied immunity. The doctrine crystallizes: *election is vocation* (chosen to serve truth), *never vacation* (exempted from judgment). Communities are chosen the way ambassadors are commissioned — for mission, under review, recallable. The Jew, Christian, or Muslim who treats communal membership as eschatological insurance commits verse 18's exact error in local accent. Privilege in this Book flows one direction only: toward *taqwā* — *"the noblest among you before God is the most reverent"* (49:13). Everything else is commentary; everything contrary is *shana'ān*-grade presumption.

**Sovereignty and the Journey's End: Forgiveness as Royal Prerogative**

*He forgives whomsoever He will, and He punishes whomsoever He will* — mercy and punishment are not tribal entitlements but sovereign acts, flowing from the *mulk* (sovereignty/ownership) over heavens, earth, and all between, unto whom all journeys end (*maṣīr*). The verse thus relocates hope from the right address to the right one: not "we are safe because of who we are" but "we may be safe because of who He is" — Forgiving toward the repentant, Just toward the defiant. For the anxious believer this is liberation: your fate rests not on the strength of your communal card but on the mercy of your Lord, which no sin outnumbers. For the presumptuous believer it is a summons: the same mercy that encourages the despairing terrifies the complacent. Both stand before the same throne, and the throne's rule is single — *unto Him is the journey's end*.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:19

> **O People of the Book! Our Messenger has come unto you, making things clear to you, after an interval in [the series] of messengers, lest you should say, "There came unto us no bearer of glad tidings, nor any warner." But there has come unto you a bearer of glad tidings and a warner. And God is Powerful over all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Interval Ends: Prophecy After the Silence**

*After an interval* (*fatrah*) *in the series of messengers* — *fatrah* denotes a pause, a stillness between movements, used for gaps between prophetic eras and even between revelations to Muḥammad ﷺ (17:106). Here it names the long centuries between Jesus and Muḥammad ﷺ. Some commentators held no prophet came between them (IK); others counted four minor figures — three in the Judeo-Christian line (perhaps the unnamed "message bearers" of 36:14) and the Arab Khālid ibn Sinān al-ʿAbsī (Q, Z). Either way, the theological point stands: the silence is over, and with its ending expires the excuse *there came unto us no bearer of glad tidings, nor any warner*. The Qur'an treats unreached peoples with principled mercy — *We never punish until We send a messenger* (17:15) — which makes the arrival of a messenger an event of double edge: mercy to those who accept, proof against those who refuse. Several Companions reported the verse answered Madinan Jews who claimed no warner had come after Moses (Ṭ). The claim, whatever its sincerity, is now time-barred. History had paused; history has resumed; the court is in session.

**Ahl al-Fatrah: The Fate of Those in the Gap**

The *fatrah* (interval) raises one of theology's tenderest questions: what of those who lived *between* messengers — never reached, never warned? The Qur'an's principle is mercifully explicit — *"We never punish until We send a messenger"* (17:15) — and the tradition extends it: the unreached (including the *ahl al-fatrah*, the insane, the deaf-blind-untaught, and those to whom Islam never authentically arrived) face a *separate examination* on Resurrection Day. The ḥadīth corpus reports:

> *"Four will protest on Resurrection Day: the deaf man, the insane, the decrepit elderly, and one who died in the interval... God will take their covenant and send them a messenger commanding them to enter the Fire. Whoever obeys will find it cool and safe; whoever refuses will be dragged to it."* (Musnad Aḥmad 16301; Ibn Ḥibbān; graded ḥasan by several authorities)

Ibn Taymiyyah and Ibn al-Qayyim affirmed this testing-doctrine: Divine justice tries each soul by *its* light, never another's. The implications humble every judge of others: the remote tribesman, the misled-by-caricature Westerner, the child of hostile propaganda — all stand under 17:15's mercy, examined by standards we cannot apply. Verse 19's logic cuts both ways: messengers end excuses *for the reached* — and Mister Reach is God's department, not ours. Proclaim purely to all; presume damnation of none. The interval-people belong to the Most Merciful's special docket.

**Clarification as a Prophetic Office**

*Making things clear to you* (*yubayyinu lakum*) — clarification (*bayān*) is prophecy's standing office. Muḥammad ﷺ clarifies what was hidden (v. 15) and what was disputed (16:64); Jesus clarified disputes before him (43:63). Human beings left alone do not converge on truth; they accumulate obscurity — through error, forgery, faction, and simple forgetting. The messenger arrives as a restorer of legibility, the way a conservator cleans a darkened painting: the colors were always there, but centuries of grime made them arguable. And he comes as *bearer of glad tidings and warner* (*bashīr wa nadhīr*) — the Qur'an's inseparable pair (2:213; 4:165; 6:48; 18:56). Tidings without warning produce complacency; warning without tidings produces despair. The complete messenger carries both medicines and prescribes each to its patient: hope to the despairing, fear to the heedless. Any preaching that deals in only one has halved the prophetic pharmacy.

**Glad Tidings and Warning: The Twin Pharmacies**

*Bashīr* (glad-tider) and *nadhīr* (warner) are not two moods but two *medicines*, and the Prophet's ﷺ career shows the prescription-pad in use. To the despairing — the murderer of ninety-nine (al-Bukhārī 3470; Muslim 2766), the woman who lost all (cf. 39:53: *"despair not of God's mercy"*), Abū Sufyān the lifelong enemy — he dispensed *bushrā* (gladness): forgiveness wider than sin, welcome warmer than past. To the complacent — Quraysh's feasting chiefs, the slandering wealthy (104:1–9), oath-breaking hypocrites — he dispensed *nidhārah* (warning): Fire described in surgical detail. The Qur'an doses both across every audience because every soul contains both patients: despair lurking beneath bravado, complacency beneath piety. Preachers inherit the pad: diagnose before prescribing — the guilt-crushed need *bashīr* ("your Lord's mercy outruns your sins"), the sin-comfortable need *nadhīr* ("the Fire's fuel is men and stones," 2:24). Verse 19's final clause — *God is Powerful over all things* — underwrites both pharmacies: power to *fulfill* the tidings (Gardens, forgiveness, victory) and power to *execute* the warnings (Fire, disgrace, defeat). Prescribe both; trust the Power behind both.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:20

> **And when Moses said unto his people, "O my people! Remember God's Blessing upon you, when He appointed prophets among you, and appointed you kings, and gave you that which He gave unto no other in all the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses the Reminder: Gratitude Before Command**

The verse opens the sūrah's Moses narrative (vv. 20–26), and Moses begins exactly where the sūrah began with Muslims: *remember God's Blessing* (cf. 2:231; 5:7, 11; 14:6; 35:3). Only after gratitude comes the command to enter the land (v. 21). The sequence is leadership wisdom: obligation lands softly on a heart freshly reminded of favor, and lands as tyranny on a heart that feels only burden. Moses lists three blessings. *Prophets among you* — Abraham and the prophetic line through his descendants, the supreme honor a people can receive: God speaking from within their own ranks. *Appointed you kings* (*mulūk*) — read as sovereignty over their own affairs after Pharaoh's tyranny (Q): a slave people made self-governing, which is what "kingship" means at the communal level. *That which He gave unto no other in all the worlds* — manna and quails (2:57; 7:160; 20:80), prophets and signs (Q), goods of both worlds (IK) — unique *in their time*, the commentators caution (IK, Q), lest favor harden into supremacism (cf. 2:47, 122; 7:140; 45:16). Every community's zenith is dated; gratitude remembers the Giver without idolizing the gift.

**Prophets Among You: The Supreme Communal Honor**

*Jaʿala fīkum anbiyā'* — "He appointed prophets among you": Moses names first what heaven values most — not wealth, armies, or territory, but *prophets from your own loins*. Israel's honor-roll is unmatched: Abraham the Friend, Isaac, Jacob-Israel himself, Joseph the Truthful, Moses the Spoken-To, Aaron, David the Psalmist, Solomon the King-Prophet, Job, Jonah, Elijah, Elisha, Zechariah, John, Jesus — a constellation no other lineage holds. The Qur'an's point, pressed across its Israel-narratives: prophetic density *increases* accountability proportionally — *"to whom much is given, much is required"* finds its Qur'anic twin in *"those who disbelieved among Israel were cursed by David's and Jesus' tongues"* (5:78). Privilege ungratefully held becomes evidence at trial. Muslims inherit the logic exactly: the final Prophet ﷺ from *their* ranks, the final Book in *their* tongue — honor exceeding Israel's, accountability matching it. Communities blessed with scholars, saints, and righteous forebears should tremble with gratitude, not swagger with pedigree. Prophets among you is not a trophy but a trust — and trusts are audited.

**"Kings" from Slaves: The Political Miracle of the Exodus**

*Jaʿalakum mulūkan* — "appointed you kings": the commentators' reading (self-governance after Pharaoh (Q)) understates the miracle unless Pharaoh's Egypt is pictured: state infanticide of Israelite boys (2:49; 7:141), slave-labor quotas with straw withheld (Exodus 5), a god-king demanding worship. From this — *kingship*: a self-ruling people with law (Torah), courts (Deuteronomy 16–17), army, and land-grant. The political scientists' term is *decolonization*; the Qur'an's is *niʿmah* (blessing). The Exodus becomes scripture's master-template of liberation: God sides with the enslaved against empires — *"We desired to show favor to the oppressed in the land, making them leaders and heirs"* (28:5). Every liberation movement in history has drunk here; the Qur'an universalizes the vintage: Pharaoh is every tyranny, Egypt every oppression, the sea every impossible barrier, and *kingship* every people's God-given right to self-rule under Divine law. Moses' reminder thus carries marching orders: the God who made slaves into kings commands His kings to *act* royally — entering the land (v. 21), not cowering before giants. Liberation received obligates liberation *completed*.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:21

> **O my people! Enter the Holy Land, which God has prescribed for you, and do not turn back, or you shall become losers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Holy Land: A Qur'anic Geography of Blessing**

*Al-arḍ al-muqaddasah* — "the Holy Land." The Qur'an uses this resonant title only here, though the land's blessedness echoes elsewhere: the Farthest Mosque *whose precincts We have blessed* (17:1), and the land *We blessed for all peoples* given to Abraham and Lot (21:71). Reports locate it variously — Sinai, Syria, Jericho, the region spanning Palestine, Syria, and Jordan, or simply the Jerusalem sanctuary (R, Ṭ, Z); al-Ṭabarī judged its exact bounds unknowable, somewhere between Nile and Euphrates (Ṭ). Some commentators tie it to Abraham, shown the land and promised it to his progeny — which, Islam notes, includes Arabs through Ishmael (R, Z). *Which God has prescribed* (*kataba*) *for you* — inscribed, some say, in the Preserved Tablet (85:22; R, Ṭ, Z). Two observations follow. First, the land's holiness in the Qur'an is universal ("blessed for all peoples"), not an ethnic deed but a trust bound to obedience — as the following verses prove, the same generation that received the promise was barred from it for forty years. Land-promise and moral condition travel together; either alone distorts the covenant. Second, sacred geography exists to orient hearts, not to license conquests; the command here comes through a prophet, for a specific people, at a specific hour — not as a standing warrant for anyone holding this verse as a title-deed.

**Jerusalem in Islam: The Third Sanctuary**

The Holy Land's heart — Jerusalem (*al-Quds*, the Holy) and its sanctuary (*al-bayt al-muqaddas*) — holds Islam's third-holiest station, and the dossier deserves assembly. It was Islam's *first qiblah* (sixteen-seventeen months of Madinan prayer faced it, until 2:144 redirected to Makkah); the Prophet's ﷺ Night Journey (*isrā'*) traveled *to* the Farthest Mosque (17:1) and his Ascension (*miʿrāj*) launched from its rock; ʿUmar received its keys personally (15/636), granting the Pact of ʿUmar — lives, property, churches, and crosses protected; the Umayyads crowned it with the Dome (72/691), Islam's first monumental architecture. The Prophet ﷺ ranked it:

> *"Do not saddle mounts except to three mosques: this mosque of mine, the Sacred Mosque, and al-Aqṣā Mosque."* (al-Bukhārī 1189; Muslim 1397)

And prayer therein exceeds ordinary prayer manifold (Ibn Mājah 1408–1413, various gradings). Jerusalem's sanctity in Islam is thus *prophetic*, not merely Israelite: David's psalms, Solomon's temple, Zechariah's prayer, Mary's seclusion, Jesus' cradle-ministry, Muḥammad's ﷺ ascension — the city is scripture's shared stage. Muslims honor it as the prophets' common sanctuary, its stones sacred by association with *all* of them — which is why its occupation wounds and its peace would bless universally. The verse's *muqaddasah* (sanctified) outlives every flag: holiness no conquest confers, no occupation removes.

**"Do Not Turn Back": Retreat as Ruin**

*Do not turn back* (*lā tartaddū ʿalā adbārikum*) — do not retreat from the land without striving for it; or, in the deeper reading, do not turn your backs on the Divine command and the prophet's obedience. *Or you shall become losers* (*khāsirīn*) — the Qur'an's term for total, eschatological loss. The warning's logic is military, moral, and spiritual at once: armies that break before engaging lose more in rout than they would have in battle; souls that refuse commanded striving lose the very capacity for courage; and communities that refuse their appointed hour find the hour does not wait. Moses speaks as a commander who knows his troops' fragility — the blessing-recital of verse 20 was meant to steel them. What follows shows it did not. The verse stands as the Qur'an's clearest portrait of opportunity with an expiry date: *prescribed for you* — but only if you *enter*.

**Irtidād: The Grammar of Turning Back**

* Tartaddū* — from *riddah* (returning, turning back) — is the Qur'an's verb for apostasy itself (*murtadd*, 2:217; 5:54), and the overlap is intentional theology: military retreat from commanded striving and spiritual retreat from commanded belief share one root — *reversal after commitment*. The Qur'an's psychology of reversal: it begins with *feet* (reluctance to march), spreads to *hearts* (doubting the cause), and ends in *backs* (turned fully). Uḥud's archers enacted the physical form (abandoning the post for spoils — 3:152–153); the Hypocrites' desertion enacted the spiritual (9:42–47). The cure is *thabāt* (firmness): *"O believers! When you meet a force, stand firm and remember God much"* (8:45) — firmness *plus* remembrance, the verse's two anchors. Moses' *lā tartaddū* thus commands more than marching: it commands *non-reversal as identity* — the believer advances, pauses, regroups, even strategically withdraws (8:16 permits tactical turning) — but never *turns back* on the cause. Direction is destiny: faces toward the land inherit it; backs toward it inherit wandering (v. 26). Keep the face forward.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:22

> **They said, "O Moses! In this land are a domineering people. We shall not enter it till they leave it. But if they leave it, we shall enter."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scouts' Report and the Tyranny of Fear**

The backstory, preserved in the commentaries: Moses sent twelve men, one per tribe (cf. v. 12), to scout the land; they witnessed inhabitants of terrifying size and might — some reports say a single giant seized them and released them as a warning (Ṭ). Against Moses' orders they reported publicly: *in this land are a domineering people* (*qawman jabbārīn*). *Jabbār* denotes overwhelming, compelling force — the tyrant who bends all wills; as *al-Jabbār* it is God's Name (59:23), for absolute compulsion belongs to Him alone, while in human hands it is usurpation (Ṭs). The scouts' sentence is fear disguised as intelligence. Every reconnaissance mission in history has faced this corruption: the scout who lets terror write his report disarms his own army more thoroughly than any enemy. Moses had assured them of victory with righteousness and obedience (cf. v. 12) — but assurance heard through fear sounds like fantasy. The verse diagnoses a permanent organizational disease: risk reports drafted by the faint-hearted become self-fulfilling prophecies of paralysis.

**Jabbārīn: Giants, Tyrants, and the Name They Stole**

*Jabbārīn* (plural of *jabbār*) fuses two terrors: *gigantic* (the Canaanites' reported stature — Numbers 13:33's "we were grasshoppers") and *tyrannical* (compelling force crushing resistance). The Qur'an's seven human *jabbārs* are all villains: Pharaoh's(expected) defier? — no: every occurrence condemns — *"every obstinate tyrant"* despaired (14:15), *"a tyrant wretched"* (19:14, 32 — what prophets are *not*), *"you seize like tyrants"* (26:130). Against them stands *al-Jabbār*, God's Name (59:23): the Irresistible Mender (the root also means bone-setting — *jabr*: compulsion that *heals*). The theology: *jabr* (irresistible force) belongs to God alone because only His compulsion mends; human compulsion fractures. The scouts' error was thus metaphysical before military: attributing *jabr*-grade irresistibility to Canaanites — treating giants as unbeatables — while forgetting *al-Jabbār* stood in their own ranks (v. 12's *"I am with you"*). Every intimidated believer replays it: the problem inflated to *jabbār*, the Helper shrunk to absentee. The verse corrects the proportions: giants are large creatures; God is *al-Jabbār*. No contest.

**"We Shall Not Enter Till They Leave": The Fantasy of Costless Victory**

*We shall not enter it till they leave it; but if they leave it, we shall enter* — the demand for a struggle-free triumph. Enemies who obligingly vacate, lands that conquer themselves, goals achieved without opposition: the Israelites articulate the daydream every fearful soul nurses. The Qur'an's answer, unfolding across the next verses, is that God does not work that way — not because He cannot (He could empty the land in an instant) but because *entering* is the point. The struggle is the formation; the victory without struggle would leave the victors unformed, still slaves in their souls though kings on the map. Modern readers meet the same demand in gentler forms — the student who wants knowledge without study, the reformer who wants change without conflict, the believer who wants paradise without striving. The verse holds up a mirror to all costless-victory fantasies: God prescribes lands, but He prescribes *entering* them.

**Ṭālūt's River: The Qur'an's Other Test of Entering**

The Qur'an pairs this refusal with its mirror-image — an army that *did* enter against giants. Ṭālūt (Saul) marched Israel against Jālūt (Goliath), testing troops at the river (most drank and were dismissed — 2:249), until the remnant few faced the giant host and despaired: *"No power have we today against Jālūt and his forces."* The faithful among them answered:

> *"How often has a small company overcome a numerous company, by God's leave! And God is with the steadfast."* (Qur'an 2:249)

Then David slew Goliath (2:251) — the *entering* rewarded, the land inherited. The juxtaposition is curriculum: verses 22–24 show refusal's wages (wandering), 2:249–251 show entering's wages (kingdom) — same enemy-type (giants), same God, opposite choices. The river-test adds the mechanism: *discipline before battle* — those who mastered thirst mastered fear; those who failed the small trial failed the great. Israel at Kadesh failed even to *reach* the river; Israel under Ṭālūt drank discipline and slew giants. The lesson for every daunting enterprise: shrink the problem to its true size (giants, not *al-Jabbār*), pass the small disciplines (rivers of appetite), enter by the gate (v. 23) — and watch small companies overcome numerous ones. The formula has never failed; only the unwilling have.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:23

> **Two men whom God had blessed among those who feared [Him] said, "Enter upon them by the gate, for once you have entered it, you will be victors. And trust in God, if you are believers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Courageous Minority: Joshua and Caleb Against the Crowd**

Against ten fearful scouts stand *two men whom God had blessed among those who feared Him* — identified as Joshua (Yūshaʿ ibn Nūn) and Caleb (Kālib ibn Yūfannah), matching the Biblical account (R, Ṭ, Th, Z); a minor report makes them converted inhabitants who returned with the scouts (R, Ṭ). Ten to two: the mathematics of courage in every age. Majorities do not confer truth — they confer comfort — and the verse pointedly attributes the minority's stance to *blessing* (*anʿama'Llāhu ʿalayhimā*), not temperament. Courage here is grace before it is character. Their counsel is tactical as well as spiritual: *enter upon them by the gate* — assault the strongpoint directly rather than besieging it with dread; *once you have entered, you will be victors* — the decisive moment is the threshold, for commitment itself transforms the equation. Every difficult enterprise confirms it: the project begun in earnest generates resources the project merely contemplated never reveals. Beginnings are divinely subsidized; hesitations are self-taxed.

**Joshua: The Blessed Scout Who Became Prophet**

Yūshaʿ ibn Nūn (Joshua) rewards biography, for his arc vindicates the verse's *blessing* fully. Moses' devoted attendant (*fatāhu*, 18:60–65 — the journey to Khiḍr), present at revelation's mountain, spy faithful among spies fearful — then Moses' successor-prophet, leading the *next* generation across the Jordan into the very land this generation refused. The traditions add his miracle: the sun delayed at his plea that Friday's battle finish before Sabbath — *"the sun was never withheld for any human but Joshua"* (Aḥmad 2:325; cf. al-Bukhārī 3124). Consider the symmetry: the scout who said *enter* was granted *entry*; the generation that said *we shall sit* (v. 24) sat into graves. Blessing (*niʿmah*) in the verse proves prophetic: God's favor on Joshua flowed *through* his courage into his succession. Caleb's quieter fidelity earned the same entry (both survived to inherit — Numbers 14:30). The verse thus teaches career-spirituality: obscure faithfulness (an attendant, a scout) precedes public trust (a prophet, a conqueror). Serve the Moses of your age faithfully; speak courage when ten speak fear; and the land refused to cowards opens to the courageous — sometimes with the sun itself pausing to watch.

**Trust as the Proof of Belief**

*And trust in God, if you are believers* (*wa ʿala'Llāhi fa-tawakkalū in kuntum mu'minīn*) — trust (*tawakkul*) is made the *test* of belief, not its ornament. The conditional stings: to refuse trust when commanded is to fail the very thing one claims. The two men's formula binds means and reliance perfectly — *enter* (take the means, march to the gate) *and trust* (rest the outcome on God). Neither quietism (trust without entering) nor presumption (entering without trust) passes. The verse thus becomes a standing examination for every believer facing a fearful majority: when the ten speak prudence and the two speak trust, which voice will you amplify? History remembers the names of Joshua and Caleb; the ten have no names, only a number. Courage is remembered; panic is merely counted.

**"Enter by the Gate": Threshold Theology**

*Udkhulū ʿalayhimu'l-bāb* — "enter upon them *by the gate*": the tactic encodes theology. Gates (*abwāb*) in the Qur'an are decision-points: gardens entered by gates (39:73), Hell entered by gates (39:71–72), provision descending through heaven's gates (7:40, inverted for deniers). The two men's counsel — assault the strongpoint *directly*, through its gate, not around it — rejects both avoidance (waiting for evacuation, v. 22) and infiltration (scheming without commitment). *Fa-idhā dakhaltumūhu fa-innakum ghālibūn* — "once you have entered it, you *will be* victors": entering *constitutes* victory's certainty — the threshold crossed in trust triggers the promise. Military history confirms the pattern (burning boats, crossing Rubicons — commitment generating victory), but the verse's deeper register is spiritual: every soul faces gates — repentance's gate, truth-speaking's gate, generosity's gate — before which dread gathers like giants. The counsel stands eternal: *enter by the gate*. Step through; the entering itself enrolls Divine aid; the threshers of dread discover giants hollow. Victors are simply enterers whom God met inside.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:24

> **They said, "O Moses! We shall never enter it, so long as they remain therein. So go forth, thou and thy Lord, and fight! We shall sit here."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Shall Sit Here": The Anatomy of Collective Cowardice**

*Lan nadkhulahā abadan* — "We shall never enter it" — *abadan*, never, ever: fear absolutized into policy. Then the sentence that rang through fourteen centuries of Muslim memory: *go forth, thou and thy Lord, and fight; we shall sit here* (*qāʿidūn*). *Go forth, thou and thy Lord* means "go with thy Lord's help," since God does not "go forth" corporeally (Ṭ). The insult is double: they draft the prophet for a war they refuse, and they speak of *thy* Lord — distancing themselves from the very God whose land they were promised. Sitting (*quʿūd*) becomes, in Qur'anic idiom, the posture of dereliction — the opposite of *striving* and *entering*. Psychologists of groups recognize the moment: responsibility diffused across a crowd evaporates entirely; each Israelite felt fractionally braver for sharing his cowardice with thousands. The verse warns every community that cowardice, pooled, feels like consensus. A thousand voices saying "we shall sit" do not make sitting wise; they make it unanimous, which is a different and far more dangerous thing.

**Qāʿidūn: The Qur'an's Gallery of Sitters**

*Qāʿidūn* (sitters) becomes a Qur'anic type — and the gallery of sitters convicts by comparison. The Hypocrites *sat* from Tabūk, mocking marchers (9:46, 81, 83–84, 86–87) — *"God hates their marching forth, so He held them back, and it was said: sit with the sitters"* (9:46): sitting as *sentence*, abandonment made audible. The Bedouin *sat* with excuses (9:90; 48:11, 15–16). Against them: *"Not equal are the sitters among believers — except the disabled — and the strivers with wealth and selves"* (4:95) — striving outranks sitting by degrees unbridgeable. The *Excused* sitters (blind, lame, ill, resourceless — 9:91; 48:17) prove the rule: legitimate inability *exempts*, illegitimate unwillingness *indicts*. Israel's sitting here compounds all guilt: no disability, no excuse, Divine guarantee in hand (v. 12), giants before them *and* God with them. Their *qāʿidūn* is sitting's archetype — comfort chosen over command with every advantage supplied. The reader's self-examination writes itself: where am I *sitting* despite ability, guarantee, and command? The verse photographs every armchair — then develops the negative in verse 26.

**"We Shall Fight Alongside You": The Companions' Reversal**

The Prophet's Companions took this verse as a personal warning. At Ḥudaybiyah (6/628) — outnumbered, barred from Makkah, facing the powerful Quraysh — al-Miqdād ibn al-Aswad declared: "By God, we will not be like Israel when they said to their prophet, 'Go forth, thou and thy Lord, and fight; we shall sit here' — but [we say]: 'Go forth, thou and thy Lord, and fight; verily we shall fight alongside you'" (Ṭ; a variant attaches the scene to Badr, 2/624 (IK)). The Prophet's ﷺ face shone with joy at the words, the reports add. This is how scripture is meant to be read — not as ancient Israel's embarrassment but as every generation's examination. The Companions heard "we shall sit here" and resolved to stand; the reader who hears it as mere history has missed the examination entirely. Each generation gets its own gate, its own giants, its own choice between sitting and entering. The verse asks only which sentence will be yours.

**Miqdād at Badr: The Standing in Full**

The reversal-scene deserves its Badr setting (IK's variant), for Badr *is* Islam's anti-Kadesh. Outnumbered three-to-one (313 vs ~1000), fasting Ramaḍān, facing Quraysh's cavalry with two horses and seventy camels — the Prophet ﷺ consulted his men. Abū Bakr and ʿUmar spoke loyalty; then al-Miqdād ibn ʿAmr rose and uttered the immortal reversal: *"O Messenger of God! Proceed as God commands. We are with you. By God, we shall not say as Israel said to Moses: 'go, thou and thy Lord, and fight; we shall sit here' — but: go, thou and thy Lord, and fight — we shall fight alongside you. By Him who sent you with truth, if you led us to Bark al-Ghimād* [Yemen's edge — i.e., the world's end], *we would plunge with you."* The Prophet's ﷺ face beamed; then Saʿd ibn Muʿādh pledged the Anṣār's like resolve (Ibn Hishām; al-Bukhārī 3952; IK). Badr's outcome — the few routing the many, angels descending (8:9–12) — is verse 23's promise historically receipted: *entered → victors*. Kadesh and Badr stand as scripture's controlled experiment: same odds-type, same Divine guarantee, opposite answers — wandering versus triumph. Every daunting *bāb* (gate) since re-poses the question; Miqdād's sentence remains the model answer. Memorize it; the examination is continuous.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:25

> **He said, "My Lord! I have no power over anyone save myself and my brother; so separate us from the iniquitous people!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Lament: Leadership at Its Limit**

Moses has preached blessing (v. 20), commanded entry (v. 21), watched his scouts spread panic (v. 22), seen his two faithful men overruled (v. 23), and heard his people's final refusal (v. 24). Now he turns from the people to God: *I have no power* (*lā amliku*) *over anyone save myself and my brother* — no control, and by extension no moral accountability, beyond himself and Aaron. Al-Zamakhsharī wonders why Joshua and Caleb are excluded: perhaps Moses, in despair, cannot vouch for anyone's loyalty but a fellow prophet's; or perhaps *brother* here is religious brotherhood — all faithful followers (Z). Either reading captures the loneliness of failed leadership. Every reformer, parent, and teacher reaches this hour: the message delivered perfectly, the audience unmoved, the limits of human agency exposed.
