Moses' response is the model — he does not coerce, manipulate, or despair of God; he *reports* to God. Prophets are not guarantors of results but deliverers of messages; outcomes belong to the Sender. The leader who learns this sleeps at night; the leader who confuses delivery with conversion burns out or turns tyrant.

**Aaron Beside Moses: Brotherhood in the Breach**

*Illā nafsī wa akhī* — "save myself and my brother": Aaron's (Hārūn's) inclusion honors the Qur'an's great prophetic partnership. Moses *requested* Aaron — *"appoint for me a minister from my family: Aaron my brother; strengthen my back with him"* (20:29–32) — and God granted: *"you are granted your request, O Moses"* (20:36). Aaron shared the mission to Pharaoh (20:42–44), the Torah's forty nights (as deputy — 7:142), and the people's leadership — yet bore blame for the calf-crisis meekly (*"O son of my mother, seize me not by beard nor head"* — 20:94). His presence here, as Moses' sole-certain ally, teaches leadership's deepest need: *one* trusted companion outweighs multitudes. The Prophet ﷺ had Abū Bakr (the cave's second — 9:40); every Moses needs his Aaron — the brother who stays when crowds refuse, who deputizes faithfully, who absorbs blame gently. Organizations should note the structure: paired leadership (visionary + sustainer) outlasts solo heroism. And the *ukhūwwah* (brotherhood) reading (Z) widens it: every faithful follower is the leader's "brother" — the certain remnant in every refusing crowd. Count brothers, not crowds; two certain outweigh two million wavering.

**"Separate Us": The Prayer for Moral Distinction**

*So separate us* (*fa'fqur baynanā* — literally "decide, judge between us") *from the iniquitous people* (*al-qawm al-fāsiqīn*) — a plea for Divine judgment distinguishing the obedient from the rebellious (Ṭ, Z). *Fisq* is breach, rupture — the soul broken out of obedience's enclosure. Moses asks not for vengeance but for *distinction*: let it be clear, in history's record and eternity's court, that he and the faithful did not consent. The prayer matters because collective punishment was coming (v. 26), and the innocent dread being swept with the guilty. God's answer will show His precision: the land forbidden for forty years, yet Moses and Aaron sustained without hardship within the wandering (R, Z) — like Abraham in the cool fire (21:69). The lesson: when judgment falls on a community, God does not blur persons into crowds. The faithful suffer the weather of their people's winter, but the frost does not reach their roots.

**Furqān Prayers: Asking God to Distinguish**

*Ufqur* (decide, separate) — from *furqān* (criterion, separator) — places Moses' plea in scripture's *distinction-prayer* tradition. Abraham prayed it against his idolatrous father and people (26:86–89, implicitly; 60:4, explicitly: *"we disown you"*). Noah prayed its flood-form (*"my Lord, I am overcome, so help"* — 54:10). The Prophet ﷺ prayed its protection-form at every persecution-peak. The prayer's logic: when communities fuse obedience with rebellion indistinguishably, the righteous risk *guilt by association* — historically (punishment's sweep) and morally (compromise's stain). God's answers always *distinguish precisely*: Noah's ark from flood, Lot's household from brimstone (save his wife — distinction *within* households, 66:10), the cave-sleepers from Decian persecution. Verse 26 will show the mechanism again: wandering shared geographically, suffering unshared spiritually — Moses and Aaron hardship-free inside hardship (R, Z). The practical discipline: in corrupt environments, *pray distinction daily* — then *practice* it (non-participation, private worship, truthful speech). God separates the separator-prayers from the compromised — in history's record first, eternity's court finally.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:26

> **He said, "Then verily it shall be forbidden them for forty years, while they shall wander on the earth. So grieve not for the iniquitous people."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Forty Years of Wandering: Punishment as Pedagogy**

*Forbidden them for forty years* — the land (*muḥarramah ʿalayhim*), barred either physically or as devotional prohibition (R); *wandering* (*yatīhūn*) *on the earth*. The verse may also be read "forbidden them [permanently]; for forty years they shall wander" — permanent for the disobedient adults, who would die before entry (Ṭ). And so it happened: of that generation only Joshua and Caleb entered, under Joshua's later leadership as prophet after Moses (Q, Ṭ, Z; cf. Numbers 14); some reports have Moses himself entering Jericho and slaying a giant (R, Ṭ, Z). Why forty years? A generation must pass. Slaves freed from Egypt carried Egypt in their souls — the reflexes of the whipped cannot build a free polity. God did not re-educate them; He outwaited them, raising desert-born children without memories of chains. Organizations replicate the pattern: cultures of fear cannot be reformed by memo, only outgrown by new cohorts formed under new norms. The wandering was thus mercy disguised as penalty — forty years of manna, water from the rock (2:60; 7:160), and divine sustenance (Ṭ, Z) while a slave generation died and a free one grew. Sometimes God's kindest answer to "we cannot change" is: very well — your children will.

**Tīh: The Geography and Spirituality of Wandering**

*Yatīhūna fī'l-arḍ* — "wandering, bewildered, on the earth": *tīh* is not travel but *lostness* — movement without arrival, the desert's disorientation made communal. Traditions locate the *tīh* in Sinai's wilderness — some adding the miraculous detail that the wanderers marched all day only to wake where they started, the desert folding around them. Manna descended (2:57), quails flew in, water gushed from twelve springs (2:60; 7:160) — *sustained* wandering: punishment without destruction, discipline with provision. The Sufis read *tīh* as the soul's station between liberation and arrival: freed from Egypt (sin) yet unfit for the Land (presence) — wandering the desert of purification until slave-reflexes die. Every serious spiritual path knows this middle desert: conversion's Red Sea behind, holiness's Jordan ahead, and years of manna-monotony between. The verse counsels the wanderers: the desert is *fem* — forty years with manna beats four hundred with chains; keep marching (manna gathers daily — hoarding rots, 2:57-comm.), drink from struck rocks (grace in hardness), and trust the wandering ends at inheritance. Lostness with God provisions; settlement without Him starves.

**"Grieve Not": Comfort for the Prophet Who Prayed the Prayer**

*So grieve not for the iniquitous people* — addressed to Moses, who was dismayed at the severity his own supplication had invoked and regretted it (R). The tenderness is extraordinary: God consoles the prophet for the answered prayer. It teaches that intercessors may plead for judgment in righteous frustration and then ache at judgment's weight — both the anger and the ache are human, and both are held by God. But the command stands: *grieve not*. Grief for the defiant, past its proper measure, becomes a quarrel with justice. The prophet must release his people to the consequences they chose — the land remains *prescribed* (v. 21) in the Tablet, held in suspension (Z), not cancelled. Judgment here is never God's last word about a people; it is His severe middle word. The last word belongs to mercy, in its season, for those who turn.

**Grief's Limits: When Sorrow Becomes Insubordination**

*Lā ta'sa* — "grieve not" — belongs to the Qur'an's *grief-discipline* larger than this scene: the Prophet ﷺ forbidden grief over rejecters (v. 41, 68; 3:176; 6:33; 10:65; 16:127; 18:6: *"perhaps you will kill yourself with grief over them"*), Jacob permitted grief over Joseph (12:84–86 — personal loss, not defiant judgment), believers forbidden grief-mongering (*"grieve not, God is with us"* — 9:40). The taxonomy: grief over *loss* is human and healing; grief over *justice* questions the Judge; grief over *rejecters* exceeds the warner's portfolio (delivery, not reception). Moses' grief here mixed all three — love for his people (licit), horror at severity (human), implicit second-guessing of his own prayed-for sentence (needing correction). God's *lā ta'sa* surgically removes the third while honoring the first two: love them still, ache humanly — but *release* the sentence. Leaders, parents, and preachers need the surgery regularly: having warned faithfully and prayed sincerely, release outcomes to Wisdom. Grief thatsurrenders to God consoles; grief that argues with Him corrodes. *Lā ta'sa* — feel, release, march on.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:27

> **And recite unto them, with truth, the account of Adam's two sons, when they each offered a sacrifice, and it was accepted from one of them, though not accepted from the other. One said, "I will surely slay you!" [The other] said, "God accepts only from the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why Cain and Abel Here: Envy as the Sūrah's Hidden Theme**

*Recite unto them, with truth* (*bi'l-ḥaqq*) — the Cain-and-Abel account (cf. Genesis 4) arrives as humanity's prototype of fratricidal violence. But why *here*, in a sūrah about covenant and the People of the Book? The commentators trace the threads: *stretch forth hands* against the Prophet (v. 11) echoes the *stretching forth* of the murderous hand (v. 28); spiritual envy toward Muḥammad ﷺ (cf. 2:90, 109, 213; 3:19; 4:54; 45:17; 5:59) mirrors Cain's envy of Abel (R); and the account closes with a ruling *prescribed for the Children of Israel* (v. 32) on murder's gravity (Z). Al-Ṭabarī adds a pastoral purpose: Muslims are to answer plotters with Abel's forbearance, citing the ḥadīth, "God gives you the example of Adam's two sons; take the path of the better, leave the path of the worse" (Ṭ). The sūrah's deepest diagnosis thus surfaces: beneath doctrinal disputes and broken treaties runs the oldest poison — seeing another's acceptance and wanting him dead for it. Every honor killing, sectarian bombing, and assassination of the righteous is Cain's hand restretched.

**Ḥasad: Envy as the First Sin in Heaven and Earth**

Cain's motive — *ḥasad* (envy) at Abel's acceptance — makes this narrative envy-scripture's ground zero, and the dossier demands assembly. Envy is the *first* sin in heaven (Satan envying Adam — 2:34; 7:12: *"I am better than him"*) and the first on earth (Cain envying Abel) — the only sin inaugural in both realms. The Prophet ﷺ warned:

> *"Beware envy, for envy devours good deeds as fire devours wood."* (Abū Dāwūd 4903)

And: *"Envy is not permitted except in two: a man God gave wealth, empowering him over it in truth; and a man God gave wisdom, judging and teaching by it"* (al-Bukhārī 73; Muslim 816) — *ghibṭah* (emulative admiration) permitted, *ḥasad* (begrudging-hope-of-removal) banned. The Qur'an's envy-gallery convicts across history: Joseph's brothers (12:8–9), Israel envying Muḥammad ﷺ (2:109; 4:54), Quraysh envying revelation's recipient (43:31: *"why was this Qur'an not sent to a great man of the two towns?"*). The cure-protocol: bless the envied (*"mā shā'a'Llāh, bāraka'Llāh"*), conceal blessings prudently (12:5), compete in goods (v. 48's *fastabiqū*), and beg refuge (113:5: *"from the envier's evil when he envies"*). Cain's story is envy untreated to term: begrudged acceptance → threatened murder → committed fratricide → eternal loss. Treat envy at begrudging; the alternative is burial-lessons from crows (v. 31).

**Two Sacrifices: What Made One Acceptable**

Cain (Qābīl) the crop-grower offered poor produce; Abel (Hābīl) the herdsman offered a prized fattened sheep (Ṭ) — some add a rivalry over marrying Cain's twin sister (R, Ṭ, Z), though al-Ṭabarī doubts this, insisting both sought nearness to God (Ṭ). *Qurbān* (sacrifice) comes from *q-r-b*, nearness: sacrifice is nearness-technology, the offering that bridges creature and Creator. Like Genesis, the Qur'an does not state why one was accepted — Abel himself supplies the criterion: *God accepts only from the reverent* (*muttaqīn*) — with proper *taqwā*, reverent fear (Ṭ, Z). The offering's quality revealed the heart's quality: Abel gave his best because he revered the Recipient; Cain gave his worst because he did not. Al-Ṭabarī extends the principle to Islam's own "sacrifices" — prayer, fasting, charity (*qarraba* can denote giving charity (R, Z)) — crowned by the ḥajj's own *ʿĪd al-Aḍḥā* slaughter, of which God says:

> *"Neither their flesh nor their blood reaches God, but the reverence from you reaches Him."* (Qur'an 22:37)

The altar tests nothing but the offerer. Two identical acts — two opposite receptions. The difference was never in the sheep or the grain; it was in the breast behind them.

**Acceptance Theology: What Makes Deeds Rise**

*Innamā yataqabbalu'Llāhu mina'l-muttaqīn* — "God accepts only from the reverent": Abel's sentence founds Islam's *acceptance-theology* (*fiqh al-qabūl*), and its conditions deserve codifying. The tradition names acceptance's pillars: *īmān* (faith — deeds without it voided, v. 5/53), *ikhlāṣ* (sincerity — *"whoever does a deed associating others with Me, I abandon him and his association"* — Muslim 2985), *mutābaʿah* (Prophetic conformity — *"whoever does a deed not in our matter, it is rejected"* — Muslim 1718), *taqwā* (this verse — reverent quality), and *ḥalāl* sustenance (*"God accepts only the good"* — Muslim 1015). ʿUmar's caliphal anxiety modeled it: *"If I knew God accepted one prostration from me, I would wish for death"* — acceptance-uncertainty as devotion's engine. The despair-antidote: acceptance depends on *quality sincerely offered*, not *quantity anxiously amassed* — Abel's one sheep outweighed Cain's harvest. Offer your best (time, wealth, prayer), purify motive, follow the Sunnah, eat lawful — then *hope* acceptance without claiming it. The altar still tests only offerers; the criterion still reads *muttaqīn*.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:28

> **Even if you stretch forth your hand against me to slay me, I shall not stretch forth my hand against you to slay you. Truly I fear God, Lord of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Abel's Refusal: The Strongest Weakness in Scripture**

*Even if you stretch forth your hand against me to slay me, I shall not stretch forth mine against you* — Abel's sentence has puzzled and inspired for fourteen centuries. Why not resist? The commentators offer layers: to expose the enormity of fratricide by refusing to dignify it as combat (R, Ṭ); because defensive killing was not yet legislated in humanity's dawn (Ṭ, Z); or — most subtly — Abel renounces only the *intent to kill*, whether preemptively or in self-defense, not necessarily all self-protection (Q, R). Some reports have Cain striking the sleeping Abel, mooting the question (Q, R); others suggest Abel judged being killed better than killing (R). Al-Qurṭubī records the verse's afterlife: pious early Muslims like ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, refusing to take sides in the First Civil War (36–40/656–61), cited Abel — greater strength lies in abstaining from violence against a fellow monotheist, even a disobedient one, than in joining it. The verse does not abolish self-defense (elsewhere legislated); it establishes its limit. There are conflicts — brother against brother, Muslim against Muslim — where victory costs more than defeat, and the refusal to kill, even at the price of being killed, is the last prophetic witness available. Abel lost his life and won eternity's argument.

**Fitnah Ethics: Abel's Sentence in Muslim Civil Wars**

Abel's refusal became Islam's *fitnah*-ethic charter — the jurisprudence of Muslim-on-Muslim strife — and its applications demand tracing. The Prophet ﷺ foresaw civil strife and legislated Abel's posture:

> *"There will be tribulations in which the sitting is better than the standing, the standing better than the walking, the walking better than the running. Whoever seeks them out will be swallowed by them. Whoever finds refuge, let him take it."* (al-Bukhārī 3601; Muslim 2886)

And specifically: *"Be the better of Adam's two sons"* (Abū Dāwūd 4259 — Ṭ's cited version). The First Fitnah (36–40/656–61) tested it: Ibn ʿUmar, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, Muḥammad ibn Maslamah, and Usāmah ibn Zayd refused both ʿAlī's and Muʿāwiyah's banners (Q) — not from indifference (they wept over the blood) but from Abel-conviction: killing fellow *qiblah*-facers for disputable claims forfeits more than abstention risks. Later jurists calibrated: rebellion against patent tyranny may obligate resistance (the majority's *khurūj* conditions — manifest disbelief, capability, proportionality), but *ambiguous* Muslim-vs-Muslim bloodshed defaults to Abel. The ethic scales down perfectly: family feuds, community splits, online pile-ons — wherever believers war believers over contestable claims, Abel's sentence counsels: *refuse the killing-hand, fear the Worlds' Lord*. Victory in fitnah belongs to clean hands.

**"I Fear God, Lord of the Worlds": Reverence as Restraint**

Abel's reason is one clause: *innī akhāfu'Llāha rabba'l-ʿālamīn* — "Truly I fear God, Lord of the worlds." Not "I fear you," not "I am unable," but God-fearing. *Khawf* here is reverent dread — the awareness that the universe has a Lord to whom hands will answer. Notice *Lord of the worlds* (*rabb al-ʿālamīn*): Abel invokes God's universal lordship at the moment his brother acts as though no Lord existed. Every murderer is a practical atheist for the duration of the murder; every refuser of murder, a theologian. The sentence also quietly universalizes the story beyond two brothers in a field: the Lord of *all worlds* watches *every* stretched hand in *every* age. Street violence, domestic brutality, state terror — each perpetrator stretches a hand Abel refused to stretch, under the gaze of the Lord Abel feared. Civilization advances exactly to the extent that Abel's sentence multiplies in human breasts.

**Khawf and Rajā': Fear's Station in the Soul**

*Innī akhāfu'Llāh* — "I fear God": Abel's motive names *khawf* (fear), worship's first wing alongside *rajā'* (hope) and *ḥubb* (love). The tradition's ornithology: Ibn al-Qayyim's bird — head is love, wings are fear and hope; beheaded (loveless), the bird dies; wing-clipped (fearless or hopeless), it cannot fly (*Madārij al-Sālikīn*). Fear's stations ascend: fear of *punishment* (beginners — Fire deters), fear of *reckoning* (adepts — the Audit awes), fear of *veiling* (lovers — distance from the Beloved terrifies most). Abel's fear blends all three: Fire named (v. 29), reckoning implied, and — *Lord of the worlds* — the cosmic Lord whose displeasure dwarfs all. The Prophet ﷺ calibrated fear's dosage: *"If the believer knew God's punishment, none would hope for Paradise; if the disbeliever knew God's mercy, none would despair of Paradise"* (Muslim 2755) — fear and hope each totalized, held simultaneously. Abel feared *before* striking distance — preventive fear, the only kind that restrains. Cultivate it likewise: picture the Audit *before* the anger peaks, the Fire *before* the hand rises. Fear arriving early is mercy; arriving late, it is regret.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:29

> **I desire that you should be burdened with my sin and your sin and so become one of the inhabitants of the Fire. Such is the recompense of the wrongdoers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"My Sin and Your Sin": Unpacking Abel's Hard Sentence**

*I desire that you bear my sin and your sin* — the verse's most exegetically delicate clause. It cannot mean transferred guilt, for the Qur'an is adamant that *no bearer bears another's burden* (6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38). The commentators resolve it by ellipsis: "my sin" means *the sin of killing me* (*ithm qatlī*, with *qatl* elided), while "your sin" means Cain's accumulated disobedience (R, Ṭ, Z). Abel thus declares he will add no counter-violence for which *he* would answer, leaving Cain to carry the full weight of both the murder and his prior record. "I desire" (*innī urīdu*) is not vindictiveness but forensic clarity — Abel states the outcome, leaving the choice to Cain. There is a courtroom gravity here: the victim, refusing vengeance, simply reads the sentence the crime carries. The murderer who expected a fight receives instead a verdict.

**No Transferred Burdens: Individual Responsibility Defended**

Abel's sentence, rightly parsed, *defends* the Qur'an's non-transfer axiom — and the axiom's architecture deserves exhibiting, for cultures of collective guilt constantly assail it. The texts: *"no bearer bears another's burden"* (6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38), *"every soul earns only against itself"* (6:164), *"whoever is guided is guided for himself; whoever strays, strays against himself"* (17:15). Against apparent exceptions the tradition harmonizes precisely: *inherited* consequences (children suffering parents' sins *in this world* — poverty, stigma) are *trials*, not *charges* — no Hereafter-debit transfers. *Led-astray followers'* additional burdens on misleaders (16:25: *"they bear their burdens fully, plus burdens of those they misled"*) punish the *leading*, not transfer the *led's* — each pays his own; the leader pays his plus his crime of misleading. *Intercession* (granted by permission, 2:255) is *mercy's* transfer, never *guilt's*. Abel's "my sin" = "my *killing's* sin" fits perfectly: Cain pays for *his* act against Abel, not Abel's acts. The doctrine liberates universally: no blood-guilt, no ancestral curse, no communal damnation — each soul opens its *own* book (17:13–14). Judge persons, never pedigrees; audit selves, never lineages.

**The First Eschatology: Fire for the Wrongdoer**

*So become one of the inhabitants of the Fire; such is the recompense of the wrongdoers* (*ẓālimīn*) — among the Qur'an's earliest narrated warnings of Hell, placed on righteous lips at humanity's dawn. *Ẓulm* (wrongdoing) is putting things where they do not belong — and murder is *ẓulm*'s archetype: a soul removed from its body before its term, a brother made into a corpse. That the warning comes from the victim rather than a prophet gives it unique pathos: Abel evangelizes his killer even while refusing to fight him, offering truth to ears about to close forever. The Prophet ﷺ later confirmed Abel's doctrine of murder's cumulative burden:

> *"No soul is killed unjustly but that a share of its blood is upon the first son of Adam, for he was the first to institute killing."* (al-Bukhārī 3335; Muslim 1677)

Every murderer pays his own crime; the institution's founder pays a commission on all. Cain's innovation — solving envy by homicide — became humanity's most franchised sin, and the franchise fees accumulate against him still.

**Sunnah Sayyi'ah: The Franchise of Evil Precedent**

The "first son of Adam" ḥadīth founds the *sunnah sayyi'ah* (evil precedent) doctrine — and its twin, *sunnah ḥasanah* (good precedent) — codified in the Prophet's ﷺ charity-sermon:

> *"Whoever institutes a good practice in Islam earns its reward plus the rewards of all who act upon it, undiminished. Whoever institutes an evil practice bears its burden plus the burdens of all who act upon it, undiminished."* (Muslim 1017)

The arithmetic staggers: innovators of guidance (compilers of ḥadīth, builders of schools, authors of beneficial books) accrue centuries of commission; innovators of corruption (inventors of scams, pioneers of vice-industries, normalizers of cruelty) accrue centuries of debit — Cain's franchise-model universalized. Abel's warning to Cain thus carries an unspoken corollary Cain couldn't hear: *you are not merely killing a brother; you are incorporating Murder Inc., with yourself as perpetual chairman*. Every "first" matters disproportionately: the first cheat in an office, the first lie in a friendship, the first compromise in a movement — precedents metastasize. Conversely every first-integrity compounds redemptively. Choose your incorporations carefully: you chair every franchise you found, forever.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:30

> **Then his soul prompted him to slay his brother, and he slew him, and thus came to be among the losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Soul That Commands Evil: Inside the First Murderer**

*Fa-ṭawwaʿat lahu nafsuhu qatla akhīh* — "Then his soul *prompted* (literally: made easy, made compliant, emboldened) him to slay his brother" (Aj, R, Ṭ, Z). *Ṭawwaʿa* is the psychology of temptation in one verb: the soul does not merely suggest evil but *facilitates* it — smoothing objections, minimizing horror, supplying technique (some reports: Satan showed him the two rocks; the victim slept (R, Ṭ, Z)). *Nafs* here is the lower soul, the seat of passion:

> *"Surely the soul commands to evil."* (Qur'an 12:53, Joseph's confession)

Al-Qushayrī reflects that once the soul, driven by vain desire, has settled its evil intention (v. 27), subsequent appeals — Abel's whole noble speech — arrive muted, as though heard underwater (Qu). Neuroscience now describes the same sequence: resolve forms, then rationalization recruits itself, then empathy circuits dim. The verse is thus a case study in how murder becomes possible — not in a moment but by a *process*, the soul's gradual compliance-training. Every killer was first a rehearsed: the intention entertained, the victim dehumanized, the act facilitated. The time to arrest murder is at the *ṭawwīʿ* stage — when the soul first starts making it easy. Once facilitation completes, hands follow automatically.

**The Three Selves: Ammārah, Lawwāmah, Muṭma'innah**

*Nafsuhu* (his soul) here is the *nafs ammārah* (soul commanding evil, 12:53) — first of the Qur'an's three-self psychology, and the full ascent deserves mapping. *Ammārah*: commands evil, excuses compliance (*ṭawwīʿ* — this verse), deadens empathy — Cain's station, the unregenerate default. *Lawwāmah*: the self-reproaching soul (75:2 — God *swears by it*, dignifying conscience) — sins then stings, the believer's battleground station: *"the believer sees his sins as a mountain about to fall on him; the hypocrite sees his as a fly on his nose"* (al-Bukhārī 6308). *Muṭma'innah*: the soul at peace (89:27–28 — *"return to your Lord, content and pleasing"*) — desires aligned Godward, obedience effortless, the saints' station. The ascent's mechanics: *mujāhadah* (striving against *ammārah* — *"those who strive in Us, We guide to Our paths,"* 29:69), *muḥāsabah* (self-auditing — ʿUmar: *"audit yourselves before you are audited"*), *dhikr* (remembrance — hearts' rest, 13:28). Cain's tragedy: *ammārah* obeyed to term, *lawwāmah* bypassed (his remorse, v. 31, centered shame not God), *muṭma'innah* never glimpsed. Every soul houses all three potentially; daily choices elect which governs. Starve the commander, heed the reproacher, feed the peace — the ascent is lifelong, the summit is *riḍwān*.

**"Among the Losers": The Arithmetic of Eternity**

*Fa-aṣbaḥa min al-khāsirīn* — "and thus came to be among the losers." He gained the field and lost everything: his brother, his peace (Adam, the reports say, never laughed again (R, Z)), and his Hereafter. *Khusrān* (loss) is the Qur'an's term for the trade where one surrenders eternity for appetite — the worst exchange rate in existence. Cain's story is the template: every sin trades infinite capital for finite pleasure, and the pleasure expires while the invoice does not. The verse's terseness is its power — *he slew him* (*faqatalahu*), two syllables in Arabic for the act that invented homicide. Scripture refuses to sensationalize the killing; it will not grant violence the dignity of description. The camera, so to speak, looks away from the gore and fixes on the consequence: loss. All murder mysteries end with the discovery of the body; this one ends with the discovery of the loser.

**Khusrān: The Qur'an's Economics of Loss**

*Khāsirīn* (losers) is the Qur'an's technical term for eschatological bankruptcy — and its economics deserve full exposition. The *trade*-metaphor runs throughout: souls *buy* and *sell* — *"God has purchased from believers their selves and wealth, in exchange for Paradise"* (9:111); losers *"purchased error for guidance — their commerce profited nothing"* (2:16); the ultimate loss: *"losers are those who lose themselves and their families on Resurrection Day"* (39:15; 42:45) — self *and* kin, capital *and* dependents. Cain's transaction audited: *paid* — brother, peace, father's joy, eternity; *received* — an empty field and crow-tutored burial. Every sin re-runs his ledger: adultery trades marriage-trust for minutes; corruption trades reputation for cash; apostasy-of-convenience trades eternity for comfort. The Prophet ﷺ defined the truly bankrupt (*muflis*): one arriving with prayers and fasts, but having insulted, slandered, consumed, beaten — good deeds distributed to victims till none remain, then victims' sins loaded onto him — cast into Fire (Muslim 2581). Audit every temptation as transaction: price versus prize, Cain's way or Abel's. The losers' ledger always balances — against them.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:31

> **Then God sent a crow, scratching the earth, to show him how he might conceal his brother's nakedness. He said, "Oh, woe unto me! Am I not able to be even as this crow and conceal my brother's nakedness?" And he came to be among the remorseful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Crow as Teacher: When a Bird Outranks a Murderer**

Abel was, the commentators say, the first human to die — Cain knew killing but not burial (Ṭ, Z). So *God sent a crow, scratching* (*yabḥathu* — digging, searching) *the earth* — burying another dead crow, in most reports (Ṭ) — *to show him how to conceal his brother's nakedness* (*saw'ah*: corpse, shame, exposedness; al-Zamakhsharī stresses shame, al-Ṭabarī corpse (R, Ṭ, Z)). The scene's irony is bottomless: the creature that reasoned, sacrificed, envied, and murdered must take instruction from a bird following instinct. Humanity's first school of funeral rites had a crow for a master and a killer for its only pupil. God thereby establishes burial as a divinely taught rite — the dead are concealed with dignity, shielded from beasts and exposure — while humbling the murderer beneath the animal kingdom. Knowledge unused for good degrades below instinct: the crow, knowing nothing, did right; Cain, knowing good and evil, did wrong and then could not even manage the aftermath. Every tyrant who must be taught basic decency by his victims' simple dignity reenacts this field.

**Burial in Islam: The Dignity God Taught the Crow**

The crow's lesson became Islam's funeral civilization — washing (*ghusl*), shrouding (*takfīn*), prayer (*ṣalāt al-janāzah*), burial (*dafn*) — each legislated as communal obligation (*farḍ kifāyah*), each dignifying the dead. The Prophet ﷺ detailed the ethos: wash odd-times with lote-and-camphor (al-Bukhārī 1253), shroud in white (Abū Dāwūd 3878: *"shroud your dead in white, for it is finest"*), pray though the deceased be sinful (intercession hoped — *"no Muslim dies with forty interceding but God grants it,"* Muslim 948), bury swiftly (*"hasten the funeral"* — al-Bukhārī 1315), level graves (Muslim 969 — structures banned), visit graves for remembrance (*"visit graves, for they remind of the Hereafter"* — Muslim 976). The philosophy: the body, even soulless, retains inviolability (*ḥurmah*) — *"breaking a dead believer's bone is like breaking it alive"* (Abū Dāwūd 3207). Cremation, display, desecration — all barred; concealment (*satr*, the verse's *saw'ah*-covering) commanded. Cain learned burial from a bird; believers inherit it as liturgy — every funeral a reenactment of the crow's scratch, every grave a classroom teaching the living: *conceal your brother's shame as earth conceals his corpse*.

**"Woe Unto Me": Remorse That May or May Not Be Repentance**

*Yā waylatī* — "Oh, woe unto me!" — the Qur'an's cry of realized ruin (2:79; 18:49; 21:14, 47, 97; 25:28; 36:52; 37:20; 68:31). *Am I unable to be even as this crow?* — the first recorded instance of self-contempt, and *he came to be among the remorseful* (*nādimīn*). But remorse (*nadam*) is not yet repentance (*tawbah*): remorse looks backward at the exposed self, repentance turns forward to God. Cain grieves his shame and helplessness; the text records no turning, no plea, no *Lord, forgive me*. The distinction diagnoses countless modern sorrows — the caught corrupt official's press-conference tears, the exposed abuser's "apology" that centers his own suffering. True repentance names the wrong *against God*, makes amends *to victims*, and changes *course*; remorse merely aches at consequences. Cain buried his brother having learned burial from a bird — whether he ever learned repentance, the Book pointedly does not say. The silence is the warning.

**Nadam Versus Tawbah: The Anatomy of True Return**

The remorse-repentance distinction is Islam's spiritual diagnostics, and its criteria need codifying. *Nadam* (remorse): backward-looking pain at exposure, loss, or self-image damage — Judas-grade sorrow, Cain-grade ache. *Tawbah* (repentance): forward-turning return with four classical conditions (al-Nawawī, *Riyāḍ al-Ṣāliḥīn*): immediate *cessation*, sincere *remorse-toward-God*, firm *resolve* never to return, and *restitution* of rights (wealth returned, reputations restored, victims sought). The Prophet ﷺ gave remorse its due — *"remorse is repentance"* (Ibn Mājah 4252; Aḥmad — ḥasan) — meaning Godward remorse *constitutes* repentance's heart, not that worldly regret suffices. David's model (38:24–25: fell prostrate, sought forgiveness, was forgiven), Adam's own words (*"our Lord, we wronged ourselves..."* — 7:23) — note: Adam *did* what Cain didn't: named the wrong Godward, pleaded jointly, was restored. Cain's *yā waylatī* named only incapacity (*am I unable?*) — self-pity, not God-seeking. The diagnostic question for every sorry soul: does your sorrow face the mirror (my shame) or the Throne (His right)? Mirrors produce Cain; Thrones produce Adam. Turn the face; the Turning One faces back (2:222: *"God loves the repentant"*).

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:32

> **For this reason, We prescribed for the Children of Israel that whosoever slays a soul — unless it be for another soul or working corruption upon the earth — it is as though he slew mankind altogether, and whosoever saves the life of one, it is as though he saved the life of mankind altogether. Our messengers have certainly come unto them with clear proofs. Yet even after that, many of them are prodigal on the earth.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Soul as All Souls: The Arithmetic of the Sacred**

*For this reason* (*min ajli dhālika*) — because of Cain's fratricide — *We prescribed for the Children of Israel*: to kill one soul unjustly is *as though he slew mankind altogether*; to save one is *as though he saved mankind altogether*. The Mishnah and Talmud preserve the same teaching rooted in the same story, making this verse a documented bridge between the Qur'an and rabbinic ethics. The commentators unfold the equivalence several ways: the sanctity violated is one — God's hallowing of life — whether the victim count is one or millions (Q); the murderer of a prophet or just imām kills guidance itself (Q, Ṭ); Hell's sentence for murdering a believer is not multiplied by body count, since one such murder already earns the maximum (Q, Ṭ, via Mujāhid); and the earthly penalty is likewise one life for lives taken (Q, Ṭ). Beneath all readings runs one principle: *human life is not priced by quantity*. Utilitarianism counts lives; revelation weighs them — and each soul, bearing the divine breath, weighs infinite. A civilization that internalizes this verse cannot produce body-count morality, collateral-damage euphemisms, or "acceptable losses." One is all. That is the whole ethic.

**The Sanctity of Life: Islam's Fivefold Fortress**

The verse crowns Islam's fivefold fortress around life, each wall worth naming. *Theological*: life is God's *amānah* (trust) — *"do not kill yourselves"* (4:29), suicide barred, self-harm barred. *Penal*: intentional murder earns *qiṣāṣ* (2:178) or Hell unforgiven-unrepented (*"whoever kills a believer intentionally — his recompense is Hell, abiding"* — 4:93). *Procedural*: proof-burdens mountainous (confession, two witnesses to the act, all doubts voiding *ḥudūd* — *"avert penalties by doubts,"* al-Tirmidhī 1424). *Communal*: collective liability (*qasāmah* oaths, blood-money solidarity) ensures no corpse goes unanswered. *Eschatological*: *"the first cases judged on Resurrection Day will be blood-cases"* (al-Bukhārī 6533; Muslim 1678) — murder heads the Docket. Together the walls make Muslim civilization history's most life-guarding order in *law* (however practice faltered): no duel-culture, no honor-killing sanction, no infanticide (condemned — 6:137, 140; 16:58–59; 81:8–9), no gladiatorial blood-sport, no euthanasia-on-demand. Verse 32 is the fortress's flag: one soul, all souls. Societies dismantling any wall should expect the siege-engines of violence at every other.

**The Two Exceptions: Justice and the Defense of Order**

*Unless it be for another soul* — lawful retribution for murder (and, by extension, other capital crimes such as married adultery (R)) — *or working corruption upon the earth* (*fasād*) — idolatry-as-treason, war against the community, terrorizing roads (Q, Ṭ, Z; cf. v. 33). The exceptions prove the rule's seriousness: life may be taken only by *lawful authority* (*the state, the court*), for *defined causes*, through *due process* — never by private hands, mobs, or vigilantes. Cain is precisely what the exceptions exclude: personal killing, however provoked. And *saving* a life spans the direct (rescue from fire or drowning (Ṭ, Z)), the legal (the victim's kin forgoing retaliation for compensation or forgiveness (2:178; Ṭ, Z)), and — in the widest reading — the spiritual and economic: lifting one from poverty or guiding one from error (Qm). The verse thus commissions every reader twice: *do not destroy; do rescue*. Non-violence is the floor; life-saving is the calling. The paramedic, the peacemaker, the poverty-lifter, the suicide-preventer — each, by this verse's arithmetic, has saved not one but all.

**Saving One as Saving All: The Callings Enumerated**

*Whoever saves one life — as though he saved all*: the commission's *careers* deserve enumeration, for the verse founds professions. *Physical rescue*: medicine (the Prophet ﷺ: *"whoever relieves a believer's distress, God relieves his on Resurrection Day"* — Muslim 2699), firefighting, lifeguarding, midwifery — every resuscitation a micro-resurrection. *Legal rescue*: defending the wrongly accused, ransoming captives, forgiving *qiṣāṣ* (2:178's charity-expiation) — justice administered as life-support. *Economic rescue*: feeding the starving (5:89's expiation-meals institutionalize it), employing the destitute, micro-lending the striver — poverty kills slowly; livelihood resuscitates. *Spiritual rescue*: guiding the astray (Qm's reading) — *"whoever guides to good earns its reward"* (Muslim 1893) — rescuing from Fire outranks rescuing from fire. *Psychological rescue*: the suicide-preventer, the despair-counselor, the loneliness-befriender — hope resuscitated. The verse's *all* dignifies each equally: no hierarchy of rescue, no prestige-rankings among saviors. The ER nurse and the street-preacher, the famine-reliever and the *qiṣāṣ*-forgiver — each saved *mankind*. Choose a rescue; the verse signs its paycheck in eternity's currency.

**Why Israel? The Universality Inside the Particularity**

The prescription is addressed *to the Children of Israel* — some say because they were first threatened with eternal punishment for murder (Q); al-Rāzī ties it to Madinan plots against the Prophet ﷺ (R). But all agree the principle binds Muslims equally (IK, Q, Ṭ) — indeed all humanity, since its rationale (life's sanctity) precedes and exceeds any one community. The verse closes in sorrow: *Our messengers came to them with clear proofs, yet even after that, many are prodigal* (*musrifūn* — excessive, wasteful) *on earth*. *Isrāf* (prodigality) is expenditure beyond limit — here, the spending of blood beyond all bounds. Messengers brought the clearest proofs; still the extravagant kept spending lives. Revelation can prescribe, warn, and promise — it cannot compel. The prodigal with blood, like the prodigal with wealth, must choose thrift himself. The verse leaves him the arithmetic and walks away.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:33

> **Verily the recompense of those who wage war against God and His Messenger, and endeavor to work corruption upon the earth is that they be killed or crucified, or have their hands and feet cut off from opposite sides, or be banished from the land. That is their disgrace in this world, and in the Hereafter theirs shall be a great punishment,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥirābah: The Crime of Terrorizing the Road**

This verse ordains one of the Qur'an's five *ḥudūd* (Divinely fixed penalties), for *ḥirābah* — waging war (*muḥārabah*) against God and His Messenger, understood as armed terror against the community, since none can literally war against God (Q). The crime comprises armed robbery, assault (including rape), and murder — especially of travelers — distinguished by weapons, extreme violence, and the deliberate spreading of fear; cities are included with highways by scholarly consensus. *Working corruption* (*fasād*) here is technically narrowed to violent predation, though the Qur'an elsewhere widens *fasād* to spiritual ruin (2:11–12, 27; 7:56; 10:40, 81; 16:88) — the angels' primordial forecast that vicegerents would "work corruption and shed blood" (2:30). Cain's story (vv. 27–31) is the archetype; *ḥirābah* is its professionalization. The law's target is precise: not dissent, not sin, not even ordinary theft — but *armed terror that makes roads impassable and societies unlivable*. Every age names it differently — brigandage, banditry, terrorism — but the Qur'anic category captures what all share: violence as a livelihood and fear as a product.

**Terrorism's Ancient Name: Ḥirābah in Modern Dress**

*Muḥāribūn* (war-wagers) are scripture's term for what modernity calls terrorists — and the fit deserves demonstrating point-by-point. *Targets*: innocents (travelers then, civilians now — non-combatants by definition). *Means*: weapons and fear-display (swords brandished on highways; explosives broadcast on screens — the medium evolves, the theater persists). *Aims*: *fasād* (corruption — destabilizing order, extorting societies, advertising power). *Livelihood*: violence as economy (plunder then; ransoms, trafficking, and patronage now). Classical jurists' expansions already cover modern variants: urban terror (included by consensus),-banditry and piracy (sea-*ḥirābah*), and — by direct analogy (*qiyās*) — kidnapping rings, narco-armies, and ideological bombers. The verse's four penalties scale to gravity (v. 33-comm.): killing-plus-plunder → execution-plus-exposure; plunder-only → cross-amputation; terror-only → banishment/imprisonment. Note what the category *excludes*: legitimate resistance under lawful authority (jihād's strict ethics — no terrorizing non-combatants ever), political dissent (speech, however hostile), and ordinary crime (different penalties). The verse thus arms Muslim states against terror *theologically*: terrorists are not *mujāhidūn* (strivers) but *muḥāribūn* (war-wagers against God) — the Law's harshest human category, its perpetrators promised disgrace-now and torment-then.

**The ʿUraynah Incident: The Occasion That Fixed the Law**

The widest-attested occasion: men of ʿUraynah and ʿUkl feigned conversion, complained to the Prophet ﷺ that Madinah's climate sickened them as Bedouin, were lent milch-camels with a herdsman — then outside the city maimed and murdered the herdsman and stole the camels. Apprehended, they were punished by amputation, blinding, and exposure till death (Q, Ṭ, W). The verse then fixed the four penalties: execution, crucifixion (public exposure), cross-amputation, banishment (cf. Pharaoh's threats, 20:71). Commentators divide on the relation: clarification or endorsement of the Prophet's ﷺ act (Ṭ); partial correction, since blinding and death-exposure are absent here — some reading an implicit ban on torture (Q, Ṭ), noting the Prophet ﷺ never tortured before or after (Q); or the blinding as *qiṣāṣ* (equal retribution) for the herdsman's maiming specifically (Q, Ṭ). A small minority extended the verse to apostates generally since the criminals had also apostatized (Ṭ) — but the mainstream confines it to *violent terror*, not belief-change. The law punishes what hands do to travelers, not what hearts do with convictions.

**Graduated Penalties and the Ban on Torture**

Al-Shāfiʿī and others correlated penalty to crime: murder-without-theft → execution; theft-without-murder → cross-amputation; both → execution plus crucifixion-exposure; terror-without-either → banishment; a minority left the choice to the ruler's discretion (JJ, Q, Ṭ, Ṭs). Juristic humanity hedged the penalties round: crucifixion historically meant post-execution public exposure as deterrence, not execution by cross; those permitting pre-execution exposure required near-immediate killing lest suffering become torture, which is forbidden (Q, Ṭs); banishment was read as pursuit-into-exile, territorial transfer, or — most humanely — imprisonment until repentance, first implemented by ʿUmar (Q). *That is their disgrace in this world, and in the Hereafter great punishment* (cf. 5:41; 2:114; 9:74; 13:33–34). Two reflections close the matter. First, severity here protects the vulnerable: the severed hand of one highwayman keeps a thousand travelers' hands on their goods. Second, even this severest law keeps mercy's door open — the very next verse says so.

**The Torture Ban: Islam's Absolute Red Line**

The verse's torture-question opens Islam's absolute red line, and its texts need mustering. The Prophet ﷺ declared:

> *"God punishes those who punish people in this world."* (Muslim 2613 — Hishām ibn Ḥakīm to Syrian torturers, freeing their victims)

And: *"The most severely punished on Resurrection Day will be those who inflict the severest punishment on people in this world"* (Aḥmad 4:90). ʿUmar's governors operated under written orders: no flogging suspects for confession, no *shubhah*-punishments, no mutilation. Jurists voided torture-extracted confessions unanimously; Mālik permitted only the *sultan's* discipline of known criminals under strict limits — and later scholars (Ibn Taymiyyah, al-Ghazālī's school) fenced even that. The ʿUraynah blindings, read as *qiṣāṣ*-for-maiming (Q, Ṭ) rather than interrogation-torture, fit the line: *equal* retribution for *proven* maiming differs categorically from *coercive* pain for *suspected* crime. Modern Muslim states practicing torture violate not Western imports but *this verse's* tradition — the Prophet ﷺ who never tortured before or after one ambiguous incident (Q), the caliphs who punished torturers, the jurists who voided torture's fruits. *Ḥirābah*'s severity targets *convicted* terrorizers through *courts*; torture targets *suspects* through *pain*. The Law's sword is sharp; its handle is justice — wield one without the other and the cut is *ẓulm*.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:34

> **save those who repent before you overpower them. And know that God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Repentance Before Capture: The Door That Stays Open**

*Save those who repent before you overpower them* — even *ḥirābah*, the Qur'an's gravest public crime, yields to timely repentance. The parallel is deathbed theology: repentance avails before capture as it avails before death, but not after the sentence becomes certain (4:17–18). Once the criminal is caught, convicted, and brought before authority, the *ḥadd* becomes mandatory in most schools; but if victims forgive and the matter never reaches the authorities, punishment may be averted (Q, Ṭ). Note the precision: repentance lifts the *Divine* penalty (the ḥadd) and the Hereafter's punishment, but victims' rights persist — stolen property must be restored, and *qiṣāṣ* (retribution) or compensation remains claimable (cf. 2:178). God forgives His rights freely; human rights require human forgiveness or restitution. The architecture is exquisite: mercy for the criminal, justice for the victim, deterrence for society — all three served without sacrificing any. And the higher path is named by implication: victims who forgive outright participate in the very Names sealing the verse — *Forgiving, Merciful* — turning their injury into an imitation of God.

**Ḥuqūq Allāh and Ḥuqūq al-ʿIbād: The Two Ledgers**

The verse operates Islam's two-ledger jurisprudence — *ḥuqūq Allāh* (God's rights: public order, fixed penalties, worship) and *ḥuqūq al-ʿibād* (servants' rights: property, bodies, reputations) — and the distinction's mechanics deserve full statement. *God's rights*: He forgives freely upon repentance (this verse — the *ḥadd* lifted), mercifully, even eagerly (2:222). *Servants' rights*: God does *not* forgive without the servant's forgiveness or restitution — *"whoever wronged his brother in honor or wealth, let him settle today before currency becomes deeds"* (al-Bukhārī 2449). The *muflis* (bankrupt) ḥadīth (Muslim 2581) shows the settlement-court: prayers and fasts transferred to victims as damages, victims' sins loaded when deeds exhaust. The wisdom: public-order penalties deter generally (God's ledger, mercifully waivable); private injuries wound specifically (victims' ledger, justly claimable). Terrorists repenting pre-capture thus walk free of *ḥudūd* yet indebted to victims — plunder restored, blood-money owed, forgiveness begged. The two ledgers together prevent both laxity (Divine rights unenforced) and cruelty (human rights ignored). Repentance settles heaven's account; restitution settles earth's. Settle both; the Names *Forgiving, Merciful* seal the first, victims' pardon the second.

**The Amnesty Precedent: Pre-Capture Repentance in History**

The verse's pre-capture amnesty has governed Muslim statecraft's noblest surrenders. The Prophet's ﷺ conquest-amnesty (Ibn Hishām) operationalized it at scale: Makkan war-criminals laying down arms pre-capture walked free — *"go, you are free"* — while the few named war-criminals (nearly all subsequently pardoned individually) faced justice. ʿUmar's governors offered highwaymen repentance-terms: surrender arms, restore plunder, walk free of *ḥirābah*-penalties. Later dynasties' *amān* (safe-conduct) proclamations to rebels echo the verse's structure: repentance-before-overpowering = amnesty; defiance-after = penalty. Counter-terrorism's modern dilemma — deradicalization versus prosecution — finds its classical template here: *genuine* pre-capture abandonment (arms surrendered, networks exposed, victims compensated) earns the verse's shelter; capture-then-claimed repentance earns courts. The distinction incentivizes exactly right: surrender early (mercy maximal), persist late (justice certain). The verse even prices procrastination theologically: repentance's door narrows as capture nears, closing at overpowering — like death's door (4:18), like the sun-rising-west (6:158). The wise fugitive from terror's path runs through it early; the Names *Forgiving, Merciful* wait inside.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:35

> **O you who believe! Reverence God, and seek the means to approach Him, and strive in His way, that haply you may prosper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Al-Wasīlah: The Means of Approach to God**

*Seek the means* (*wasīlah*) *to approach Him* — *wasīlah* is whatever draws one near: a station, a deed, a door. Nearness (*qurb*) to God is the Qur'an's loftiest goal and reward (17:57; 38:25, 40), earned by obedience and virtue (9:99; 34:37), possessed in highest rank by *those brought nigh* (*muqarrabūn*; 56:11, 88; 83:21, 28). Coming right after the repentance verse, *wasīlah* may hint at the criminal's repentance or the victim's forgiveness as approaches to God. The celebrated ḥadīth qudsī defines the hierarchy:

> *"My servant draws not nigh unto Me with anything I love more than what I have made obligatory upon him. And My servant continues drawing nigh unto Me with supererogatory works until I love him..."* (al-Bukhārī 6502)

Obligations first, supererogation second — the order corrects both the minimalist (obligations only, no love-offerings) and the enthusiast (voluntary excess atop neglected duties). Sufi commentators add the interior means — patience, contentment, sincerity (Su, Qu) — and Ibn ʿAjībah the guide (*shaykh*) who helps purge the soul's impurities. False *wasīlahs* — intercessors of invention (39:3; 46:28) — are rebuked. The verse thus maps the entire spiritual path in three words: *reverence* (the guard), *approach* (the journey), *strive* (the effort).

**Tawassul: Lawful Means and Their Counterfeits**

*Wasīlah*-seeking (*tawassul*) is among Islam's most contested practices — and the verse's own contours settle the lawful core all accept. *Unanimous*: approaching God *by His Names* (*"to God belong the most beautiful Names, so call Him by them"* — 7:180), *by one's good deeds* (the cave-three ḥadīth — al-Bukhārī 2272; Muslim 2743: each invoked a righteous deed; the rock shifted), *by the Prophet's ﷺ supplication* (seeking his *duʿā'* in life — the blind man's ḥadīth, al-Tirmidhī 3578), and *by righteous persons' supplications* (ʿUmar seeking al-ʿAbbās's rain-*duʿā'* — al-Bukhārī 1010). *Contested*: invoking God *by the Prophet's ﷺ rank* after his passing, or by saints' essences — Sunnī-Salafī scholars generally restricting to the unanimous modes (Ibn Taymiyyah's *Qāʿidah Jalīlah*), others permitting rank-based forms with classical precedents debated. *Unanimously barred*: supplicating the dead *as* hearers-answerers (the Qur'an's *shirk*-polemic — 10:18; 39:3; 46:5), treating graves as intercession-bureaus. The verse's placement teaches the safest *wasīlah*: the *criminal's repentance* and *victim's forgiveness* (v. 34's context) — approach God through *your own* rectitude, not others' ranks. The nearest *wasīlah* is the prostrating forehead (96:19: *"prostrate and draw near"*) — no intermediary required, none permitted to obstruct.

**Striving in His Way: Jihād in Its Native Habitat**

*Strive in His way* (*jāhidū fī sabīlihi*) — the Qur'an's constant injunction (2:218; 4:95; 8:72, 74; 9:20; 49:15). *Jihād* (striving, exertion) here sits between reverence and prosperity, framed by approach-to-God — its native habitat is devotion, not aggression. Al-Qushayrī reads it purely spiritually: perfect obedience, detachment from worldliness, witnessing God at every moment (Qu). The mainstream holds both the martial and spiritual senses in their proper places: armed struggle under lawful authority against aggression, and the greater struggle against the soul's own *ḥirābah* — its inner highwaymen of lust, rage, and heedlessness. *That haply you may prosper* (*tufliḥūn*) — *falāḥ* is the farmer's success (*falḥ*: to split earth for seed): prosperity as harvest, not windfall. Reverence plants, approach waters, striving tills — and God gives the increase.

**Jihād's Levels: The Striving Scaled**

*Jihād* (exertion) in the tradition scales across four theaters — and the verse's context (post-*ḥirābah*, pre-eschatology) invokes all. *Against the soul* (*jihād al-nafs*): the Prophet's ﷺ *"best jihād is one who strives against his own soul"* (al-Tirmidhī 1621, ḥasan) — the inner *muḥāribūn* (vv. 33–34's highwaymen within: lust ambushing chastity, rage robbing peace). *Against Satan*: resisting whisperings (*waswasah* — 114:4–5) through remembrance and refuge. *Against disbelief-and-hypocrisy*: *"strive against disbelievers and hypocrites"* (9:73) — by argument (*"strive against them with it [the Qur'an], a great striving"* — 25:52), the *tongue-and-pen* jihād. *Martial*: under lawful authority, defensive-or-just cause, strict ethics (no non-combatants, no mutilation, no treachery — 2:190; Abū Dāwūd 2614). Ibn al-Qayyim's thirteen sub-levels (*Zād al-Maʿād*) detail the finding: most *jihād* is *non*-martial — learning, teaching, earning-lawfully, raising-righteously. The verse orders the *genus* (strive), leaving species to circumstance: the student's all-nighter over sacred texts is *jihād*; the breadwinner's lawful toil is *jihād*; the soldier's lawful defense is *jihād*. *Fī sabīlihi* (in *His* way) restricts all: striving *for* God, *by* God's rules, *toward* God's proximity. Striving otherwise directed — for tribe, ego, or spoils — is labor, not *jihād*, however strenuous.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:36

> **Truly those who disbelieve, were they to possess all that is on the earth and the like of it besides with which to ransom themselves from the punishment of the Day of Resurrection, it would not be accepted from them. And theirs shall be a painful punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ransom That Cannot Be Paid: Wealth's Final Bankruptcy**

*Were they to possess all that is on earth and the like besides* — double the world's wealth — *as ransom* (*fidyah*) *from the Day's punishment, it would not be accepted*. The Qur'an hammers this futility across a dozen passages (2:48, 123; 3:91; 10:54; 13:18; 39:47; 57:15; 70:11). Why the repetition? Because wealth's power in *this* world is so total — buying lawyers, legislators, and luxuries — that the soul naturally assumes it spends in the next. The verse declares the ultimate currency reform: on that Day, earthly riches are demonetized absolutely. The Prophet ﷺ illustrated with God's question to Hell's lightest sufferer:

> *"If you possessed all on earth, would you ransom yourself with it?" He will say, "Yes." God will say, "I asked you for less than that when you were in Adam's loins — that you associate nothing with Me — but you refused."* (al-Bukhārī 3340; Muslim 2805, paraphrased)

The economics of salvation invert worldly economics: here the poor struggle and the rich glide; there, only *taqwā* spends, and it was free to all. Every act of worship is thus a foreign-exchange transaction — converting perishable hours into imperishable currency before the markets close.

**Fidyah's Boundaries: What Ransom Covers and What It Cannot**

*Fidyah* (ransom, redemption) *does* operate in this world — and mapping its valid zones sharpens the verse's negation-zone. *Valid*: fasting-exemptions ransomed by feeding (2:184), ḥajj-shortcomings expiated (2:196), oath-breaches covered (5:89), captives ransomed (*"thereafter grace or ransom,"* 47:4), *qiṣāṣ* settled by blood-money (2:178). *Invalid*: the Day's punishment (this verse and its dozen parallels), intercession-for-deniers (*"no intercession will benefit them,"* 74:48), and post-mortem wealth-benefit (*"neither wealth nor children will avail,"* 26:88 — save the sound heart's). The pattern: ransom works *within* mercy's appointed windows (this life's expiations, war's exchanges, courts' settlements) and fails *beyond* them (the sealed Audit). The Prophet ﷺ applied it to his own kin preemptively: *"O Ṣafiyyah, aunt of God's Messenger! O Fāṭimah, daughter of Muḥammad! ... I avail you nothing against God"* (Muslim 206) — lineage non-transferable, ransom non-spendable. The financial-planning moral: invest in transferable assets (ongoing charity — Muslim 1631, beneficial knowledge, righteous offspring praying) *before* the markets close. Death is the bell; the verse describes the after-hours market — all earthly holdings frozen, only *taqwā*-denominated assets trading.

**Wealth's Trial Now: Fitnah Before Bankruptcy**

If wealth bankrupts *then*, it *trials* now — and the Qur'an's wealth-theology needs stating as the verse's mirror. Wealth is *fitnah* (trial — 8:28; 64:15), *zīnah* (adornment — 18:46), *amānah* (trust — 57:7: *"spend from what He made you trustees of"*), never *ownership* absolute. Its perils catalogued: hoarding (*"those who hoard gold and silver and spend it not in God's way — give them tidings of painful torment,"* 9:34), arrogance (Qārūn — 28:76–82), distraction (*"wealth and children diverted you,"* 102:1), and ransom-delusion (this verse). Its virtues channeled: spending (*infāq* — 2:261's seven-hundredfold), purifying (*zakāh* — 9:103), lending goodly (v. 12), bequeathing (vv. 106–108). The Prophet ﷺ modeled the balance: wealthy Khadījah's household funding the mission, then voluntary poverty (*"what is the world to me? A rider shading under a tree, then departing"* — al-Tirmidhī 2377); yet praying *"O God, I seek refuge from disbelief and poverty"* conjoined (Abū Dāwūd 5090) — poverty no virtue in itself. The investment rule condenses: *hold wealth in hand, never in heart; spend it forward, never hoard it still*. Hands holding lightly release easily into expiations, charities, and ransoms-that-work — converting bankruptcy-bound holdings into Audit-passing assets. The verse bankrupts hoarders hereafter; wisdom liquidates holdings heavenward now.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:37

> **They will wish to come forth from the Fire, but they shall not come forth from it. And theirs shall be a lasting punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wish That Defines Damnation**

*They will wish* (*yurīdūn*) *to come forth from the Fire, but they shall not come forth* — the damned retain desire but lose efficacy. Will without power is the precise formula of Hell: on earth, wishing can become striving and striving can become escape; there, the machinery connecting wish to outcome is disconnected. *Theirs shall be a lasting* (*muqīm* — established, abiding) *punishment*. The verse's terror lies in its stillness — no pealing thunder, just a door that does not open. Theologians across schools affirmed the Fire's eternity for persistent disbelievers while debating its edges; the Qur'an's own emphasis is pastoral rather than speculative: *you can still come forth now*. Every sermon, every Ramadan, every moment of repentance is a door still open. The verse shows the door closed only so that readers will walk through it while it stands wide. Regret in this life is a vehicle; regret in the next is a venue. The wise transport themselves with the first before they are housed in the second.

**The Fire's Petitions: A Tour of Refused Exits**

The damned's exit-petitions recur across the Qur'an — and touring them assembles this verse's full horror. *To the keepers*: *"Call upon your Lord to lighten for us one day of torment"* — answered: *"Did your messengers not bring clear proofs? ... The prayer of disbelievers is only in error"* (40:49–50). *To Mālik, Hell's keeper*: *"Let your Lord finish us!"* — answered: *"You shall abide"* (43:77). *To God*: *"Our Lord, remove us; we will do righteousness!"* — answered: *"Did We not grant you a life long enough for remembrance? ... Taste, then — wrongdoers have no helper"* (35:37). *To Paradise's people*: *"Pour upon us some water!"* — answered: *"God has forbidden both to disbelievers"* (7:50). Five petitions, five refusals — the Qur'an's most pitiless sequence, each refusal citing *earth's* squandered chances. The pastoral function is terror-with-address: every refusal references an *open* door (messengers came, life sufficed, warnings reached). Readers still *in* life's sufficiency, still *hearing* messengers' proofs, can answer every petition preemptively — lighten torment now through repentance, exit Fire's path now through righteousness. The petitions will be refused *then*; the preparations are accepted *now*. Prepare.

**Lasting Punishment: Eternity's Justice**

*ʿAdhāb muqīm* — "lasting, established punishment": *muqīm* (from *iqāmah*, establishment — the word for erecting prayer) pictures torment *erected*, settled, domiciled — not passing through but residing. The Qur'an's eternity-texts for persistent disbelief are explicit (*"abiding therein forever"* — 4:169; 33:64–65; 72:23; *"neither finished for death nor lightened"* — 35:36), and the schools affirm them while differing on sinning believers (exiting by mercy/intercession — al-Bukhārī 7439; Muslim 183 — the *Jahannamiyyūn*). Theodicy's question — infinite punishment for finite sin? — receives the tradition's layered answers: persistence *would* sin eternally if lived eternally (will's infinity, not act's); the Offended is infinite (offense scales with Object); and — most pastorally — eternity was *warned* with perfect clarity, making persistence a *chosen* infinite. Ibn Taymiyyah's school adds mercy's asymmetry: Paradise's eternity is *pure grace* (no deed earns infinity), Fire's is *pure justice* (warnings exhausted). The verse's own logic suffices most: punishment *lasting* because denial *lasted* — settled aversion earning settled sentence. Exit routes stand open *now* (repentance, belief, righteousness); their persistence-open status is mercy's scandal. Walk out while walking is possible.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:38

> **As for the male thief and the female thief, cut off their hands as a recompense for what they have earned, as an exemplary punishment from God. Truly God is Mighty, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ḥadd for Theft: Severity in Its Legal Habitat**

*As for the male thief and female thief, cut off their hands* — another of the Qur'an's five *ḥudūd* (cf. v. 33), applied equally to both sexes (as with adultery, 24:2–3). The Prophet ﷺ stressed its universality:

> *"By God, if Fāṭimah the daughter of Muḥammad were to steal, I would cut off her hand."* (al-Bukhārī 3475; Muslim 1688)

Al-Qurṭubī notes amputation was also pre-Islamic Arabia's penalty — revelation regulated rather than invented it. But the *ḥadd* operates inside a dense thicket of conditions developed from ḥadīth and early caliphal precedent: a minimum threshold (*niṣāb*) — commonly a quarter-dīnār (a gram of gold) or three dirhams, or a shield's worth (Q, Ṭ); theft by stealth from secure custody (*ḥirz*), excluding embezzlement, snatching, and familial taking; absence of doubt (*shubhah*) — hunger (ʿUmar suspended the penalty in famine), need, disputed ownership, or necessity all void it; and full due process with testimony or confession. Below the threshold: restitution and discretionary discipline; al-Shāfiʿī held restitution mandatory even with amputation (Q). The procedure must preserve life — bleeding stanched, death prevented. What emerges is not a chopping regime but a rarely-applied maximum penalty whose *existence* deters while its *conditions* mercifully restrict. Classical crime statistics are unavailable, but the design logic is clear: make the penalty severe enough to protect property, and the proof strict enough to protect suspects.

**The Makhzūmī Woman: Equality Before the Ḥadd**

The Fāṭimah-ḥadīth's occasion deserves full narration — it is this verse's *sīrah* seal. A noblewoman of Banū Makhzūm (Quraysh's aristocracy) stole; her clan, dreading tribal shame, sent Usāmah ibn Zayd — the Prophet's ﷺ beloved — to intercede. His face changed color; he rebuked: *"Do you intercede regarding one of God's fixed penalties?"* Then, publicly: *"Those before you were destroyed because they spared the noble thief and punished the weak one. By God, if Fāṭimah daughter of Muḥammad stole, I would cut her hand"* (al-Bukhārī 3475; Muslim 1688) — and ordered the penalty. *Sāriq wa sāriqah* (male *and* female thief) already encodes sex-equality; the incident encodes *rank*-equality: nobility neither shields nor dooms. ʿUmar's caliphate extended it: governors' sons whipped equally, his own son punished publicly. The verse's *jazā'an bi-mā kasabā* (recompense for *what they earned*) individualizes desert: hands earn, hands answer — no clan pays, no clan shields. Modern applications write themselves: corruption-prosecutions sparing elites while jailing petty thieves invert the verse exactly; the Makhzūmī precedent indicts every two-tier justice. The Law's blade cuts one way — downward onto proven guilt, never sideways onto convenient scapegoats nor upward-spared onto protected names.

**"Exemplary Punishment": Deterrence as Public Mercy**

*Nakālan mina'Llāh* — "an exemplary punishment from God": *nakāl* is deterrent chastisement, punishment-as-warning-to-others. The *ḥudūd* are God's rights against crimes that destabilize society itself — each serves as *recompense* (*jazā'*) for the criminal and *deterrence* for the public. The modern reader must weigh the system's own moral claim: a penalty that keeps theft near zero across a society protects millions of poor owners for whom a stolen goat or tool is catastrophe. Whether one accepts amputation or not, the verse forces the question every penal theory must answer — *deterrence for whom?* The Qur'anic answer prioritizes the vulnerable many over the convicted few. *Truly God is Mighty* (*ʿAzīz* — irresistible in executing judgment), *Wise* (*Ḥakīm* — placing each penalty exactly where wisdom requires). Might without wisdom would be tyranny; wisdom without might, wishfulness. The pairing seals every ḥadd verse: severity exactly measured, never vengeful, never lax.

**Property's Sanctity: Why Theft Ranks Ḥadd-Grade**

Theft's *ḥadd*-grade severity puzzles modern sensibilities — until property's Qur'anic sanctity is grasped. Wealth is *amānah* (trust — 57:7), *qiyām* (society's standing — 4:5: *"do not give the foolish your wealth which God made a support for you"*), and *ḥaqq* (right — *"do not consume wealth among you falsely,"* 2:188; 4:29). Theft violates all three: trust betrayed, support stolen, right seized — and *stealth* (*sariqah* specifically: secret taking from custody) adds violation's cowardice: the thief bypasses confrontation, striking the absent and vulnerable. The Prophet ﷺ ranked property with blood and honor: *"Your blood, wealth, and honor are sacred as this day, in this month, in this land"* (Farewell Sermon — al-Bukhārī 67; Muslim 1679). Societies confirm the ranking empirically: theft-epidemics collapse commerce, empty streets, and immiserate the poor first (the rich hire guards; the poor lose all). The *ḥadd* thus protects the *poor's* goat more than the rich's vault — severity as the weak's bodyguard. *ʿAzīzun Ḥakīm* (Mighty, Wise) seals it: Might executing what Wisdom measured. Question the measure if you must — but propose instead a system guarding the poor's property *better*. The verse awaits the alternative.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:39

> **But whosoever repents after his wrongdoing, and makes amends, God will relent unto him. Truly God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Repentance Plus Repair: The Two Wings of Return**

*Whosoever repents after his ẓulm* (wrongdoing) *and makes amends* (*aṣlaḥa* — sets right, repairs) — repentance here has two wings: the inward turn (*tāba*) and the outward repair (*aṣlaḥa*; al-Qurṭubī: abandoning all disobedience). The thief must return the goods or compensate; the verse refuses cheap grace. *God will relent unto him* (*yatūbu'Llāhu ʿalayh*) — literally "God will turn toward him": repentance is a turning met by a Turning, the human rotation answered by Divine approach. Commentators debated whether repentance before capture lifts the amputation: some said yes, others that God's "relenting" concerns the Hereafter while the ḥadd proceeds (Q, Ṭ) — with aḥādīth assuring punished-and-repentant thieves of forgiveness and purification (IK). Abū Ḥanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī held the penalty avertable only by the victim's forgiveness before the case reaches authority (JJ, Z). The architecture mirrors verse 34: God's rights yield to repentance; human rights require restitution. *Forgiving, Merciful* closes the severest penal passage in the sūrah — the Qur'an will not let the reader leave the courtroom without passing the mercy-seat. Justice amputates the hand; mercy reattaches the soul.

**Iṣlāḥ: Repair as Repentance's Proof**

*Aṣlaḥa* (he repaired, set right) — repentance's second wing — deserves its jurisprudence, for inward turning without outward mending is half-flight. The Qur'an pairs *tāba wa aṣlaḥa* repeatedly (2:160; 3:89; 4:146; 6:54; 16:119; 24:5) — the formula's consistency proves repair's necessity. For theft specifically: goods returned if extant, value compensated if consumed, victims sought if unknown (charity given in their name per jurists when owners untraceable). For sins generally: prayers made up (Ḥanafī-Shāfiʿī majority), fasts repaid, backbiting countered (praising the maligned where slandered, seeking pardon where reachable), public sins publicly renounced. The Prophet ﷺ modeled repair-theology in the Ghamidiyyah's case: her self-surrender, penalty, and funeral-prayer — *"she repented a repentance that, divided among seventy Madinans, would suffice them"* (Muslim 1696) — repair *through* penalty, purity *through* punishment. *Iṣlāḥ* also scales socially: corrupt officials restituting public funds, polluters remediating land, slanderers publishing retractions. The verse's order is therapeutic: *tāba* (stop, turn) *then aṣlaḥa* (mend, rebuild) — cessation first, construction after. Repentance without repair is apology without amendment; repair without repentance is PR. Fly both wings; the *Ghafūr-Raḥīm* receives the landing.

**Purification Through Penalty: The Ḥadd as Kaffārah**

The aḥādīth assuring punished thieves of purification open the *ḥadd-as-expiation* doctrine — and its texts need exhibiting. ʿUbādah ibn al-Ṣāmit reports the pledge (*bayʿah*): *"Whoever commits something of these then is punished in this world, it is expiation for him; whoever's sin God veils, his affair rests with God — pardoning or punishing"* (al-Bukhārī 18; Muslim 1709). The Māʿiz case (stoning for confessed adultery): the Prophet ﷺ said *"he repented a repentance that, divided among the ummah, would suffice them"* (Muslim 1695); the Ghamidiyyah likewise (Muslim 1696). The theology: *ḥudūd* operate doubly — deterrence socially, expiation individually. The punished-and-repentant meet God *cleansed*, penalty transformed into purification — suffering transmuted to sin-removal. This reframes the entire penal philosophy: Islamic punishment *saves* the criminal eschatologically while protecting society temporally — rehabilitation-through-atonement, not mere warehousing. Contrast purely retributive or purely deterrent systems: neither promises the convict *anything*. The verse promises *yatūbu'Llāhu ʿalayh* (God turns toward him) — the punished welcomed, the repenting embraced. *Ghafūr, Raḥīm* — the courtroom's exit-sign reads mercy, and the penal code's final chapter is reception, not rejection.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:40

> **Dost thou not know that unto God belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth? He punishes whomsoever He will, and He forgives whomsoever He will. And God is Powerful over all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sovereignty as the Last Word on Punishment**

*A-lam taʿlam* — "Dost thou not know?" — the question is rhetorical, addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and through him to every reader: *unto God belongs sovereignty* (*mulk* — dominion and ownership) *over heavens and earth*. Al-Rāzī reads here the Ashʿarī thesis in miniature: God's right to punish some and forgive others rests on Creator-ownership — the potter's right over clay — even where human minds cannot trace the wisdom (R). The verse closes the sūrah's penal section (vv. 33–39) by lifting eyes from earthly courts to the Ultimate Court: human judges execute fixed penalties; God alone dispenses final deserts. Two errors die here. Presumption dies: no one can claim forgiveness as wages — it is *whomsoever He will*. Despair dies: no one is beyond forgiveness as though sin created entitlement to punishment — it is equally *whomsoever He will*. Between the two wills stretches the entire life of faith: strive as though all depended on striving, hope as though all depended on mercy. *Powerful over all things* (*ʿalā kulli shay'in qadīr*) — including the pardon of the worst thief and the punishment of the most polished hypocrite. The gavel is His; the courtroom below is a shadow of the Court above.

**Mulk: Ownership as the Ground of Judgment**

*Mulk* (sovereignty-ownership) — the verse's load-bearing term — fuses two concepts English splits: *dominion* (right to rule) and *ownership* (right to dispose). The Qur'an's ownership-texts ground judgment metaphysically: *"to God belongs all in heavens and earth"* (2:255; 4:131–132), *"He cannot be questioned about what He does"* (21:23), the potter-clay logic Paul and the Qur'an share in form (cf. Romans 9; Qur'an's *"He created you, then sustained you, then will cause death, then give life"* — 30:40). The Ashʿarī thesis (R) follows: questioning God's distributions presumes a *law above* God — but no law binds the Lawgiver; justice *is* His acting, not His conforming. The Muʿtazilī counter (God *must* do the optimal, *aṣlaḥ*) the verse answers by grammar: *whomsoever He will* — will, not compulsion, governs; wisdom *adorns* will (He wills wisely — *Ḥakīm*), never chains it. Ibn Taymiyyah's middle path harmonizes: God's will *is* wise (never arbitrary), yet wisdom is *His* wisdom (never man's constraining). Practically: the verse kills complaint-litigations against providence (*why him forgiven, me tried?*) — ownership answers all. The clay questions not the Potter; the servant trusts the *Mālik* (Owner-King). Sovereignty pondered produces serenity; serenity produces worship; worship produces — by the Owner's will — forgiveness.

**The Two Wills: Hope and Fear Calibrated**

*Yuʿadhdhibu man yashā' wa yaghfiru li-man yashā'* — "He punishes whom He will, forgives whom He will": the twin wills calibrate the twin wings (fear, hope) with theological precision. *Punishment-willed*: against persisting defiance (warnings exhausted, signs denied — vv. 10, 37), never capriciously — *"your Lord wrongs no one"* (18:49). *Forgiveness-willed*: toward repentance (39:53: *"God forgives all sins"* — the hope-verse Ibn ʿAbbās called the Qur'an's most hopeful), toward monotheists (4:48, 116: all-but-*shirk* forgivable), toward whomever — the *man yashā'* leaving mercy's door uncloseable by any theology. The Prophet ﷺ embodied the calibration dying: visiting a youth at death's door, asking his state — *"I hope in God, yet fear my sins"* — he replied: *"These two never combine in a servant at such a moment but God grants his hope and secures his fear"* (al-Tirmidhī 983; Ibn Mājah 4261; ḥasan). The verse's order (punishment *then* forgiveness) suits its penal context — courts closing, mercy opening. The believer's daily calibration: fear enough to strive (punishment *willed* against defiance), hope enough to breathe (forgiveness *willed* toward return). Between the wills, the whole life of faith — striving as though all depended on striving, hoping as though all depended on mercy.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:41

> **O Messenger! Let them not grieve thee, those who hasten unto disbelief, those who say, "We believe" with their mouths, while their hearts believe not, and those who are Jews, who listen to lies and to others who have not come to thee. They distort the meaning of the word, saying, "If you are given this, then take it, but if you are not given this, then beware!" For whomsoever God desires that he be tried, thou hast no power to avail him aught against God. They are those whose hearts God desired not to purify. Theirs is disgrace in this world, and in the Hereafter they shall have a great punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**
