**"Let Them Not Grieve Thee": Consolation for the Rejected Warner**

*Lā yaḥzunka* — "Let them not grieve thee." The Prophet ﷺ grieved over rejecters the way a physician grieves over patients who refuse cure — and God repeatedly consoles him (v. 68; 3:176; 6:33; 10:65; 16:127; 31:23; 36:76). Here the grief's objects are two: hypocrites — *those who say "we believe" with mouths while hearts believe not*, *hastening* (*yusāriʿūn* — racing, competing) *unto disbelief* — and hostile Madinan Jews — *listeners to lies*, whether believing rabbis' misrepresentations or listening to the Prophet ﷺ only to deny him (R). Some construe the groups separately: "while their hearts believe not. Those who are Jews listen to lies" (R, Z). The consolation's logic unfolds at the verse's center: guidance is God's act, not the warner's achievement. The messenger delivers; God disposes. Every teacher, parent, and preacher must internalize this division of labor or break: responsibility for *delivery* is total, responsibility for *reception* is zero. Grief beyond that border is a trespass on Divine prerogative — love the student, perfect the lesson, and release the outcome.

**The Prophet's Grief: Portraits of a Bleeding Heart**

The consolation's *need* reveals the Prophet's ﷺ heart — and the portraits deserve gathering. He grieved over Abū Ṭālib's deathbed refusal, pleading *"uncle, say the word"* till revelation intervened (28:56; al-Bukhārī 1360). He grieved over Ṭā'if's stoning-blood, yet refused the mountains' crushing-offer: *"perhaps God will bring from their loins those worshipping Him"* (al-Bukhārī 3231). He grieved over Uḥud's seventy martyrs, Ḥamzah mutilated, yet forbade mutilation-return. Revelation repeatedly *restrained* his grief's excess: *"perhaps you will kill yourself with grief, following after them"* (18:6), *"grieve not over them, nor be distressed by their scheming"* (16:127), *"it is not upon you to guide them"* (2:272). The psychology: perfect compassion *feels* rejecters' self-destruction as personal wound — the physician weeping over refusing patients. God's consolation never rebukes the *compassion* (it is prophetic perfection) but bounds its *burden* (reception is Divine portfolio). Modern caregivers — imams watching youth drift, parents watching children stray, teachers watching students fail — inherit both the grief and its bounds: bleed compassionately, labor excellently, release trustingly. *Lā yaḥzunka* — the grief honored, the burden lifted.

**The Adulterers of Khaybar: A Case Study in Scriptural Fraud**

The widest-reported occasion: two Jewish adulterers from Khaybar (or Fadak) were brought to the Prophet ﷺ by Madinan Jews; asked for the Torah's ruling, they concealed its stoning penalty, coached from afar by Khaybar Jews — *listeners to others who have not come to thee* — with the instruction: *if you are given this* (flogging) *take it; if not* (stoning), *beware* (Ṭ, W, Z). The Prophet ﷺ demanded the Torah; the reader covered the stoning verse with his hand until ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām exposed it; the young rabbi Ibn Ṣūriyā confirmed it; the Prophet ﷺ ordered stoning as revival of the Torah's own ruling (Ṭ, Z). Their leaders had substituted flogging-plus-humiliation when reluctant to stone high-status offenders (Ṭ, Z). *Distorting the word from its places* (*yuḥarrifūna'l-kalima min baʿdi mawāḍiʿihi*) — moving text from context (JJ) — is *taḥrīf* in its literal form (cf. 2:75; 4:46; 5:13). A minority report ties vv. 41–47 to a Naḍīr–Qurayẓah dispute over blood-money versus execution across tribal ranks (Q, Ṭ). Either way the disease is one: scripture edited to flatter the powerful. Every communion that softens texts for donors and sharpens them for dissidents reenacts Khaybar.

**Ibn Ṣūriyā and Ibn Salām: Two Rabbis, Two Verdicts**

The Khaybar drama's Jewish principals deserve their portraits — the verse's *listeners* embodying opposite responses. *Ibn Ṣūriyā*: the young Madinan rabbi-scholar, Torah-memorized, summoned to read; his hand covered the stoning-verse (the tradition's most vivid image of *kitmān* — concealment *manual*); exposed by Ibn Salām, he confirmed the text truthfully when cornered (Ṭ, Z) — knowledge surrendering to pressure, then to proof. Some reports count him among verse 49's tempters-offering-conversion-for-rulings. *ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām*: the senior rabbi who *did* convert — approaching the Prophet ﷺ with three questions only prophets answer (Hour's first sign, Paradise's first food, child's resemblance), converting upon hearing, then requesting his people's testimony-test (they praised him highest; learning his conversion, they reversed instantly — *"our most evil, son of our most evil"* — al-Bukhārī 3329). The juxtaposition is the section's (vv. 41–50) human core: identical scriptures, identical summons — one hand covering, one heart opening. Knowledge never determined outcomes; *reception* did. Every learned audience re-stages it: Ibn Ṣūriyās covering, Ibn Salāms converting. The verse consoles the warner: both responses are *theirs*; delivery was *yours*.

**Trial, Unpurified Hearts, and the Two Disgraces**

*For whomsoever God desires trial* (*fitnah* — trial, temptation, or being left in error), *thou hast no power to avail him aught against God* — when God leaves one to error as consequence of evil choices, no intercession, not even the Prophet's ﷺ, overrides it (R, Ṭ). *Those whose hearts God desired not to purify* — refusing them spiritual aid as consequence of their deeds, or refusing to cleanse the passions driving their denial (R). *Theirs is disgrace in this world* — exposure, like the uncovered hand on the scroll — *and great punishment in the Hereafter* (cf. v. 33). The verse's severity must be read whole: it condemns specific deceivers and hypocrites, not Jews as such — verse 5 licenses their food and marriage, verse 44 honors their Torah as guidance and light. The Qur'an's critique is prosecutorial, not racial: it names acts (lying, concealing, forum-shopping for lenient verdicts), tries them publicly, and sentences them — while leaving the door open for every Jew or hypocrite who abandons the acts.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:42

> **Those who listen to lies, and consume what is unlawful — if they come to thee, then judge between them, or turn away from them. If thou turnest away from them, they will not harm thee in the least. But if thou judgest, judge between them with justice. Truly God loves the just.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bribery and the Corruption of Judgment**

*Listeners to lies, consumers of the unlawful* (*suḥt* — *suḥt*, illicit gain, specifically bribery (Ṭ)) — the pairing is causal: ears that consume lies produce hands that consume bribes. Bribery among the Prophet's ﷺ Jewish contemporaries recurs in verses 62–63. The Prophet ﷺ cursed both ends of the transaction:

> *"God's curse is upon the briber and the bribed."* (Abū Dāwūd 3580; al-Tirmidhī 1337; Ibn Mājah 2313; Aḥmad)

Why does the Qur'an treat bribery as a theological crisis rather than a regulatory one? Because bribery privatizes justice — converting the court from God's scales into a market stall. A judge who sells verdicts does not merely err; he abolishes the very idea that right has claims independent of price. Societies can survive many corruptions, but the sale of judgment dissolves the last referee — after which only force arbitrates. The verse's placement is thus strategic: before discussing *how* the Prophet ﷺ should judge (with justice), it establishes *why* his judgment is needed — because purchasable justice is no justice at all.

**Suḥt: The Qur'an's Economics of Illicit Gain**

*Suḥt* (from *saḥt*: eradication, uprooting) — illicit gain named for its *effect*: it *uproots* blessing, the consumed wealth destroying its consumer (*"flesh nourished on unlawful shall not enter Paradise"* — al-Tirmidhī 614, ḥasan). The Qur'an's *suḥt*-catalogue: *ribā* (usury — 2:275–279), *maysir* (gambling — vv. 90–91), *gharar*-fraud (deception — *"whoever cheats us is not of us,"* Muslim 102), orphans' wealth (*"consuming fire into bellies,"* 4:10), bribes-for-judgment (2:188: *"do not consume wealth falsely nor offer it to rulers to consume sinfully"*), and *all* earnings-by-falsehood. The Madinan *suḥt* here — lenient-verdicts sold to high-status offenders (v. 41's flogging-substitution), Torah-rulings retailed — shows *suḥt*'s religious form: *selling* God's judgments. The caliphal precedent enforced it: ʿUmar dismissed governors for gift-accepting (*"gifts to officials are *ghulūl* [embezzlement]"*), Muʿādh returned surplus collections. Modern *suḥt*-forms multiply: kickbacks, insider-trading, grade-selling, fatwa-retailing, contract-rigging — each *suḥt* by the verse's logic: gain uprooting blessing. The audit-question for every income: *would this earning survive verse 42's court?* *Suḥt*-fed bodies, the Prophet ﷺ warned, feed Fire. Earn cleanly; the clean alone nourishes.

**To Judge or Not to Judge: Jurisdiction Over Non-Muslim Disputes**

*If they come to thee, judge between them or turn away* — the Prophet ﷺ (and by extension Islamic authority) may adjudicate intra-non-Muslim disputes or decline them to their own communal courts. Al-Shāfiʿī and Mālik upheld this option for cases involving no Muslim; cases involving a Muslim must be heard — and then by Islamic law (Q, Ṭ; cf. vv. 48–49). Some held the option abrogated by verse 48's *judge between them* (Q, Ṭ); others kept it. Beneath the jurisprudence lies a pluralist architecture: religious communities retain internal legal autonomy, with the Islamic state as optional appellate forum — and a mandatory one where Muslims are parties. *If thou turnest away, they will not harm thee in the least* — declining jurisdiction carries no risk; God's protection covers the judge who refuses to be used. *But if thou judgest, judge with justice* (*bi'l-qisṭ*; cf. 4:58, 135; 4:65) — no communal favoritism, no forum-shopper's discount. *God loves the just* (*muqsiṭīn*; cf. 60:8) — justice here is not procedural nicety but a loves-of-God virtue. The judge who rules fairly for a hostile litigant has done something God *loves* — a motive beyond any judicial code.

**Dhimmah Courts: The Architecture in History**

The verse's jurisdictional option built Islamic history's *millet*-architecture — and its record deserves recounting. The Madinah Charter (1/622) first drew it: Jews *"a community with the believers,"* retaining *"their religion"* and internal jurisdiction, sharing defense. ʿUmar's Pact extended it imperially: churches, crosses, and courts protected empire-wide. Mature *fiqh* codified: *dhimmīs* (protected non-Muslims) judged internally by own laws (marriage, worship, diet — even wine-and-pork *among themselves*), Islamic courts intervening only for public-order crimes (murder, highway robbery), Muslim-involved disputes (Islamic law), or voluntary submission (this verse's *if they come to thee*). The Ottomans' *millet*-system (Greek, Armenian, Jewish courts with patriarchal chief-justices) ran it for centuries — religious liberty through legal pluralism, predating Western toleration by ages. Abuses occurred (over-taxation, occasional forced uniformity), but the *architecture* — revealed here — outperformed every contemporary alternative: Christendom expelled (Spain 1492) while the Caliphate absorbed (Ottoman rescue of Sephardim). *Judge-or-decline, but justly*: fourteen centuries of courts prove the verse governs pluralism better than either forced uniformity or lawless relativism.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:43

> **And how is it that they come to thee for judgment, when they have the Torah, wherein is God's Judgment? Yet even after that, they turn their backs, and they are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Forum-Shopping for Verdicts: The Question That Exposes Motive**

*How is it that they come to thee for judgment when they have the Torah, wherein is God's Judgment?* The question is not about jurisdiction but sincerity: litigants who possess a clear ruling yet shop for another forum want not justice but leniency (Ṭ). The Khaybar adulterers' case (v. 41) is the exhibit — the Torah prescribed stoning; they sought flogging from Muḥammad ﷺ. The verse universalizes the exposure: whenever parties bypass their own clear law for a hoped-for softer verdict, the maneuver confesses what argument denies — that they know what the law says and wish it said otherwise. Modern analogues abound: jurisdiction-shopping corporations, fatwa-shopping consumers of scholarship, partisans who cite principles only when convenient. The verse's question follows them all: *you have the Book — why are you here?* If the answer is genuine doubt, welcome; the Prophet ﷺ is a judge. If the answer is verdict-hunting, the question itself is the verdict.

**Fatwa-Shopping: The Verse's Mirror for Muslims**

The verse's question — *you have the Book, why seek another verdict?* — reflects devastatingly on contemporary *fatwa*-shopping, and the parallel needs pressing. The syndrome: questioner seeks ruling, dislikes answer, seeks second scholar, third website, fourth satellite-shaykh — until *desire* finds its *muftī*. The Khaybar-structure replays exactly: clear text possessed (stoning-verse / plain prohibition), softer forum sought (Muḥammad's ﷺ hoped-leniency / lenient *muftī*), verdict-hunting disguised as inquiry. The Prophet ﷺ warned the demand-side: *"Whoever seeks knowledge to argue with scholars, dispute fools, or turn faces toward him — Fire"* (al-Tirmidhī 2654); and the supply-side: scholars issuing desire-fatwas sell *suḥt* (v. 42). Legitimate second-opinions differ categorically: genuine doubt, qualified scholars, evidence-weighed — the questioner seeking *truth*, not *taste*. The verse's diagnostic distinguishes them: does the seeker *stop* at the first qualified answer, or *shop* till desire is licensed? Stopping indicates sincerity; shopping confesses verdict-hunting. *Kayfa yuḥakkimūnaka* — how is it they seek *your* judgment? — the question every desire-driven fatwa-seeker must answer. The Book you have suffices; the verdict you seek indicts.

**"They Are Not Believers": Disbelief as Disobedience to One's Own Book**

*Yet even after that, they turn their backs* — after the Prophet's ﷺ ruling (or after the Torah's own clarity), they withdraw — *and they are not believers*. The charge is devastating in its specificity: their disbelief is measured not against Islam's claims but against *their own* scripture's demands. One need not accept Muḥammad ﷺ to stand condemned here; it suffices to betray Moses while claiming him. The verse thus establishes a principle the sūrah will press repeatedly (vv. 44, 45, 47, 66, 68): each community is accountable to the revelation it *acknowledges*. The Torah remains, in this verse's frank admission, a vessel of *God's Judgment* — valid, binding, luminous — even in the Prophet's ﷺ own presence. The Qur'an does not say "your Book is void, so submit to mine"; it says "your Book is true, so submit to *it*." That is a far more searching challenge — and, for interfaith relations, a far more respectful one.

**"God's Judgment" in the Torah: The Qur'an's Canon Within the Canon**

*Fīhā ḥukmu'Llāh* — "wherein is God's judgment": the Qur'an affirms *genuine* Divine content surviving in the Torah — and the affirmation's scope needs careful bounding, for two errors flank it. *Error one* (wholesale validation): treating the present Torah-text as fully intact revelation — contradicted by the Qur'an's *taḥrīf* charges (2:75; 4:46; 5:13, 41) and internal textual evidences. *Error two* (wholesale dismissal): treating it as fully corrupted — contradicted by *this verse* (God's judgment *therein*), verse 44 (*guidance and light*), and the Prophet's ﷺ Torah-honoring (placing it on a cushion, swearing *"I believe in thee and Him who revealed thee"* — Abū Dāwūd 4449). The middle path (Ibn Taymiyyah's *al-Jawāb al-Ṣaḥīḥ*): the Torah contains *authentic* Divine judgment (monotheism, Decalogue-morality, stoning, *qiṣāṣ* — confirmed by Qur'an) admixed with *human* alteration (anthropomorphisms, prophetic slanders, legal distortions — refuted by Qur'an). The Qur'an functions as *furqān* (criterion, v. 48's *muḥaymin*): confirming the genuine, correcting the altered. Muslims approach the Torah thus: reverence its Divine strata, test its texts by Qur'an, mine its wisdom where confirmed. *Fīhā ḥukmu'Llāh* — therein, genuinely, God's judgment survives. Seek it with criterion in hand.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:44

> **Truly We sent down the Torah, wherein is a guidance and a light, by which the prophets who submitted [unto God] judged those who are Jews, as did the sages and the rabbis, in accordance with such of God's Book as they were bidden to preserve and to which they were witnesses. So fear not mankind, but fear Me! And sell not My signs for a paltry price. Whosoever judges not by that which God has sent down — it is they who are disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Torah as Guidance and Light: The Qur'an's Tribute**

*Truly We sent down the Torah, wherein is guidance and light* (*hudan wa nūr*; cf. 6:91) — the Qur'an's tribute to Moses' Book, ungrudging and explicit. By it judged *the prophets who submitted* (*aslamū* — Israelite prophets between Moses and Jesus (Z); a few read Muḥammad ﷺ via *islām* (Ṭ)), *the sages* (*rabbāniyyūn* — legal authorities (Ṭ), Aaronide ascetics/priests (Z), or supreme jurisprudents (R)) and *rabbis* (*aḥbār* — scholars). They judged *by what they were bidden to preserve* (*istuhfiẓū*) *of God's Book, to which they were witnesses* (*shuhadā'*). Two offices: *preservation* (guarding text and meaning) and *witness* (testifying it publicly). Scholarship that preserves without witnessing is hoarding; witness without preservation is noise. The verse quietly defines the Rabbinate's — and by extension every clergy's — job description: keep the Book intact, speak it aloud. Wherever scribes altered penalties for the powerful (v. 41) or swallowed rulings for bribes (v. 42), they failed both offices at once.

**Rabbāniyyūn and Aḥbār: The Scholarly Orders**

The verse's two scholarly orders — *rabbāniyyūn* (sages) and *aḥbār* (rabbis) — map Israel's learned hierarchy, and the offices illuminate by parallel. *Rabbāniyyūn* (Lordly-ones — from *Rabb*): the God-devoted elite — legal-political authorities (Ṭ), Aaronide ascetic-priests (Z), supreme jurisprudents (R) — paralleling Islam's *mujtahidūn* and *awliyā'*. *Aḥbār* (from *ḥibr*: ink-trace, scholarship): the text-scholars, scribes, teachers — paralleling Islam's *ʿulamā'*. Their twin commission: *preservation* (*istiḥfāẓ* — entrusted guarding: *"with what they were bidden to preserve"*) and *witness* (*shahādah* — public testimony: *"to which they were witnesses"*). The Qur'an elsewhere defines the *rabbānī* ideal universally: *"Be lordly-scholars, by teaching the Book and studying it"* (3:79) — scholarship *plus* pedagogy *plus* devotion. Failure-modes indicted across the sūrah: preserving-without-witnessing (Khaybar's covered verse — hoarded truth), witnessing-without-preserving (distorted rulings — corrupted testimony), selling both (v. 44's *paltry price*). The job description transfers to every *ʿālim*, pastor, and professor: guard the text intact, testify it publicly, sell it never. Two offices, one entrustment — audited hereafter (v. 63's terror-verse for the silent).

**"Sell Not My Signs": The Economics of Religious Betrayal**

*Fear not mankind, but fear Me! Sell not My signs for a paltry price* (*thamanan qalīlan*) — the Qur'an's standing idiom for trading eternal truth for worldly gain (2:41, 79, 174; 3:77, 187, 199; 9:9; 16:95). Two motives for distortion are named: *fear* (of the powerful whose adultery or murder the text condemns) and *price* (wealth, status, position). Every pulpit faces the same two bidders: intimidation and purchase. The verse prices the transaction honestly — whatever is gained is *paltry* (*qalīl*), however large it looks: all the world's gold against one verse of God is small change against a kingdom. *Whosoever judges not by what God sent down — they are the disbelievers* (*kāfirūn*) — *kāfir* also meaning *coverer*: those who cover scripture's rulings (Ṭ). Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have calibrated the trilogy across verses 44, 45, 47: active rejection of God's judgment is disbelief; mere failure to apply it is wrongdoing (*ẓulm*) and iniquity (*fisq*) (Z). The calibration matters: it reserves the gravest verdict for defiant repudiation, not for every judicial lapse — severity with a scale, not a blanket.

**Fear of Men Versus Fear of God: The Scholar's Crucible**

*Fear not men, but fear Me* (*lā takhshawu'n-nāsa wa'khshawnī*) — the scholar's crucible-verse, and its martyrology needs honoring. The *fear-of-men* corrupts: Khaybar's flogging-substitution (fearing high-status adulterers — v. 41), court-*ʿulamā'* blessing tyrannies, academics self-censoring for tenure, preachers trimming sermons for donors. The *fear-of-God* liberates: Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal flogged senseless under the *miḥnah* (inquisition) yet refusing *"the Qur'an is created"*; Ibn Taymiyyah imprisoned in Damascus's citadel, declaring *"what can my enemies do to me? My garden is in my breast"*; al-ʿIzz ibn ʿAbd al-Salām — *sulṭān al-ʿulamā'* — selling amīrs publicly (they were technically unmanumitted slaves, legally unsuited to rule) and defying kings to their faces. The Prophet ﷺ priced pulpit-courage: *"The best striving is a word of truth before a tyrannical ruler"* (Abū Dāwūd 4344; al-Tirmidhī 2174). The verse's economics completes it: fear-of-men *pays* paltry (salaries, safety, status — *qalīl*, all of it), fear-of-God *pays* eternal (the *ṣiddīqīn*'s company — 4:69). Every scholar, journalist, and witness faces the two fears daily; the verse commands the portfolio: divest men-fear fully, invest God-fear wholly. The crucible tests all; the verse grades eternally.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:45

> **And therein We prescribed for them: a life for a life, an eye for an eye, a nose for a nose, an ear for an ear, a tooth for a tooth, and for wounds, retribution. But whosoever forgoes it out of charity, it shall be an expiation for him. Whosoever judges not by that which God has sent down — it is they who are wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Retribution Without Rank: The Torah's Egalitarian Code**

The verse rehearses the Torah's *lex talionis* (Exodus 21:24; Leviticus 24:20; Deuteronomy 19:21) — *a life for a life, an eye for an eye...* — to indict its corruption: the Banū Naḍīr (high-status) paid mere compensation for killing a Qurayẓah member, while the reverse killing brought execution or multiplied payment (R, Ṭ). The Torah's formula knows no such arithmetic of rank: *a life for a life* — the prince's eye and the pauper's eye weigh identically. The Qur'an's endorsement here (with Islam's parallel in 2:178; 4:92; 42:40) makes equality before retributive law a Mosaic *and* Muḥammadan principle. Note what *lex talionis* actually limits: in blood-feud Arabia, one killing licensed seven; the formula caps vengeance at equivalence — a ceiling as much as a sentence. Civilization's first victory over revenge was not forgiveness (that comes next) but *proportion*: only an eye for an eye, never a village for an eye. The verse then adds what the Biblical passages lack — the higher option.

**Qiṣāṣ in Islamic Law: Equivalence with Mercy**

The Qur'an's own *qiṣāṣ*-code (2:178–179; 4:92; 17:33; 42:40) builds on this Torah-foundation — and its complete structure needs exhibiting. *Scope*: intentional homicide and intentional wounding (equivalence strictly measurable — eye, tooth, limb; immeasurable wounds convert to compensation). *Standing*: the victim's heir (*walī al-dam*) chooses — *qiṣāṣ*, *diyah* (blood-money), or free pardon (2:178). *Safeguards*: intent proven (accidents → *diyah*-only, 4:92), equivalence exact (excess barred — 17:33: *"do not exceed in killing"*), doubt voiding, minors-and-insane exempted, pregnancy delaying. *Purpose stated*: *"in retribution is life for you, O possessors of intellect"* (2:179) — *life*: deterrence preserving the would-be killer's victim *and* the would-be avenger's restraint. The Naḍīr-Qurayẓah corruption this verse indicts — rank-priced blood — the Prophet ﷺ abolished structurally: *"the blood of all Muslims is equal"* (Ibn Mājah 2682; al-Nasā'ī), the Farewell Sermon's *"all blood-revenge of Jāhiliyyah is under my feet"* (Muslim 1218). *Qiṣāṣ* is thus *equality's* penal form: one scale, all persons, no multipliers for nobility. Proportion first (this verse's ceiling), pardon highest (this verse's crown) — justice ascending toward mercy without abandoning equity.

**Forgiveness as Expiation: The Better Justice**

*Whosoever forgoes it as charity, it shall be expiation for him* (*kaffārah*) — debated: expiation for the forgiver's sins (al-Ṭabarī's view, citing ḥadīth that charity-forgiveness expiates the forgiver (Ṭ)) or for the offender (Ṭ, Z); 42:40 promises the forgiver a Divine reward either way. The structure offers three tiers: equivalent retribution (justice), compensation (mercy — available in Islamic law though, per the commentators, the Torah allowed only retribution or outright forgiveness (Q, R)), and free forgiveness (excellence). The tiers ascend without abolishing: retribution remains *available* so forgiveness remains *meaningful* — pardon costs nothing where punishment is impossible. *Whosoever judges not by it — they are the wrongdoers* (*ẓālimūn*) — the trilogy's middle term (cf. v. 44): distorting equal retribution into rank-based pricing is *ẓulm*, putting persons where they do not belong — some above the scales, some beneath them. Justice holds one scale for all; the verse holds one verdict for all who tamper with it.

**Pardon That Heals: Forgiveness Narratives**

The *forgo-as-charity* tier deserves its narratives — forgiveness *performed* at blood's highest stakes. The Prophet ﷺ *preferred* pardon visibly: every *qiṣāṣ*-case brought to him, he urged forgiveness first (Abū Dāwūd 4497: *"no case of retribution came to God's Messenger but he commanded pardon"*). At Makkah's conquest he pardoned Hind (Ḥamzah's mutilator), Wahshī (Ḥamzah's killer — converted, though the Prophet ﷺ asked him to veil his face, grief's honesty preserved — al-Bukhārī 4072), and Ibn Abī Sarḥ (apostate-scribe). ʿUmar's courts institutionalized *diyah*-negotiation; ʿAlī's caliphate modeled it personally — pardoning his assassin Ibn Muljam's torture (forbidding mutilation: *"kill him as he killed me, no excess"* — Nahj al-Balāghah; Ṭabarī's history). The *kaffārah*-for-forgiver reading (Ṭ) adds the incentive-structure: forgiving murder *expiates the forgiver's* sins — victim's pardon purchasing pardon's own pardon. Modern restorative-justice movements (victim-offender mediation, truth commissions) stumble toward this tier without revelation's vocabulary; the verse legislated it fourteen centuries prior: retribution available (justice secured), compensation structured (mercy organized), pardon expiating (excellence rewarded). Three tiers, one staircase — ascending from equity to heaven.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:46

> **And in their footsteps, We sent Jesus son of Mary, confirming the Torah that had come before him, and We gave him the Gospel, wherein is a guidance and a light, confirming the Torah that had come before him, as a guidance and an exhortation to the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**In Their Footsteps: Prophecy as a Single File**

*In their footsteps* (*ʿalā āthārihim*) — of the submitted prophets of verse 44 (R, Z) — *We sent Jesus son of Mary*. Prophecy is a single file, each messenger stepping where predecessors stepped, not a scatter of rivals. Jesus is *confirming* (*muṣaddiq*) *the Torah before him* (cf. 3:50; 61:6; John the Baptist likewise, 3:39) — upholding every Torah ruling the Gospel does not specifically abrogate (Ṭ), and relaxing some prohibitions (3:50: *making lawful part of what was forbidden*). The confirmation runs both directions in the verse: Jesus confirms the Torah, and *the Gospel* — *wherein is guidance and light*, the Torah's exact honors (v. 44) — *confirms it too, as guidance and exhortation to the reverent* (*mawʿiẓah li'l-muttaqīn*). The Qur'an's Jesus is thus no law-abolisher but a law-renewer: same covenant, deepened interiority, lightened load. The passage quietly answers two opposite errors — those who claim Jesus abolished the Law entirely, and those who claim Muḥammad ﷺ dishonors Jesus. The Qur'an honors him precisely *as* a confirmer: greatness in prophecy is measured by fidelity to the file, not by deviation from it.

**Taṣdīq: Confirmation as Prophecy's Signature**

*Muṣaddiq* (confirming) — Jesus' office toward the Torah — is prophecy's *universal* signature in the Qur'an, and the chain needs exhibiting. Each messenger confirms predecessors: Muḥammad ﷺ confirms all (2:41; 3:3; 5:48), Jesus confirms Torah (here; 3:50; 61:6), John confirms the Word (3:39), the Qur'an confirms *"what was before it"* (10:37; 12:111; 35:31; 46:30). The mechanism: *same* God, *same* core (tawḥīd, accountability, virtue), *developing* law (details adapted per community-era — v. 48's *shirʿah wa minhāj*). Confirmation *plus* calibrated abrogation (*naskh*): Jesus relaxing Sabbath-and-dietary stringencies (3:50), Muḥammad ﷺ finalizing law's evolution — each step *confirming* foundations while *updating* applications. The anti-pattern is *takdhīb* (denial): each community affirming its prophet while denying others (2:87–91; 4:150–151: *"we believe in some, disbelieve in others"* — branded *true disbelief*, 4:151). The believer's confession inverts it: *"we make no distinction among His messengers"* (2:285) — the *taṣdīq*-chain honored whole. Test every claim to guidance by the signature: does it *confirm* established truth while advancing it? Confirmers continue prophecy's file; deniers break it; innovators abandon it. Jesus' *taṣdīq* authenticates him; the criterion authenticates all.

**The Injīl's Light: Guidance and Exhortation**

*Ātaynāhu'l-Injīl, fīhi hudan wa nūr* — "We gave him the Gospel, wherein is guidance and light": the Torah's *exact* honors (v. 44) transferred undiminished — and the Gospel's Qur'anic profile needs completing. Its content-character: *confirmation* of Torah (this verse), *guidance* (*hudā* — direction), *light* (*nūr* — illumination), *exhortation* (*mawʿiẓah* — heart-preaching) *for the reverent* (*muttaqīn* — the receptive). Its historical form per the tradition: revelation to Jesus (not later compositions *about* him), dominated by *wisdom-and-exhortation* more than legislation (Z) — parables, beatitudes, warnings — with *few* legal abrogations of Torah (dietary easings, Sabbath-spirit). Its present-text status (Ibn Taymiyyah's criterion): genuine Injīl-strata (monotheistic sayings, ethical parables, Jesus' prophecies of Aḥmad — 61:6) admixed with later accretions — tested, like Torah, by Qur'anic *furqān*. The *muttaqīn*-restriction (*exhortation to the reverent*) teaches receptivity's law: the same Gospel hardens Pharisees and softens fishermen — guidance avails the God-fearing, whatever its testament. Muslims honor the Injīl accordingly: revering its Divine light, testing its text by Criterion, loving its Bearer as Messiah — guidance recognized across testaments, exhortation heeded in every age.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:47

> **Let the people of the Gospel judge by what God has sent down therein. Whosoever judges not by that which God has sent down — it is they who are iniquitous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gospel's Standing Court: Validity in the Prophet's Presence**

*Let the people of the Gospel judge by what God sent down therein* — the Torah's endorsement (vv. 43–44) extended to the Gospel: Jesus lived largely by Torah rulings except where the Gospel abrogated (IK), the Gospel supplying teachings and exhortations more than codes (Z). The command's plain sense is present-tense validity: Christians *now*, even with Muḥammad ﷺ among them, are to judge by their Gospel — matching verse 43's question (why come to the Prophet ﷺ when you have the Torah?) and verse 68's *observe the Torah and the Gospel*. Some commentators restricted the command to the pre-Qur'anic era, holding the Qur'an abrogated earlier scriptures (IK, R) — but the source commentary judges this implausible precisely because the Prophet ﷺ himself *revived* Torah rulings (the stoning judgment) rather than superseding them (see vv. 41–44). The sūrah's architecture supports the plain sense: verses 43–47 form one chamber in which both Books stand as living law for their peoples. *Whosoever judges not by it — they are the iniquitous* (*fāsiqūn*) — completing Ibn ʿAbbās's trilogy: disbelief (44), wrongdoing (45), iniquity (47) (Z). Three verdicts, one disease: possessing God's judgment and judging by something else.

**Naskh of Scriptures? The Abrogation Debate Examined**

The abrogation-reading (Qur'an *naskh*-ing Torah-and-Gospel wholesale — IK, R) needs full hearing and answer, for it shapes Muslim-Christian-Jewish relations fundamentally. *The abrogation case*: Qur'an as final revelation supersedes (2:106's *naskh*-principle generalized), Muḥammad ﷺ as final prophet universalizes (7:158; 34:28), and post-Qur'anic adherence to prior laws defies finality. *The validity case* (this section's plain sense): verses 43–47 command *present-tense* judging-by-own-scriptures *during* Muḥammad's ﷺ ministry; verse 48 appoints *multiple* contemporaneous laws-and-ways; verse 68 commands observing Torah-Gospel-*plus*-Qur'an; the Prophet ﷺ *enforced* Torah (stoning) rather than voiding it; and 2:62/5:69 envision salvation *within* prior communities. *The harmonization* (mainstream classical): prior scriptures' *authentic Divine content* remains valid-and-binding (moral law, monotheism, unabrogated rulings), while *Muḥammad's ﷺ prophethood* binds all who authentically receive it (4:136; 7:157) — Jews and Christians addressed *both* as Torah-Gospel observers *and* as Muḥammad-invited (vv. 15, 19, 68). The abrogation applies to *superseded particulars* and *exclusive claims* (salvation-monopoly, finality-denial), not to scriptures' Divine substance. The section's architecture confirms: three scriptures standing, one Criterion crowning (v. 48) — supersession of *particulars*, never demolition of *predecessors*.

**The Trilogy Complete: Kāfirūn, Ẓālimūn, Fāsiqūn**

The thrice-repeated verdict — *disbelievers* (44), *wrongdoers* (45), *iniquitous* (47) — *"whoever judges not by what God sent down"* — completes the section's most-quoted triad, and Ibn ʿAbbās's calibration (Z) needs its full implications. *Active repudiation* (denying God's judgment's authority, preferring other law *as superior*) = *kufr* (disbelief) — the Khārijite-proof-text rightly bounded: the verse condemns *ideological* rejection, not every sinful judge. *Mere non-application* (knowing the ruling, failing it through weakness, bribery, or bias) = *ẓulm/fisq* (wrongdoing/iniquity) — grave sin, not apostasy. Ibn ʿAbbās's reported gloss — *"kufr less than kufr"* (*kufr dūna kufr* — Ṭ; al-Ḥākim) — became Sunnī orthodoxy's firewall against *takfīr*-excess: unjust Muslim rulers sin grievously but exit Islam only by *repudiating* Divine law's authority. The calibration's modern stakes are mortal: extremists wielding verse 44 to excommunicate entire societies (then wage war on them) violate Ibn ʿAbbās, the trilogy's gradation, and fourteen centuries of jurisprudence. The verses grade precisely: repudiate → disbelief; distort → wrongdoing; neglect → iniquity. Judge judges by this scale — and leave *takfīr* to its impossibly strict conditions. Severity with gradation; never blankets, always scales.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:48

> **And We have sent down unto thee the Book in truth, confirming the Book that came before it, and as a protector over it. So judge between them in accordance with what God has sent down, and follow not their caprices away from the truth that has come unto thee. For each among you We have appointed a law and a way. And had God willed, He would have made you one community, but [He willed otherwise], that He might try you in that which He has given you. So vie with one another in good deeds. Unto God shall be your return all together, and He will inform you of that wherein you differ.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Muḥaymin: The Qur'an as Guardian of the Scriptures**

*Confirming* (*muṣaddiq*) *the Book before it, and as protector* (*muḥaymin*) *over it* — *muḥaymin* (trustee, keeper, guardian; Ṭ, Z) shares its form with God's own Name *al-Muḥaymin* (59:23): as God guards creation, the Qur'an guards revelation — testifying to earlier scriptures' validity, preserving their truths, and arbitrating their corruptions (cf. 2:41, 89, 91, 97, 101; 3:3, 81; 6:92; 35:31; 46:30). The Prophet's ﷺ revival of Torah penalties (vv. 41, 45) demonstrates the office in action: the Qur'an does not bulldoze previous revelation but *restores* it. *Judge between them by what God sent down* — most read: by the Qur'an (Bḍ, Ṭ, Z); others: each people by its own scripture (as the Prophet ﷺ did with the Torah) — *and follow not their caprices* (*ahwā'*) *away from the truth*: neither their distortions of their own law (Ṭ) nor their pressure to bend his (Bḍ; cf. 2:120, 145; 5:49; 42:15). *Hawā* (caprice) is desire disguised as judgment — the verse's standing villain across this whole section. The judge's only loyalty is to *what God sent down*; every other loyalty — to tribe, donor, faction, or flatterer — is caprice in costume.

**How Muḥaymin Works: The Criterion in Operation**

*Muḥaymin*-ship (guardianship) operates through three functions — and each needs its case-law. *Confirmation* (*taṣdīq*): affirming authentic strata — monotheism (Deuteronomy 6:4 // 112:1), Decalogue-morality (Exodus 20 // 17:22–39), prophetic narratives (Joseph, Moses, David, Jonah — retold purified). *Correction* (*taṣḥīḥ*): repairing corruptions — prophetic slanders voided (David's adultery, Solomon's idolatry, Aaron's calf — all Qur'anically exonerated: 2:102; 38:17–40; 20:85–94), anthropomorphisms transcended (*"nothing is like Him,"* 42:11), legal distortions restored (stoning revived, v. 41). *Completion* (*takmīl*): supplying finality — universal law, sealed prophethood, preserved text (15:9). The Prophet ﷺ demonstrated the method live: honoring Torah's monotheism while correcting its tradents' concealments; affirming Jesus' virgin-birth-and-miracles while correcting deification. Muslims wielding *muḥaymin* in dialogue should mirror the triad: *confirm* genuinely (begin with shared truth), *correct* gently (criterion applied, persons honored), *complete* humbly (Qur'an offered, never imposed). Guardianship is trusteeship, not triumphalism — the Criterion serves truth across testaments, guarding God's words wherever Divinely spoken.

**"A Law and a Way": The Providence of Religious Plurality**

*For each among you We have appointed a law* (*shirʿah* — same root as *sharīʿah*) *and a way* (*minhāj* — smooth, clear path; Ṭ, Z) — distinct ritual-legal formulations Divinely *appointed* per community, independent yet rooted in shared essential truths (IK, Q, R, Ṭ). Exclusivist readings strain here — Mujāhid's "one law, Islam" (Ṭ), Ibn Kathīr's elided "Qur'an" (IK), the era-by-era succession view (Bḍ, Z) — but the next clause resists them: *had God willed, He would have made you one community* — a counterfactual declaring plurality itself *willed* (Ṭ; cf. 2:213; 10:19; 11:118; 16:93; 42:8). Like gender, race, and status (49:13), religious difference is providential raw material — *that He might try you in what He gave you*: obedience within one's own form (Ṭ), differing qiblahs testing who follows the Messenger (2:143–45). The trial reading that reduces plurality to supersession-abrogation (IK, Q) captures part-truth; the verse's plain drift captures more: difference as Divine pedagogy, not merely human deviance.

**Shirʿah and Minhāj: Law Versus Way**

*Shirʿah* (law) and *minhāj* (way) — the pair repays philology, for it structures all Islamic pluralism-thinking. *Shirʿah* (from *sh-r-ʿ*: watering-place, the path to water): the *codified* Divine legislation — rituals, penalties, dietary codes, family law — varying per community-era (Sabbath for Israel, Friday for Muslims; dietary stringencies relaxed progressively). *Minhāj* (from *n-h-j*: smooth, open road): the *method* of traversal — spiritual praxis, ethical style, communal ethos. Both *appointed* (*jaʿalnā*) — Divinely instituted, not humanly evolved. Beneath both lies the *unvarying* core — *dīn* (religion per se): tawḥīd, prophethood, accountability, virtue (*"He legislated for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, Abraham, Moses, Jesus: establish religion, do not divide therein"* — 42:13). The architecture: one *dīn*, multiple *sharā'iʿ*, multiple *manāhij* — unity of essence, plurality of form. Ibn Taymiyyah's formulation (*al-Jawāb al-Ṣaḥīḥ*): prophets agree in *uṣūl* (foundations), differ in *furūʿ* (branches) per wisdom. The ecumenical grammar follows: unite on *dīn* (One God, final accountability, virtue), respect *sharīʿah*-differences (Sabbath-keepers, Gospel-livers), compete in *minhāj*-goods (v. 48's race). Confusing levels — demanding *shirʿah*-uniformity or surrendering *dīn*-unity — breaks the verse's architecture. One religion, many laws, one race: goodness.

**"Vie in Good Deeds": Competition Without Coercion**

*So vie with one another in good deeds* (*fa'stabiqū'l-khayrāt*; cf. 2:148; 3:114) — addressed, most plausibly, to humanity as such (Ṭ): communities coexisting *contemporaneously*, racing in virtue. The race's track is *practice*, not polemics — feeding, sheltering, healing, beautifying — while *theological* differences await the only competent Tribunal: *unto God shall be your return all together, and He will inform you of what you differed in* (cf. v. 14). This verse anchors Islamic pluralism: truth is one and worth witnessing to, yet God Himself ordained multiple lawful forms and deferred final arbitration to Himself. The believer's marching orders follow: hold your convictions without apology, honor others' God-given forms without indifference, compete in goodness without ceasing — and leave the gavel, with all clashing claims, to the Day when the Informed will inform.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:49

> **And judge between them in accordance with what God has sent down, and follow not their caprices! Beware of them, lest they tempt thee away from any part of what God has sent down upon thee. If they turn away, then know that God desires to smite them for some of their sins. And truly many among mankind are iniquitous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Temptation at the Summit: Pressuring the Perfect Judge**

*Judge between them by what God sent down; follow not their caprices; beware, lest they tempt thee* (*fitnah* — trial, seduction) *away from any part of what God sent down*. The occasion: Jewish leaders offered the Prophet ﷺ mass conversion if he would judge in their favor — "our enmity would end," they promised, dangling an entire tribe's submission as the price of one lenient verdict (Q, Ṭ, Ṭs, W). The temptation's subtlety deserves pause: it wore the costume of *daʿwah*-strategy — compromise a ruling, gain a community. Revelation names it *fitnah*: outcomes never justify corrupting judgment, and converts purchased with injustice are liabilities, not assets. Leaders face the diluted form constantly — the donor's request, the faction's litmus test, the popularity cost of an unpopular-but-true ruling. The verse's answer is absolute: *any part* (*ʿan baʿḍi mā*) — not one clause of revealed law is negotiable currency. Integrity untempted is integrity untested; integrity tested-and-held is the verse's whole demand.

**The Conversion-Bribe: Anatomy of a Pious Temptation**

The offered bribe — *rule our way, we convert en masse* — deserves anatomical study, for its species recurs wherever religion meets power. *The bait*: mass conversion (the noblest outcome — souls saved, enmity ended, *ummah* strengthened). *The price*: one verdict bent (a trivial cost, seemingly — *baʿḍ*, a mere part). *The logic*: consequentialist daʿwah — ends (conversions) sanctifying means (injustice). *Revelation's veto*: absolute — *beware lest they tempt thee from ANY part* — consequences never license corruption; justice is *constitutive* of the mission, not *instrumental* to it. The Prophet's ﷺ immunity models the response: no negotiation entertained, no counter-offer made, verdicts issued straight. History's counter-examples warn: Muʿtazilite caliphs bending creed for unity (the *miḥnah* — unity shattered, credibility burned), modern movements trimming doctrine for coalitions (coalitions dissolving, doctrine lost). The iron law: *injustice never purchases genuine conversion* — converts bought with bent verdicts learn verdicts bend, then bend them further. The verse's economics: one straight verdict outweighs a thousand purchased conversions. Hold the part; God sends the peoples (110:2's crowds came *after* integrity, never before).

**Turning Away as Self-Smiting**

*If they turn away* (*tawallaw*) — from the Prophet's ﷺ judgment or from truth itself (Ṭ) — *then know that God desires to smite them for some of their sins*. *Some* (*bi-baʿḍi dhunūbihim*) — even partial requital suffices; full requital awaits (cf. 4:62; 42:30, 34). Turning away is itself the mechanism of smiting: rejecting just judgment, the turner is left to unjust judges — caprice's courts, where verdicts go to the highest bidder. God's "desire to smite" operates through abandonment as much as through affliction. *Many among mankind are iniquitous* (*fāsiqūn*) — the section's closing census (cf. v. 47's *fāsiqūn*): persistent departure from revealed order is humanity's statistical norm, not its exception. The verse thus steadies the just judge twice: the powerful will pressure you (stand firm), and the many will spurn you (stand anyway). Majorities confer no verdicts; only God does.

**Tawallī: The Mechanics of Turning Away**

*Tawallaw* (they turned away) — the Qur'an's verb for revelation-rejected — operates through traceable mechanics worth mapping, for every community contains turners. *Stage one* (hearing-without-heeding): truth heard, deferred — *"we hear and disobey"* (2:93; 4:46). *Stage two* (arguing-against): objections manufactured — caprice costumed as critique (vv. 48–49's *ahwā'*). *Stage three* (pressuring-the-warmer): bribes offered (this verse's occasion), threats issued, coalitions mobilized. *Stage four* (turning): physical withdrawal, communal secession, rival order erected. *Divine response* (this verse): *smiting for some sins* — abandonment to consequences (unjust judges, fractured community, hardened hearts — 2:7's seal, 4:155's covering). The pattern recurs identically: Pharaoh's magicians believed (turning *toward*), Pharaoh turned (drowning); Quraysh's converts entered (turning toward), Abū Jahl turned (Badr). *Ta-ba-ʿḍa dhunūb* (for *some* sins) adds mercy's remainder: earthly smiting is *partial* — full settlement deferred, repentance still possible. The pastoral application: communities witnessing members' *tawallī* should neither chase (compulsion void — 2:256) nor despair (partial smiting may redirect) — but hold judgment straight, doors open, prayers earnest. Some turners return (ʿIkrimah at conquest); all returners find the verse's Judge still just.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:50

> **Is it the judgment of pagan ignorance that they seek? And who is better than God in judgment for people who have certainty?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Jāhiliyyah: Ignorance as a Legal System**

*Is it the judgment of jāhiliyyah* (pagan ignorance) *that they seek?* *Jāhiliyyah* here is not an era but a jurisprudence — judgment by caprice, kinship, bribery, and power, the Naḍīr-Qurayẓah double-standard (v. 45) being its local specimen. The question's sting: these are *scriptured* peoples — Torah and Gospel in hand — yet they seek *pagan* judgment, preferring pre-revelation darkness to revealed light. The verse thus defines *jāhiliyyah* functionally: any adjudication whose ultimate authority is desire rather than revelation. By that definition *jāhiliyyah* did not end in 622; it persists wherever verdicts track wealth, tribe, race, or ideology rather than truth. Modern readers should feel the question's rotation toward themselves: every legal system, religious court, and academic committee must ask — do our judgments descend from principle, or ascend from preference? The verse allows only two benches: God's, and ignorance's. There is no third.

**Jāhiliyyah's Afterlife: The Concept's Expansions**

*Jāhiliyyah*-as-jurisprudence (not era) ramifies across the Qur'an — and the concept's full semantic field needs mapping. *Qur'anic usages*: judgment-*jāhiliyyah* (this verse), boast-*jāhiliyyah* (48:26: *"the ḥamiyyah of jāhiliyyah"* — tribal partisan zeal), display-*jāhiliyyah* (33:33: *"the display of former jāhiliyyah"* — immodest self-exhibition), suspicion-*jāhiliyyah* (3:154: *"thinking of God other than truth — jāhiliyyah-thought"*). *The common thread*: *pre-/anti-revelation ordering* — zeal without guidance, display without modesty, judgment without revelation, theology without truth. *Classical extension*: Ibn Taymiyyah applied *jāhiliyyah* to post-Islamic phenomena (Mongol *Yasa*-law preferred over Sharīʿah — governance-*jāhiliyyah*); modern revivalists (Mawdūdī, Quṭb) extended to secular modernity's sovereignty-claims — *jāhiliyyah* as any order *independent* of revelation. *The verse's restraint*: it targets *adjudication-by-caprice* specifically — verdicts tracking wealth, kin, power. That precision guards the concept from inflation (labeling all modernity *jāhiliyyah* cheapens the charge) while preserving its edge (naming rank-justice, bribe-justice, ideology-justice *jāhiliyyah* truly). Two benches only: God's and ignorance's. Audit every verdict; sit on the right one.

**Who Judges Better Than God? The Question That Ends Debate**

*Who is better than God in judgment* (*ḥukm*) *for people of certainty* (*mūqinūn*)? The rhetorical question admits no answer but silence — which is the answer. Divine judgment excels human judgment in knowledge (all facts, all hearts), in impartiality (no tribe, no bribe, no fear), and in wisdom (each ruling placed where eternity requires). The qualifier *for people of certainty* is precise: God's judgment's superiority is *evident* only to the certain — the doubter, weighing revelation against desire, finds the comparison inconclusive. Certainty (*yaqīn*) here is not blind submission but tested trust: those who have *seen* Divine judgments vindicated — in history, in conscience, in consequence — no longer entertain the comparison. The verse closes the sūrah's great legal section (vv. 41–50) where it began: with the judge's choice. Two benches, two verdicts, two eternities. The certain have chosen; the verse asks the rest to catch up.

**Yaqīn: Certainty as Judgment's Prerequisite**

*Li-qawmin yūqinūn* — "for people who have certainty": the qualifier restricts Divine judgment's *evident* superiority to the certain — and *yaqīn*'s epistemology deserves unfolding. The Qur'an's certainty-ladder: *ʿilm al-yaqīn* (knowledge-of-certainty — 102:5: proven truth), *ʿayn al-yaqīn* (eye-of-certainty — 102:7: witnessed truth), *ḥaqq al-yaqīn* (truth-of-certainty — 56:95; 69:51: experienced truth). Applied to judgment: the certain have *seen* revelation's verdicts vindicated — historically (just orders flourishing, corrupt orders collapsing), conscientially (fitrah testifying), consequently (obedience blessing, defiance wounding). The doubter finds comparison inconclusive — desire's bench *looks* competitive until tested. The verse's pastoral logic: certainty is *cultivated* through judgment-experience — each obeyed ruling vindicated grows *yaqīn*; each vindication sharpens the next obedience. The Prophet ﷺ prayed for it: *"O God, apportion us fear restraining disobedience, obedience attaining Paradise, and certainty lightening afflictions"* (al-Tirmidhī 3502, ḥasan). Begin where you are: obey one ruling fully, watch its vindication, certainty grows. The certain need no debate; the obedient become certain. Two benches, two verdicts — certainty chooses God's, experience confirms it.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:51

> **O you who believe! Take not the Jews and Christians as allies. Some of them are allies of one another. Whosoever among you takes them as allies is surely one of them. Truly God guides not wrongdoing people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Alliance Against the Community: The Verse's Occasion and Object**

*Take not Jews and Christians as awliyā'* (*walī*: ally, patron, protector, intimate guardian) — the verse addresses a wartime Madinah where some Muslims maintained confederate loyalties with hostile tribes. The reported occasions converge: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy clinging to his Jewish confederates ("I fear reversals of fortune" — v. 52 verbatim); ʿUbādah ibn al-Ṣāmit renouncing his Jewish alliance for God and His Messenger ﷺ while Ibn Ubayy refused (Ṭ, W); Muslims hesitating between confederates at Uḥud (Q, Z); Abū Lubābah's indiscretion to the Naḍīr (Q). *Some of them are allies of one another* — the hostile factions coordinate; matching their coordination with divided loyalties is suicide. *Whosoever takes them as ally is one of them* — in the operative sense: sharing their hostile alignment against the Muslim polity (Ṭ), aiding them against Muslims, or (in the strongest reading) apostatizing (R). Al-Qurṭubī certainty-qualifies: alliance here means *intimate confederation against Muslim interests*, not ordinary coexistence — proven by verse 5 (their food, their women) and 60:8 (*kindness and justice to peaceful non-Muslims*). The verse governs *treason*, not *tolerance*.

**Walā' and Barā': The Alliance-Theology Systematized**

*Walā'* (alliance) and *barā'* (disavowal) — the verse's operative concepts — systematize into Islam's loyalty-theology, and the system's tiers need stating lest the verse be weaponized or voided. *Tier one* (*muwālāh* — intimate alliance): confederate loyalty, military alliance, political patronage *against Muslim interests* — barred absolutely (this verse; 3:28; 4:144; 58:22; 60:1). *Tier two* (*muʿāmalah* — dealings): trade, neighborliness, contracts, employment — permitted broadly (the Prophet ﷺ mortgaged armor to a Jew — al-Bukhārī 2068; hired ʿAbd Allāh ibn Urayqiṭ the pagan guide — al-Bukhārī 2263). *Tier three* (*birr wa qisṭ* — kindness and justice): mandated toward peaceful non-Muslims — *"God forbids you not, regarding those who fought you not over religion nor expelled you, from kindness and justice toward them"* (60:8). *Tier four* (intimacy-of-heart): natural affection for righteous non-Muslim kin permitted (the Prophet's ﷺ love for Abū Ṭālib — 28:56's context; Asmā' serving her pagan mother — al-Bukhārī 2620); *intimate confiding* (*biṭānah*) barred (3:118). The verse targets tier-one *against Muslim polity* — treason's theology. Collapsing all tiers into prohibition produces isolationist extremism (contradicting 60:8, verse 5, the Prophet's ﷺ practice); collapsing all into permission produces loyalty-less assimilation (contradicting this verse, 3:28). Four tiers, each ruled — loyalty graduated, never binary.

**"One of Them": What the Warning Does and Does Not Mean**

The warning's sharpest edge — *is surely one of them* — needs the classical calibration, for extremists wield it as a *takfīr*-machine against any Muslim cooperating with non-Muslims. The commentators bound it: *one of them* in *alignment and aid* (Ṭ), in *hostility to Muslims*, or by *apostatizing* (R) — operative solidarity with anti-Muslim hostility, not commercial, diplomatic, neighborly, or civic cooperation. The proof-texts constraining it are decisive: verse 5's table-and-marriage fellowship; 60:8's commanded kindness-and-justice; the Prophet's ﷺ treaties (Ḥudaybiyah), employment (Khaybar's Jewish farmers retained), and neighborhood (the Jewish boy visited in illness — al-Bukhārī 1356). Ibn Taymiyyah's rule governs: alliance *against Islam* is the prohibited core; interaction *within* justice is the permitted field. The Muslim physician treating Christian patients, the engineer partnering across faiths, the citizen voting shared interests — none is "one of them"; the informant betraying Muslims to hostile powers is. *God guides not wrongdoing people* (*ẓālimūn*) — betraying one's community for hostile patrons is *ẓulm*, misplacing loyalty where it destroys.

**Treason's Theology: Loyalty in the Sīrah**

The verse's treason-core illuminates through the *sīrah*'s loyalty-trials — each testing *walā'* under pressure. *Ḥāṭib ibn Abī Baltaʿah* (conquest-of-Makkah intelligence-leak to Quraysh): ʿUmar demanded execution; the Prophet ﷺ pardoned — *"he witnessed Badr; what tells you God said to Badr's people: do as you wish, I have forgiven you"* (al-Bukhārī 3007; Muslim 2494) — treason's *act* condemned, Badr's *credit* interceding. *Abū Lubābah* (Banū Qurayẓah siege: throat-slitting gesture answering their fate-question): self-bound to the mosque-pillar till revelation pardoned (9:102's context — Ṭ) — indiscretion repented dramatically. *The hypocrites' confederacies* (this verse's occasion): Ibn Ubayy's *"I fear fortune's reversals"* (v. 52) — loyalty hedged, faith exposed. The pattern: loyalty-failures are *grave* (treason, indiscretion, hedging) yet *forgivable* (Badr-credit, pillar-repentance, even hypocrites invited — 4:146). The verse's *lā yahdi'l-ẓālimīn* (guides not wrongdoers) targets *persisting* betrayal, not stumbled loyalty. Modern applications: Muslims serving hostile occupiers against Muslim populations (the verse's core-case), versus Muslims serving *justly* in plural states (60:8's field). Treason's theology is grave; loyalty's mercy is graver still. Betray never; repent always; distinguish just service from hostile aid.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:52

> **Yet thou seest those in whose hearts is a disease hastening towards them, saying, "We fear lest a turn of fortune afflict us." But it may be that God will bring victory or a command from His Presence. Then they will become remorseful for what they concealed within themselves.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Fear a Turn of Fortune": The Hedger's Confession**

*Those in whose hearts is disease* (*maraḍ* — hypocrisy's chronic ailment; cf. 2:10; 8:49; 9:125; 33:12, 32, 60; 47:20, 29) *hastening toward them* (*yusāriʿūn fīhim* — racing to the hostile confederates) — the hypocrites' diplomacy: betting on every horse. Their confession: *we fear lest a turn of fortune* (*dā'irah* — a circling calamity, reversal) *afflict us*. Ibn Ubayy's reported words verbatim (Ṭ, W): keep the Jewish alliance as insurance against Muḥammad's ﷺ possible defeat. The logic is portfolio-diversification applied to faith — hedge the eternal with the temporal, keep options open, never commit fully. Revelation diagnoses it as *disease*, not prudence: faith by definition commits — *"believers are those who believed then doubted not, striving with wealth and selves"* (49:15). Hedging is doubt monetized. Every age produces its hedgers — believers keeping one foot in the mosque and one in the mockers' club, lest piety prove unfashionable. The verse X-rays them: the foot outside reveals the disease inside.

**Marad al-Qulūb: The Qur'an's Cardiology of Hypocrisy**

*Maraḍ* (disease) *in hearts* — the Qur'an's diagnosis of hypocrisy — develops a full cardiology across the text, and its pathology needs charting. *Symptoms*: doubt (*rayb* — 9:45, 110), fear-of-fortune (this verse), laziness in worship (4:142), oath-mongering (63:2), order-avoidance (47:20–21: *"why was no sūrah sent?"* — then fainting at fighting-verses), mockery (9:64–66). *Progression*: *"God increased them in disease"* (2:10) — untreated hypocrisy metastasizes; concealment hardening into seal (2:7; 4:155). *Etiology*: worldliness over conviction (4:141's fence-sitting for spoils), fear over trust (this verse's *dā'irah*-dread), pride over submission (63:5's head-turning). *Prognosis*: Fire's lowest depth for persisters (4:145: *"hypocrites in the lowest reach of Fire"*) — worse than open disbelief, for deceit compounds denial. *Treatment*: the Qur'an's prescribed cure — sincere repentance, rectification, God-holding, sincere devotion (4:146: *"except those repenting, rectifying, holding to God, devoting religion purely — with believers"*). The hedger's *dā'irah*-fear is thus *treatable* early (commit fully), *terminal* late (seal set). The verse functions diagnostically: readers detecting hedge-impulses (faith diversified, commitment hedged) receive early diagnosis — treatable now. Commit before metastasis; the cure (4:146) awaits the willing.

**Victory as Exposure: The Hedger's Reckoning**

*But it may be that God will bring victory* (*fatḥ* — the conquest of Makkah or triumph over hostile Jews (Ṭ)) *or a command from His Presence* (*amr* — exposure of hypocrites, jizyah-imposition, or their judgment (Q, Ṭ)) — *then they will become remorseful* (*nādimīn*) *for what they concealed*. Notice the remorse's object: not their disloyalty but their *miscalculation* — the hedge failed, the insurance lapsed, the winning side needed no hedgers. Like Cain's remorse (v. 31), it centers exposure rather than repentance. History vindicated the verse swiftly: Jewish tribes' power broken, Makkah conquered, hypocrites' insurance policies voided — their confederates unable to protect them, their community unwilling to trust them. The hedger's fate is structural: the side he betrayed remembers, and the side he courted despises turncoats. Only full commitment escapes both contempt and remorse. The verse promises believers the only insurance that pays: God's victory or God's command — underwritten by neither tribe nor treaty, but by the Preserver Himself.

**Fatḥ: Victory's Forms in the Qur'an**

*Fatḥ* (opening, victory) — the verse's promised reversal — spans the Qur'an's victory-theology, and its forms need inventorying, for hedgers miscalculate *which* victory God brings. *Military fatḥ*: Badr (8:19: *"if you sought judgment, judgment has come"*), Makkah (48:1: *"We granted thee a clear opening"*; 110:1: *"when God's help and opening come"*). *Judicial fatḥ*: God *opening between* disputants (7:89; 34:26: *"our Lord will gather us, then open between us in truth"* — the Judge's verdict). *Revelatory fatḥ*: truth's opening (48:27's dream-vindication). *Mercy-fatḥ*: provision's opening (35:2: *"whatever mercy God opens to people, none withholds"*). *Exposure-fatḥ*: hypocrites' unveiling (this verse's *amr min ʿindihi*; 47:29–30: *"God will bring forth their rancors... We would show them to thee"*). The hedger hedges against *military* reversal while ignoring *all other* openings — God's victories arrive multi-doored, and every door exposes hedgers. Badr exposed (hypocrites' excuse-making — 8:49); Uḥud exposed (deserters' discipline — 3:152–153); Khandaq exposed (permission-beggars — 33:12–13); Tabūk exposed (excuse-mongers — 9:42–48). The verse's *ʿasā* (it may be) carries prophetic certainty's softness — *may* as *shall*, imminently. Hedge against every opening if you can; the Opener opens all.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:53

> **And those who believe shall say, "Are these the ones who swore by God their most solemn oaths that they were indeed with you?" Their works have come to naught, and they have come to be among the losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Solemn Oaths, Hollow Works: The Verdict of the Believers**

*Those who believe shall say* — on the Day of exposure, whether in this world (hypocrites unmasked) or the next — *"Are these the ones who swore by God their most solemn oaths* (*jahda aymānihim* — the utmost effort of their oaths; cf. 6:109; 16:38; 24:53; 35:42) *that they were with you?"* The question drips with vindicated astonishment: the loudest swearers proved the falsest friends. *Their works came to naught* (*ḥabiṭat aʿmāluhum*) — prayers prayed for show (4:142), charity given for reputation (2:264), expeditions joined for spoils (9:42–48) — all voided, weightless on scales that weigh intentions (cf. 18:103–105; 25:23: *scattered dust*). *They have come to be among the losers* (*khāsirīn*) — the Qur'an's term for those who lose themselves and their households on Resurrection Day (39:15; 42:45). The verse teaches believers to audit oaths by works and works by sincerity: solemnity of swearing correlates inversely with sincerity of meaning. Those most eager to *say* "we are with you" often least *are*; the truly-with rarely need to swear it.

**Jahd al-Aymān: Oath-Effort as Deception's Signal**

*Jahda aymānihim* — "the utmost effort of their oaths" (*jahd*: strenuous exertion): oath-*intensity* deployed as sincerity-*substitute* — and the Qur'an's oath-psychology needs unfolding. *The pattern*: hypocrites swear strenuously (this verse; 63:2: *"they took oaths as a shield"*; 9:62, 74, 95–96: swearing to please, swearing falsely, swearing for turning-away), while believers swear sparingly (2:224: *"make not God an obstacle, by your oaths"*; 5:89's expiation disciplining casual swearing). *The psychology*: oath-effort *compensates* for evidence-absence — the liar intensifies *assertion* because he lacks *demonstration*; the truthful rest in *verifiability*. *The Prophet's ﷺ rule*: *"Whoever swears by God, let him speak truth; whoever is sworn to by God, let him be content"* (Ibn Mājah 2101) — oaths solemnizing truth, not manufacturing it. *The legal corollary*: courts weigh *evidence* over oath-vehemence; the strenuous swearer invites *suspicion*, not confidence (the *qasāmah*-oaths excepted — structured, collective, evidentiary). *The social application*: leaders protesting loyalty loudest (pledges, oaths, anthems) while hedging practically (this section's hedgers) reenact *jahd al-aymān*. Audit accordingly: weighty oaths, weightless deeds = hypocrisy-pattern; quiet fidelity, costly deeds = belief-pattern. Solemnity signals; sincerity proves.

**Ḥubūṭ al-Aʿmāl: How Works Come to Naught**

*Ḥabiṭat aʿmāluhum* — "their works came to naught" (*ḥubūṭ*: abortion, voiding — deeds miscarried, never delivered): the Qur'an's deed-annulment doctrine, and its causes need cataloguing, for believers' works face the same voiding-agents. *Voiding-agent one* (*kufr/nifāq*): disbelief-and-hypocrisy void *all* (this verse; 18:105; 47:32–33: *"do not nullify your deeds"*). *Two* (*riyā'*): showing-off voids the shown deed (2:264: charity-with-reminder-and-hurt; 107:4–6: prayer-for-seeing). *Three* (*mann wa adhā*): reminding-and-hurting void charity (2:264). *Four* (raising-voice above Prophet ﷺ): *"lest your works be voided while you perceive not"* (49:2) — irreverence aborting deeds unknowingly. *Five* (opposing God-and-Messenger ﷺ persistently): 47:32–33. *The mechanics*: deeds are *vessels* — intentions their *bottoms* (al-Bukhārī 1: *"deeds are by intentions"*); bottomless vessels (show, hypocrisy, reminder) leak all contents. *The audit*: believers must check bottoms regularly — sincerity (*ikhlāṣ*) maintained, reminders withheld, voices lowered. The hypocrites' tragedy: *strenuous* works (prayers, fasts, expeditions) *totally* voided — effort maximal, delivery zero. Strenuousness never substitutes sincerity; the verse voids loudly what hollowness emptied silently.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:54

> **O you who believe! Whosoever among you turns back from his religion, God will bring a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble towards believers, stern towards disbelievers, striving in the way of God, fearing not the blame of any blamer. That is the bounty of God. He gives it unto whomsoever He will. And God is Bounteous, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Apostasy and Replacement: The Religion Needs No One**

*Whosoever among you turns back* (*yartadda* — apostatizes) *from his religion* — the verse contemplates mass apostasy (foretelling, per many, the *riddah* wars after the Prophet's ﷺ death (Q)) without panic: *God will bring a people whom He loves and who love Him*. The religion needs no one; its adherents are replaceable, its truth is not. The Prophet ﷺ reportedly gestured to Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī's people (the Yemenites/Ashʿarīs) as the promised replacements (Q, Ṭ, from ʿIyāḍ al-Ashʿarī) — and history obliged: Yemenites staffed the conquests, Persians staffed the scholarship, Turks staffed the defense, and every age's converts staff its renewal. The verse comforts the faithful (defections cannot sink the ship) and warns the wavering (your seat is reservable). God's love seeks objects; if the invited decline, the invitations go elsewhere. No one is indispensable to Islam; Islam is indispensable to everyone.

**The Riddah Wars: The Prophecy's First Fulfillment**

The verse's *riddah*-foretelling (Q) fulfilled within two years of revelation's seal — and the fulfillment's drama needs recounting. Upon the Prophet's ﷺ death (11/632), Arabia apostatized *en masse*: false prophets (Musaylimah, Ṭulayḥah, Sajāḥ), zakāh-withholders (prayer-keeping, alms-refusing — *"we pray but pay not"*), tribal secessions. Abū Bakr — advised conciliation, urged delay — refused both: *"By God, I will fight whoever distinguishes prayer from zakāh"* (al-Bukhārī 1400; Muslim 20) — the caliphate's founding resolve. Khālid ibn al-Walīd's campaigns (Yamāmah's garden-of-death, Musaylimah slain by Wahshī — Ḥamzah's killer redeeming through the arch-liar's killing), ʿIkrimah's reformed zeal, the Ashʿarī-Yemenite levies — Arabia reunited within a year. The verse's *qawm* (people) arrived multi-tribally: Muhājirūn-Anṣār core, Yemenite reinforcements, reconverted tribes. The pattern repeats perpetually: abandonments (Andalusia's fall, colonial apostasies, modern secularizations) followed by replacements (Islam's spread to sub-Saharan Africa, Southeast Asia, Western converts — the *qawm* ever-renewed). Apostasy alarms the age; the verse answers eternally: *God brings a people*. The religion outlives every leaver; love's objects are inexhaustible.

**The Portrait of the Beloved People: Five Strokes**

The replacement-people's portrait is drawn in five strokes, each a virtue-curriculum: *whom He loves and who love Him* (*yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnah*) — mutual love, Divine initiative first (cf. 2:165; 3:31); *humble toward believers* (*adhillah* — gentle, lowly-winged; cf. 15:88; 17:24) — tenderness as strength's proof; *stern toward disbelievers* (*aʿizzah* — mighty, unyielding) — dignity as tenderness's guard; *striving in God's way* — love's labor; *fearing not the blame of any blamer* (*lā yakhāfūna lawmata lā'im*) — public courage as love's seal (cf. 75:2's self-blaming soul overcome). The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"There are people who will come after me, loving me, wishing to have seen me though it cost them family and wealth."* (Muslim, paraphrased tradition)

And the Qur'an elsewhere describes love's wages (2:165; 3:31: *follow me, God will love you*). *That is God's bounty, given to whom He will; God is Bounteous* (*Wāsiʿ* — Wide, encompassing), *Knowing* — love's distribution is grace, not wages; yet the portrait's virtues remain earnable by all who strive. The verse ends the alliance-section (vv. 51–54) by replacing fear-of-fortune (v. 52) with love-of-God: hedgers trusted tribes; the beloved trust only Him.

**Mutual Love: Yuḥibbuhum wa Yuḥibbūnah Unpacked**

*Yuḥibbuhum wa yuḥibbūnahu* — "He loves them and they love Him": Divine initiative *preceding* human response — and the love-theology needs its full architecture. *Divine love's objects* (Qur'anic inventory): the good-doers (2:195; 3:134, 148), the repentant-and-purified (2:222), the reverent (3:76; 9:4, 7), the patient (3:146), the trusting (3:159), the just (5:42; 49:9; 60:8), the fighters-in-ranks (61:4). *Human love's means*: following the Prophet ﷺ (3:31: *"follow me, God will love you"*), obligatory-plus-supererogatory devotion (al-Bukhārī 6502's *walī*-ḥadīth), loving-for-God's-sake (*"where are those loving one another for My glory?"* — Muslim 2566). *Love's wages*: Divine hearing-seeing-answering (6502's continuation), creation's love commanded (al-Bukhārī 3209: Gabriel's announcement, acceptance placed on earth), the beloved's sins converted (25:70). *Love's tests*: this verse's five strokes — humility, sternness, striving, blamelessness — love *proven* in posture. The Sufis' expansion (Rābiʿah's disinterested love, al-Ghazālī's *Iḥyā'* book-of-love) explores love's summit; the verse grounds its base: loved-and-loving *people* — plural, communal, striving. Hedgers (v. 52) loved safety; the beloved love God. Love's bounty (*faḍl*) is *given* (*yu'tīhi man yashā'*) — grace initiating, virtues responding, striving sealing. Seek the gift; practice the portrait.

---
## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:55

> **Your ally is none but God, His Messenger, and those who believe, those who perform the prayer and give the alms, and bow down.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The True Alliance: God, Messenger, Believers**

*Your walī is none but God, His Messenger, and those who believe* — the positive counterpart to verse 51's prohibition: loyalty withdrawn from hostile confederates is reinvested in the Divine alliance. The verse defines the *ummah* as a *walāyah*-community: believers are one another's patrons, protectors, and intimates (cf. 8:72–73; 9:71: *believers, male and female, are allies of one another*). The qualifiers — *who perform prayer, give alms, and bow down* (*rākiʿūn* — regular worshippers (Bḍ), or the humble (IK, Q, R, Ṭ)) — make alliance conditional on practice, not mere profession: the *walī*-community is the praying-giving-bowing community. This is the theological basis of Muslim solidarity: not race, passport, or party — but shared *qiblah*, shared *zakāh*, shared *rukūʿ*. Where these bind, alienation is sin; where they are absent, alliance is hollow.

**ʿAlī's Ring: The Occasion and Its Contestations**

The famous occasion: ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib gave his ring to a beggar while bowing (*rukūʿ*) in prayer, and the verse descended honoring him (Q, R, Ṭs, W) — reports al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī transmit, some Shīʿī readings building successorship-claims upon it. Sunnī handling of the report deserves stating: the *ring-narrative*'s chains are contested (Ibn Taymiyyah and others grading them weak; al-Ṭabarī transmitting without authenticating), while the verse's *plural* (*alladhīna āmanū* — those who believe) generalizes beyond any individual — *walāyah* belonging to the *believing community* collectively. What Sunnīs affirm unambiguously: ʿAlī's *walāyah*-eminence (Ghadīr's *"whose master I am, ʿAlī is his master"* — al-Tirmidhī 3713, ṣaḥīḥ; *"you are to me as Aaron to Moses"* — al-Bukhārī 3706), his generosity-in-worship's plausibility (his charity was legendary), and the verse's inclusion of him *preeminently among* believers. The sectarian moral: the verse founds *communal* alliance (all praying-giving-bowing believers), within which ʿAlī shines foremost — loving him *walāyah*, hating him *nifāq* (*"none loves you but a believer, none hates you but a hypocrite"* — Muslim 78). The ring-story, authentic or illustrative, pictures the verse: worship interrupted never, charity delayed never — *rukūʿ* and *ṣadaqah* in a single bow.

**Prayer-Alms-Bowing: The Alliance's Three Credentials**

*Yuqīmūna'ṣ-ṣalāh, yu'tūna'z-zakāh, wa hum rākiʿūn* — the *walī*-community's three credentials, each filtering alliance-membership. *Prayer established* (*iqāmah* — not merely performed but *erected*: congregational, timely, complete): the vertical bond — alliance with God proven in prostration. The Prophet ﷺ made it the divider: *"between man and disbelief is abandoning prayer"* (Muslim 82) — alliance's first credential non-negotiable. *Alms given* (*ītā'* — paid out, delivered): the horizontal bond — alliance with believers proven in wealth-sharing. Abū Bakr fought zakāh-withholders as apostates (v. 54's *riddah*-context) — alliance's second credential enforced by sword. *Bowing* (*rukūʿ* — humility-posture, or worship generally): the interior bond — alliance's *spirit*: lowliness before God, gentleness with believers (v. 54's *adhillah*). The three credentials structure Muslim solidarity practically: pray *with* them (congregation), pay *for* them (zakāh, charity), bow *among* them (humility, no arrogance). Absent prayer: alliance claimed, Divine link severed. Absent alms: alliance claimed, communal dues unpaid. Absent humility: alliance claimed, spirit violated. Three credentials, one *walāyah* — God, Messenger, and the praying-giving-bowing.

---

## Sūrah al-Mā'idah 5:56

> **And whosoever takes God, His Messenger, and those who believe as allies, surely the party of God, they are the victors.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥizb Allāh: The Party of God and Its Victory**

*Ḥizb Allāh* — "the Party of God" — the Qur'an's name for the Divine alliance (cf. 58:22), set against *ḥizb al-shayṭān* (the devil's party; 58:19). *They are the victors* (*ghālibūn*) — victory (*ghalabah*) promised not as wish but as identity: to join God's party *is* to join the winning side, whatever intermediate battles suggest (cf. 37:173; 58:21: *"God has decreed: I and My messengers shall prevail"*). The verse answers verse 52's hedgers directly: they feared fortune's turns; the verse declares fortune's *end* — God's party prevails. History's ledger confirms the pattern if not every entry: outnumbered Badr, besieged Khandaq, boycotted Makkah — each defeat-shaped moment preceding triumph. The promise's scope needs honesty: it guarantees the *cause's* ultimate victory, not every member's immediate comfort — martyrs are victors (3:169–171), prisoners are victors (Yūsuf, 12:100), the persecuted are victors (85:11). Victory is eschatological before it is political; the party wins because its King cannot lose.

**Ḥizb al-Shayṭān: The Losing Party Profiled**

*Ḥizb Allāh*'s antonym — *ḥizb al-shayṭān* (58:19) — profiles the losing party, and the contrast completes the verse. *The devil's party*: *"Satan has subdued them, making them forget God's remembrance — those are Satan's party; Satan's party, they are the losers"* (58:19). *Membership*: hypocrites (58:14–19's context — swearing falsely, allying God's enemies), open deniers, heedless worldlings. *Program*: forgetting remembrance (the *cause* of membership — heedlessness recruiting), allying falsehood, opposing messengers. *Wages*: loss (*khusr* — self-loss, v. 53's *khāsirīn*). *God's party* (58:22): believers loving-not God's opposers (even kin), hearts inscribed with faith, spirit-strengthened, Gardens-promised — *"God pleased with them, they with Him."* The two parties' *present* appearances deceive: Satan's party often *looks* victorious (wealth, power, numbers — Pharaoh's court, Quraysh's aristocracy), God's party often *looks* losing (persecuted Makkans, boycotted clans). The verse corrects vision eschatologically: *ghālibūn* (victors) as *identity*, not *circumstance*. Choose parties by *destiny*, not *display*. The scoreboard that matters posts final scores only — and the verse leaks the result: God's party, victors.

**Victory's Guarantee: Ghalabah Across the Qur'an**
